## How Language Models 

Organize Task Information

Kaiyang Wan & Yuxia Wang

Affiliation: INSAIT, Sofia University “St. Kliment Ohridski”

Affiliation: Sofia, Bulgaria

Email: {kaiyang.wan,yuxia.wang}@insait.ai

Abstract

Dense linear probes assess task readability from language model (LM) representations, but do not directly reveal how many features suffice for prediction.
We measure readout sparsity with $k^{*}$ , the smallest feature count found by a fixed search protocol that recovers 95% of dense-probe AUROC above chance.
We evaluate 19 LMs from five labs on 14 core tasks.
Ten base models give the nine reliably readable tasks similar feature counts and nearly the same order.
This agreement extends to the selected features themselves, which on three tasks show cross-model response correspondence beyond class-mean differences and a label-correlation-matched null.
Instruction-tuned deployment selectively reduces $k^{*}$ , with both weights and input format contributing.
In controlled synthetic experiments across 20 models, successfully learned targets admit single-feature readouts, whereas readable controls require tens of features.
Reference-model $k^{*}$ is associated with label requirements across eight students, following an empirical scaling of $N^{*}\propto(k^{*})^{0.65}$ .
After continual learning, saved heads can fail while linear readability remains high, and recovery requires fewer labels than initial task learning under the tested procedures.
Pruning reduces linear readability, with wider pre-pruning readouts generally associated with larger losses.
These findings distinguish task readability from readout sparsity and establish their complementary value for predicting learning requirements and diagnosing LM changes: 

k*.

Figure 1: Task readout measurements reveal cross-model structure, its dependence on training, and implications for task learning and model modification.

## 1 Introduction

Language models (LMs) learn representations that support diverse tasks (Devlin et al., 2019; Brown et al., 2020), from assessing factual claims (Thorne et al., 2018) to recognizing harmful requests (Han et al., 2024).
These representations can make task information accessible in different ways, with some tasks requiring only a few features for prediction and others requiring many (Gurnee et al., 2023).
Dense linear probes measure how accurately the full representation predicts task labels (Alain & Bengio, 2016; Belinkov, 2022), but their performance alone does not distinguish these organizational patterns.
Building on prior work that examines probe accuracy alongside learning complexity (Hewitt & Liang, 2019; Voita & Titov, 2020), we focus on the number and identity of features supporting equally accurate predictions.
We study these differences to understand how LMs organize task information, how training shapes this organization, and what it reveals about subsequent learning and model changes.

To quantify these differences, we fit linear probes to selected feature subsets and compare their performance with a dense probe that uses the full representation.
We define $k^{*}$ as the smallest subset size our fixed search protocol identifies that recovers 95% of the dense probe’s AUROC above chance (Figure 1a).
The dense-probe AUROC and $k^{*}$ jointly characterize how accurately and how sparsely a representation supports task prediction.
To examine feature correspondence across models, we additionally compare the responses of task-relevant sparse autoencoder (SAE) features (Bricken et al., 2023; Huben et al., 2024) on shared inputs.
These measurements provide a common framework for characterizing cross-model readout structure, examining its dependence on training, and evaluating its predictive and diagnostic value for subsequent model changes.

Using this framework, we evaluate 19 LMs from five labs on 14 core tasks.
Across ten base models, we find that task readouts vary substantially in feature count across tasks but require similar counts within each task.
These readouts require only four to five residual coordinates for intent classification and yield task rankings with a median cross-lab Spearman correlation of 0.93 across nine tasks with consistently high dense-probe performance (Figure 1b).
Their selected SAE features also show response correspondence across models on three tasks, exceeding that of unselected features with comparable label correlations after removing class-mean differences (Figure 1c).
Selected language features further contribute to Gemma-2-9B-IT’s adaptation of reply language to input language, as their ablation disrupts this behavior while matched control edits largely preserve it.
Together, these findings reveal shared structure in task readouts, both in the number of features that support prediction and in their responses to individual inputs.

This shared readout structure motivates examining how training shapes access to task information.
We compare nine base models with their instruction-tuned counterparts and find task-selective reductions in $k^{*}$ .
By independently varying weights and input format, we find that both affect readout sparsity, with task-dependent interactions.
To isolate the contribution of target supervision, we train 20 controlled models on binary factors, pairing target aggregates with correct answers and control aggregates with independent random answers.
Successfully learned targets admit single-feature readouts, while readable controls require tens of features, even when a control shares half its factors with a trained target (Figure 1d).
These results show that training can selectively concentrate access to supervised targets within representations that also support broader readouts of related information.

The dependence of readout structure on training motivates evaluating its predictive and diagnostic value for subsequent learning and model modification (Figure 1e).
Across eight student models, reference-model $k^{*}$ predicts label requirements for supervised adaptation, following an empirical relation $N^{*}\propto(k^{*})^{0.65}$ .
After continual learning, dense probes retain near-baseline performance despite failures of earlier classification heads, and recovery requires fewer labels than initial learning under the tested procedures.
Under pruning, dense-probe performance declines, with wider pre-pruning readouts generally associated with greater losses.
Yet allocating labels by feature count and protecting parameters associated with selected readout features do not consistently outperform the tested baselines.
These results establish the value of readout measurements for predicting supervision requirements and distinguishing failures of classification heads from losses of linear readability.

Our contributions are as follows:

1. 

In Section 2, we introduce $k^{*}$ and specify a protocol for measuring linear readability and readout sparsity, with explicit procedures for feature search, confirmation, and aggregation.

2. 

In Section 3, we identify similar feature counts and consistent task rankings across base models, together with cross-model response correspondence among selected SAE features.

3. 

In Section 4, we show that weights and input format shape readout sparsity, and that successful learning promotes single-feature readouts of targets in controlled experiments.

4. 

In Section 5, we demonstrate the predictive and diagnostic value of readout measurements for supervision requirements, forgetting, and pruning, while identifying limitations of the tested readout-based interventions.

## 2 Task Encoding Sparsity Measurement

In this work, we measure the linear readability of task information and the number of representation coordinates needed to recover that readability.

Readout sparsity.
We formulate each task as one or more binary classification problems, using one-versus-rest labels for multiclass tasks, and treat each coordinate of a frozen LM representation as a feature.
For a fixed class decision, layer, and probe seed, let $A(k)$ denote the highest test AUROC among candidate probes using $k$ features.
Let $C>0.5$ denote the dense-probe reference specified below.
We define normalized performance $s(k)$ and the smallest qualifying feature count $k^{*}$ as

 $s(k)=\frac{A(k)-0.5}{C-0.5},\qquad k^{*}=\min\{k:s(k)\geq 0.95\},$ 

(1)

where the minimum ranges over feature counts evaluated by our search.
We set $k^{*}=\infty$ if no candidate meets the threshold and aggregate these local counts as described below.

Interpretation.
The dense-probe ceiling $C$ measures linear readability, while $k^{*}$ measures how many features suffice to recover 95% of its performance above chance under the specified search protocol.
We interpret them jointly and treat a task–model pair as reliably readable when its task-level readability ceiling is above 0.90.
A small count at a low ceiling need not support accurate prediction, and the ceiling serves as a reference for linear readout of the current LM representation.
Counts depend on the representation basis and evaluation protocol; they do not specify intrinsic task complexity or a unique set of necessary features.

Searching for sparse readouts.
For the main count comparisons, we average residual activations over input tokens.
A preliminary single-seed sweep across the model’s depth identifies a task-specific window for repeated measurement, typically comprising four consecutive entries in the probed layer grid (Appendix A.1.6).
Within this window, we construct candidate probes using two search procedures.
One ranks features by their absolute two-sample $t$ -scores on the training data and fits L2-regularized logistic probes to the top- $k$ subsets (Gurnee et al., 2023).
The other fits L1-regularized logistic probes along a regularization path, with nonzero coefficients defining each candidate support.
At each layer and seed, the local $k^{*}$ is the smallest feature count meeting the performance threshold across both procedures (Appendix A.1).

Confirmation and aggregation.
For each of the five seeds, we draw a stratified subsample containing 85% of the training data and repeat the measurement on the same test set.
For each class decision, we set $C$ to the highest dense-probe test AUROC across the measured layers and seeds, providing a shared reference for all local counts.
To avoid relying on a single layer, we take the larger local count in each adjacent pair of probed layers, then the minimum across pairs, separately for each decision and seed.
We obtain the task-level count by taking the median across class decisions within each seed, followed by the median across seeds.
The task-level dense-probe reference is the median of the class-decision references.
This aggregation summarizes feature counts across potentially different supports at different layers, decisions, and seeds (Appendix A.1.6).

Models and tasks.
We evaluate 14 tasks on 19 LMs from five labs, comprising ten base models and nine instruction-tuned counterparts.
The models range from 270M to 27B parameters and span the Gemma (Team et al., 2024; Team et al., 2025), Llama (Grattafiori et al., 2024), Mistral (Jiang et al., 2023), OLMo (OLMo et al., 2024), and Qwen (Yang et al., 2025) families.
Five dispatch tasks cover language identification (Keung et al., 2020; Conneau et al., 2018; Cer et al., 2017), tool selection (Glaive AI, 2023), routing (Cobbe et al., 2021; Hendrycks et al., 2021), and intent classification (Larson et al., 2019; Casanueva et al., 2020).
The remaining tasks comprise three safety judgments (ai4privacy, 2023; Han et al., 2024), three text judgments (Socher et al., 2013; Bajaj et al., 2016; Thorne et al., 2018), and three correctness checks on code and mathematics (Zhou et al., 2019; Zheng et al., 2025).
Ten additional tasks on Gemma-2-9B support the label-budget and input-baseline analyses.
We provide more details in Appendix A.1.

Experiment-specific readout protocols.
To compare feature responses across models, we select task-relevant SAE features at the last input token in the eight base models with available SAEs.
We select these features using the preliminary sweep results, following the protocol in Appendix A.1.10.
For the controlled synthetic experiments, we probe representations at the query position using the protocol specified in Section 4.2 and Appendix A.3.4.
In each analysis, we compare feature counts under a common representation and measurement protocol.

## 3 Shared Structure in Task Readouts Across Models

Models differ in scale and training, yet may organize task information into readouts with common properties.
In this section, we first compare feature counts identified by the fixed search protocol, then examine whether selected SAE features respond similarly to shared inputs.
We further test the behavioral relevance of selected features through targeted interventions.

Figure 2: (a) Task readout counts and rankings are similar across base models, and (b) selected SAE features exhibit cross-model response correspondence beyond class-mean differences.

## 3.1 Task Readout Structure Is Similar Across Models

Motivation and experimental setup.
To determine whether models share task readout structure despite differences in scale and training, we compare residual-coordinate counts $k^{*}$ across ten base models under the fixed protocol of Section 2.
We retain the nine tasks that are reliably readable in every model, with task-level dense-probe AUROC above 0.90.
We measure each task’s cross-model spread as its maximum count divided by its minimum, and compare task rankings using Spearman correlations over 30 cross-lab model pairs.
Both summaries use unrounded counts, and Appendix A.2.1 provides aggregation details and the full 14-task comparisons.

Similar feature counts and consistent task rankings across LMs.
Feature counts vary substantially across tasks but remain comparatively similar across LMs within each task (Figure 2a).
Language identification requires one feature in every LM, intent classification requires four to five, and harmfulness detection requires 153–391.
Eight of the nine tasks have a cross-model spread below 3.2. PII detection is the exception, ranging from one to eight features.
The LMs also rank the nine tasks similarly, with a median Spearman correlation of 0.93 across cross-lab pairs.
This agreement remains at 0.86–0.92 after controlling for how sparsely a bag-of-words probe reads each task from the raw text (Appendix A.2.1).
Together, these comparisons reveal a common ordering of tasks by readout sparsity across the tested base LMs.

Task rankings are robust to estimation choices.
Varying the performance threshold, feature-search path, or layer-selection rule yields task rankings closely aligned with the main protocol, with Spearman correlations of 0.97–0.99.
By contrast, changing the representation basis or input conditions can shift absolute feature counts (Appendix A.2.2).
These results support a stable ordering across the tested estimation procedures while showing that the number of features supporting a readout depends on how task information is represented and presented.

## 3.2 Feature Correspondence and Behavioral Relevance

Motivation and experimental setup.
Similar feature counts leave open whether selected features exhibit corresponding input responses, contribute to behavior, or provide the only available readout.
We compare selected SAE features at the last input token on shared inputs across 28 pairs of eight base models, including 13 cross-lab pairs.
For each model pair and class decision, we subtract class-specific mean activations and match the centered responses one to one by absolute correlation.
We compare each score with a label-correlation-matched null constructed from unselected features (Appendix A.2.4).
To assess behavioral relevance (Elazar et al., 2021), we ablate selected language SAE features during generation, using unselected features with similar activation frequencies and random-direction edits of equal norm as controls.
In a separate harmfulness experiment, we compare readouts from highly ranked residual coordinates with refitted dense probes after fixing those coordinates to their training means (Appendix A.2.6).

Selected features exhibit correspondence beyond class-mean differences.
Across the three tasks with sufficient SAE coverage, matching scores exceed the null’s 99th percentile in 56.2% of tests for intent classification, 48.9% for language identification, and 34.3% for tool selection (Figure 2b).
For intent classification, the median fraction of class decisions exceeding this threshold is 50% across the 13 cross-lab pairs.
Binary task labels do not specify how feature responses should vary among inputs within each class, yet the selected features exhibit partial correspondence in this variation across models.
This finding identifies a shared aspect of task readout structure that feature counts and label correlations alone do not characterize.

Language-identification features contribute to reply-language adaptation.
On Gemma-2-9B-IT, ablating one to four selected SAE features for Japanese, Greek, or Hindi reduces the gap in target-language reply probability between target-language and other-language inputs from 0.88–0.99 to at most 0.015.
Ablations of unselected features with similar activation frequencies and random-direction edits of equal norm largely preserve this gap (Appendix A.2.6).
This contrast supports a behavioral role for the features under the interventions.
Features selected for identifying input language thus also contribute to the model’s adaptation of reply language to that input.

Selected coordinates support sufficient but nonexclusive readouts.
In a separate harmfulness experiment, probes using only the 256 highest-ranked residual coordinates achieve AUROCs of 0.963–0.982 across three instruction-tuned models.
Fixing these coordinates to their training means leaves refitted dense-probe AUROC within 0.0011 of the full-representation baseline (Appendix A.2.6).
Thus, both the selected coordinates and their complements support accurate prediction.
This coexistence shows why identifying a small predictive subset does not determine which coordinates to remove to eliminate linear readability.

## 4 Training Shapes Task Readout Sparsity

The shared readout structure across base models motivates examining how training changes readout sparsity.
In this section, we first compare base models with their instruction-tuned (IT) counterparts, distinguishing the contributions of model weights and input format.
We then use controlled synthetic experiments to test whether supervision for a target promotes a sparse readout of that target.

Figure 3: Instruction-tuned deployment reduces feature counts unevenly across tasks through weight and format effects, while supervised targets admit single-feature readouts in controlled experiments.

## 4.1 Instruction-Tuned Readouts Depend on Weights and Input Format

Motivation and experimental setup.
Instruction tuning uses instruction–response supervision to shape how models answer user requests.
This motivates testing whether readout sparsity changes on tasks relevant to safe and accurate responses, including harmfulness detection and fact checking.
We compare nine base models with their IT counterparts under their respective deployment formats, retaining tasks that are reliably readable in both settings and have finite feature counts (Appendix A.3.1).
This comparison combines weight changes and input format changes.
To distinguish their contributions and test their interaction, we cross base and IT weights with plain-text and chat-formatted inputs on Gemma-2-9B (Appendix A.3.2).

Deployment reductions are task-selective.
Instruction-tuned deployment reduces readout counts unevenly across tasks (Figure 3a).
On Gemma-2-9B, harmfulness detection falls from $k^{*}=341$ to $47$ and fact checking from $253$ to $40$ , while sentiment changes from $72$ to $64$ .
Already sparse readouts, including language identification, tool selection, and routing, show little change.
Harmfulness reductions also vary across models, ranging from approximately $1.1$ -fold on Gemma-3-270M to $8.5$ -fold on Gemma-3-12B (Appendix A.3.1).
The deployment change therefore reshapes the sparsity profile across tasks, with its magnitude depending on the model.

Similar deployment reductions reflect different weight–format interactions.
Harmfulness detection and fact checking both show approximately six- to seven-fold deployment reductions, but differ in their dependence on weights and input format (Figure 3b).
For harmfulness detection, changing only the format reduces $k^{*}$ from $341$ to $135$ , changing only the weights reduces it to $128$ , and changing both reduces it to $47$ .
Each factor produces a similar reduction under either setting of the other, consistent with approximately separable effects on the multiplicative scale.
For fact checking, either change alone yields a modest reduction from $253$ to $217$ or $227$ , respectively, whereas their combination lowers the count to $40$ .
Restricting all four conditions to shared probed layers preserves these medians (Appendix A.3.2).
These results show that the effect of weight updates on readout sparsity can depend strongly on the input format used to access task information.

## 4.2 Target Supervision Promotes Sparse Readouts

Figure 4: Target queries receive correct training answers and control queries receive independent random answers, while probes predict the true aggregate values for both.

Motivation and experimental setup.
To examine whether target supervision promotes sparse readouts, we construct tasks whose answers are fully determined by the input but differ in whether training rewards their prediction.
We train 18 transformers and two GRUs on binary-factor sequences using the target–control supervision in Figure 4, which illustrates the tokenized inputs and queries in readable notation.
Within each function family, target and control aggregates have matching true-label distributions.
An overlapping threshold pair tests whether learning a related aggregate suffices for sparse readout formation.
We measure readouts of the true aggregate values for both targets and controls on fresh sequences, extracting representations at the query position before the answer appears.
Appendices A.3.3 and A.3.4 detail the training and probe protocols.

Sparse readouts are specific to supervised targets.
Across all 20 models, every reliably readable supervised aggregate admits a one-feature readout, whereas readable controls require tens of features, with family-level medians of $29$ – $60.5$ in the original basis (Figure 3c).
The controls remain linearly readable within the same models, demonstrating that linear readability alone does not determine readout sparsity.
Even the threshold control, which shares four of its eight factors with a supervised target, requires a median of $60.5$ features, showing that learning a related aggregate does not suffice to produce an equally sparse readout.
This target–control separation persists in the tested PCA basis (Appendix A.3.4).
Four- and eight-factor parity targets fail the readability criterion, limiting the single-feature result to targets that acquire reliably readable representations.
Together, these findings support a target-specific role for supervision in organizing task information into sparse readouts, beyond the availability of that information or partial overlap in its input dependencies.

Single-feature readouts summarize multiple input dependencies.
Supervised threshold targets over $2$ , $4$ , $8$ , and $16$ input factors all admit one-feature readouts under the tested protocol (Appendix A.3.4).
Thus, increasing the number of factors that determine the target need not increase the number of coordinates required to read it out after training.
A single coordinate can summarize predictive evidence from multiple factors, while the computation that produces this coordinate may involve many features.
This distinction places $k^{*}$ at the level of access to a learned representation as it measures the coordinates needed for prediction under a specified basis and probe protocol.
The observed sparsity therefore concerns the organization of target information and does not reduce the information retained by the full representation.

## 5 Implications for Learning and Maintaining Task-Specific LMs

Building on the training-induced changes in readout structure, we explore the predictive and diagnostic value of readout measurements for learning and model modification.
We examine whether feature counts anticipate label requirements, whether linear readability persists after continual learning disrupts earlier classification heads, and whether pre-pruning counts anticipate its loss.

## 5.1 Feature Counts Anticipate Label Requirements

Motivation and experimental setup.
To test whether readout structure in one model predicts supervision requirements in others, we measure $k^{*}$ in a frozen Gemma-2-9B reference model and evaluate label requirements across eight students from four model families.
We adapt each student at multiple label budgets using LoRA (Hu et al., 2021), a classification head, and ground-truth labels.
For each student–task pair, we define $N^{*}$ as the label count needed to reach 95% of the AUROC gain above chance attained at the largest tested budget.
We fit a power-law relation between reference-model $k^{*}$ and student $N^{*}$ with a shared exponent and student-specific intercepts, and assess prediction by leaving out each task in turn (Appendices A.4.2 and A.4.4).

Reference-model readouts anticipate label requirements across students.
Tasks with wider readouts in the reference model generally require more labeled examples for student adaptation.
Across 109 uncensored student–task observations spanning eight students and 16 tasks reliably readable in the reference model, the fitted relation is $N^{*}\propto(k^{*})^{0.65}$ , with a task-bootstrap 95% interval of $[0.44,0.91]$ for the exponent (Figure 5a).
Leaving out each task in turn yields a 90th-percentile multiplicative prediction error of $5.3$ , indicating substantial uncertainty in individual budget estimates.
The association thus links readout sparsity in a frozen reference representation to supervision requirements for parameter adaptation in other models.
This link remains after controlling for transferability scores or for how well a bag-of-words probe reads the raw text, as Appendix A.4.5 reports.
It supports a predictive role for readout structure across the tested students and raises the question of why sparser readouts cost fewer labels to learn.

Allocation by predicted label requirements does not consistently save labels.
To test whether the predictive relationship improves budget allocation, we replay learning curves for nine tasks and allocate labels in proportion to predicted $N^{*}$ .
Reaching the same joint utility targets requires a median of $1.03$ times the uniform-allocation budget (Appendix A.4.6).
A rule based on a 16-label pilot requires $0.66$ times that budget under the replay accounting, which excludes separate pilot costs.
The tested proportional rule therefore yields no consistent savings despite the association between feature counts and label requirements.
This outcome highlights a distinction between predicting where a learning curve reaches a performance criterion and determining where additional labels most improve a joint objective.

Figure 5: Feature counts anticipate label requirements and pruning vulnerability, while dense probes reveal retained linear readability after saved-head failure.

## 5.2 Linear Readability Can Persist after Behavioral Forgetting

Motivation and experimental setup.
An earlier task’s classification head may fail after subsequent training even if the updated representation retains linearly readable task information.
To examine this possibility, we sequentially fine-tune four models on classification tasks and compare saved-head performance with newly fitted dense probes on the same frozen backbones.
To assess the supervision needed to relearn an earlier task, we compare recovery using LoRA and a new classification head with initial learning from the pretrained model.
We measure label requirements in both settings on a shared grid of 16 to 1024 labels, against the AUROC at which initial learning attains 95% of its gain above chance at 1024 labels (Appendices A.4.2 and A.4.7).

Saved-head failure coexists with retained linear readability.
After the harmfulness–sentiment–jailbreak sequence, saved harmfulness heads lose a median of $0.55$ – $0.59$ AUROC across the four models relative to their performance immediately after harmfulness training.
Yet median dense-probe ceilings remain within $0.009$ of their pretrained baselines (Figure 5b, left).
The updated representations thus support accurate linear readouts even when the previously trained heads no longer predict reliably.
These results distinguish the persistence of an existing readout from the continued availability of linearly readable task information, motivating separate evaluation of both after sequential training (Appendix A.4.7).

Relearning requires fewer labels than initial adaptation.
Across all four models, harmfulness recovery requires fewer labels than initial learning, with medians of $101$ – $244$ and $194$ – $602$ labels, respectively (Figure 5b, right).
Against each curve’s own largest-budget performance instead, the advantage holds in three models and nearly vanishes on Qwen3-8B, and the two procedures also differ in input-length limits (Appendix A.4.7).
The recovery advantage therefore holds under the tested procedures.
Together with the probe results, this finding shows that substantial saved-head failure can coexist with retained linear readability and reduced supervision requirements for relearning.

## 5.3 Readout Sparsity Anticipates Pruning Vulnerability

Motivation and experimental setup.
The retained linear readability after sequential training raises the question of how task readouts withstand weight pruning.
We apply magnitude pruning and Wanda (Sun et al., 2024) to four models from three labs across six tasks and test whether pre-pruning $k^{*}$ anticipates losses of linear readability.
To examine whether selected readout coordinates can also guide task preservation, we protect their associated weight rows and columns during pruning.
We compare this scheme with random-coordinate protection and ordinary pruning at matched actual sparsity.
We provide more details in Appendix A.4.8 and Appendix A.4.9.

Wider readouts are associated with greater pruning losses.
Language identification and intent classification retain high dense-probe performance under pruning, whereas sentiment and harmfulness detection, which have wider readouts, incur larger ceiling losses (Figure 5c).
At sparsities of at least 60%, pre-pruning $k^{*}$ correlates with ceiling loss at approximately $0.89$ or higher in 30 of 32 model–method–sparsity conditions, with adjusted permutation $q<0.05$ (Appendix A.4.8).
The association broadly distinguishes stable from vulnerable tasks, but sentiment loses more than harmfulness detection in all four models, despite requiring fewer features in three.
Readout sparsity thus indicates pruning vulnerability but does not reliably order losses among the wider-readout tasks.
The reduced ceilings also distinguish these pruning outcomes from the retained linear readability observed after sequential training.

Readout-guided parameter protection does not consistently preserve task performance.
On Gemma-2-9B, protecting weight rows and columns associated with selected residual coordinates does not consistently outperform protection based on random coordinates.
At matched actual sparsity, it performs worse than ordinary Wanda pruning (Appendix A.4.9).
Preserving these weights need not preserve the selected activations, which also depend on computations elsewhere in the model.
The result illustrates a gap between identifying coordinates sufficient for task readout and specifying parameter constraints sufficient for task preservation.

Readout measurements anticipate supervision requirements, reveal retained linear readability after saved-head failure, and identify broad differences in pruning vulnerability.
Their predictive and diagnostic value does not automatically translate into effective intervention, as the tested allocation and parameter-protection schemes yield no consistent advantage over their baselines.

## 6 Related Work

Probing and cross-model readout structure.
Sparse probing studies how many neurons support prediction (Gurnee et al., 2023), while minimum-description-length probing measures the coding cost of labels given a representation (Voita & Titov, 2020).
Building on sparse probing, we compare the feature counts needed to recover a prescribed fraction of dense-probe performance across tasks and models.
Convergent learning examines correspondence between independently trained networks (Li et al., 2015), and SAE-based analyses extend these comparisons to LM feature spaces (Lan et al., 2024).
Our correspondence analysis focuses on task-selected features and tests whether their responses agree within classes beyond a label-correlation-matched null.

Training and behavioral relevance.
Feature-drift analysis studies how fine-tuning repurposes LM features (Galichin et al., 2026).
We examine how these changes affect task readout sparsity, separate weight and input-format effects, and test target supervision in controlled experiments.
Amnesic probing distinguishes readable information from its contribution to behavior (Elazar et al., 2021), while concept-erasure methods seek to remove linear access to specified concepts (Belrose et al., 2023).
Our interventions examine both the behavioral contribution of task-selected language features and the persistence of harmfulness readouts outside selected coordinates, separating predictive sufficiency from behavioral contribution and readout necessity.

Learning requirements and representation forgetting.
LogME and H-score assess the transferability of pretrained representations (You et al., 2021; Bao et al., 2019).
We investigate whether task readout sparsity in one reference model predicts the labels required for adaptation across student models.
Prior work uses refitted linear probes to distinguish representation forgetting from task-performance degradation (Davari et al., 2022).
We build on this distinction by jointly evaluating saved heads, linear readability, and relearning requirements across LM families, and contrast retained readability after sequential training with its decline under pruning.

## 7 Conclusion and Future Work

In this work, we studied how LMs organize task information by jointly measuring linear readability and readout sparsity.
Under a fixed protocol, base models exhibit similar task-level feature counts and partial correspondence among selected SAE features.
Training and input format reshape this structure, while controlled experiments show that successfully learned targets can admit single-feature readouts alongside wider readouts of related controls.
Readout measurements also anticipate supervision requirements and broad pruning vulnerability, while dense probes distinguish saved-head failure from losses of linear readability.
Together, these findings connect the organization of task readouts with how models learn and change.

Beyond feature counts, future work could characterize readout directions and activation magnitudes along them under controlled representation scaling.
Tracking these properties during training could distinguish changes in readout support, orientation, and the strength of task-relevant activations.
Combining these measurements with causal interventions would test when sparse readouts provide effective directions for activation steering and whether alternative readouts predict recovery after model modification.
These studies would connect the information accessible to a probe with the computations that govern model behavior.

## AI use statement

In this work, we used generative AI tools for providing feedback on the design of our experimental pipeline and for implementing methods using coding agents. We have not used generative AI tools for generating synthetic datasets, developing theoretical models or conceptual frameworks, formulating mathematical claims, providing ingredients for or writing proofs, proposing or refining hypotheses, translation, cleaning or reformatting datasets, qualitative or thematic data analysis, or interpreting results. Additionally, we used generative AI tools for creating and editing software code, summarizing existing literature, brainstorming, searching for information, and editing the paper to improve readability. We have reviewed all AI-assisted work. The research questions, hypotheses, and final experimental design were determined by the authors. AI tools were used as a discussion partner, and all suggestions were critically evaluated before adoption. All code produced or modified by coding agents was reviewed and tested by the authors to verify its correctness and that it faithfully implements the described methods. All literature summaries and information obtained via AI tools were verified against the original sources, and all cited references were manually added. AI-suggested text edits were reviewed to ensure they did not alter the technical content. We take responsibility for the final content of this work, including text, claims or artifacts.

## Ethics Statement

This work measures how language models represent task labels, and it involves no human subjects, no new data collection, and no annotation by the authors.
All 24 tasks draw on public labeled datasets under their published licenses (Table 3); we load each dataset from its public host, redistribute none of them, and reproduce no text from any pool.
Three sources contain harmful, toxic, or hateful language: WildGuardMix (Han et al., 2024), ToxicChat (Lin et al., 2023), and the offensive and hate splits of TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020); their texts enter only as probe inputs whose published labels the probes predict.
The PII detection task uses a synthetic corpus (ai4privacy, 2023) whose identifying spans are generated, so no real personal information enters the experiments.
All models are open-weight releases used under their respective licenses, and we release no model weights.

The behavioral interventions of Section 3.2 include edits that lower refusal rates on instruction-tuned models.
These edits test whether selected features carry the behavior they predict, and they apply two published methods, SAE feature ablation and LEACE (Belrose et al., 2023), to open-weight models with full access to their activations.
The reported quantity is the gap between refusal rates on harmful and benign prompts under each edit and its controls, and the released measurements contain aggregate rates and feature indices rather than model completions to harmful prompts.
The edits need white-box access to the residual stream, and they add no capability beyond what these published methods already provide.

Several findings bear on safe deployment.
Harmfulness detection has a wider readout than language identification or intent classification and loses more linear readability under pruning (Section 5.3); saved harmfulness heads degrade after later training stages, while dense probes still read the label (Section 5.2).
These measurements let practitioners audit a modified model for retained safety-relevant readouts before deployment, and the recovery experiments state how many labels restore a degraded head.
The labels for each task come from their source datasets and inherit those annotation conventions, so our measurements describe label readability and make no claim about whether a model’s predictions are fair or accurate for any group of users.

Activation extraction runs on single GPUs and probe fitting runs on CPU, which keeps the study’s compute footprint small.
The authors declare no competing interests.

## Reproducibility Statement

Appendix A.1 specifies the measurement in enough detail to reimplement it, with the count estimator as Algorithms 1 and 2 and a worked example that recomputes one reported cell from its probe outputs (Appendix A.1.7).
The same appendix gives the construction of every task pool with its source, split, deduplication, and cue-removal steps (Appendix A.1.3), the probed models and layers (Appendix A.1.9), the SAE feature protocol (Appendix A.1.10), the estimator variant used in each experiment (Appendix A.1.13), and the seeds and compute (Appendix A.1.14).
Appendix A.4.10 lists the procedure, the randomness, and the estimator behind each application result, and Appendix A.2.6 does the same for the behavioral interventions.
Every proposition and lemma states its assumptions and carries a complete proof in the appendix where it appears, and Appendix A.1.8 reports the measured precision of every ceiling against the threshold gap on which the count depends.
All datasets and model weights are public, and the pool construction is seeded, so the task pools can be rebuilt from their sources.
The supplementary material and the anonymized repository at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/taskprobe_ICLR_anonymous-8368 contain code that implements the measurement pipeline, namely task-pool construction, activation extraction, SAE encoding, probe fitting, the count estimator, and its aggregation into the count matrix, together with the configuration of every model and a synthetic test of the probe stack.

## A.1 Details of the Task Encoding Sparsity Measurement

This appendix specifies the measurement of Section 2 in enough detail to recompute every $k^{*}$ and every ceiling reported for the 14 core tasks on the 19 models and for the ten additional tasks on Gemma-2-9B.
We call this set of task–model readings the task matrix.
The appendix proceeds from definitions to estimates and then to guarantees.
Section A.1.1 states the definition formally, and Sections A.1.2 to A.1.4 specify the representations, the task pools, and the probe fits that enter it.
Sections A.1.5 and A.1.6 give the search and aggregation procedures as algorithms, with the order-theoretic properties the paper relies on.
Section A.1.7 traces one task–model cell through the procedure with its actual numbers.
Section A.1.8 measures how precisely the test split determines each ceiling and turns test-split reuse into an explicit finite-sample statement.
Sections A.1.9 and A.1.10 list the models and describe the SAE protocol.
Section A.1.11 justifies each design choice and points to the control that measures its effect, and Sections A.1.12 to A.1.15 state the statistical units, estimator variants, compute, and conditions under which each claim holds.

Table 1 maps every statement of Section 2 to the place where this appendix defines or supports it.
Table 2 collects every setting of the main protocol in one place.

Table 1: Claim-to-evidence map for Section 2.
Each row pairs one statement of the main text with its formal object in this appendix and with the quantity that supports it.

Statement in Section 2

Formal object

Supporting quantity

 $k^{*}$ is the smallest searched count recovering 95% of the dense AUROC above chance

Equations 4 and 5

Definition, with the threshold example in Section A.1.1

A finite $k^{*}$ certifies a qualifying readout, and $\infty$ records that no candidate qualified

Lemma 1(i)

Exact statement about the searched family

Raising the threshold can only raise $k^{*}$ 

Lemma 1(ii) and (iv)

Holds for local, confirmed, and task-level counts

 $k^{*}$ refers to the model’s own coordinates

Random-rotation control in Table 15

Algorithm 1

Ranked path alone keeps the task order at 0.99 (Table 19)

A single-seed sweep places a window of four grid layers

Section A.1.6

240 of 266 core cells use the four-layer window

Five seeds with 85% stratified subsamples on a fixed test split

Section A.1.6

Seed range reported for every cell

Neighbor confirmation, then medians over class decisions and seeds

Equation 14, Algorithm 2, Lemma 1(iii)

Best single layer keeps the task order at 0.98 (Table 19), worked example in Table 4

Reliable readability means a task ceiling above 0.90

Readability flag in Section A.1.6

Flag shown in every count table

Reuse of the test split has a measured and bounded effect on the count

Section A.1.8, Proposition 2

Ceiling standard errors below the threshold gap in 99.4% of readable class decisions (Table 5)

A.1.4

Regularization

Inverse strength $\gamma\in\{0.01,0.1,1,10\}$ chosen on a stratified 20% validation portion

A.1.4

Feature ranking

Absolute two-sample Welch $t$ -statistic on training data

A.1.5

Candidate sizes

Prefixes of size $1,2,4,\dots,1024$ , plus L1 supports at ten strengths from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{1.5}$ 

A.1.5

Score

Test AUROC of the probe’s decision function

A.1.1

Threshold

95% of the ceiling’s AUROC above chance

A.1.1

Layers

Four adjacent layers of the probed grid around the sweep’s best layer

A.1.6

Seeds

Five seeds, each with a stratified 85% training subsample and a fixed test split

A.1.6

Aggregation

Neighbor confirmation, then median over class decisions, then median over seeds

A.1.6

Readability flag

A task ceiling at or below 0.90 marks a count that we do not interpret as a task size

A.1.6

## A.1.1 Notation and Formal Definition

Representations.
Let $f$ be a frozen LM with $L$ transformer blocks and residual width $d$ .
For an input text $x$ tokenized into positions $1,\dots,T_{x}$ , let $h_{\ell}(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ denote the residual stream after block $\ell$ at position $t$ , with blocks indexed from $0$ .
We form the representation of $x$ at layer $\ell$ by mean pooling over attended positions,

 $\phi_{\ell}(x)=\frac{1}{T_{x}}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{x}}h_{\ell}(x,t)\ \in\mathbb{R}^{d},$ 

(2)

and each of the $d$ coordinates of $\phi_{\ell}(x)$ is one feature.
The SAE protocol of Section A.1.10 instead starts from the last position $h_{\ell}(x,T_{x})$ and maps it into a sparse dictionary.

Tasks and class decisions.
A task provides a training split $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{tr}}$ , a test split $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{te}}$ , and a finite set of class decisions $\mathcal{C}$ .
Each class decision $c\in\mathcal{C}$ assigns a binary label $y^{c}(x)\in\{0,1\}$ to every text $x$ in both splits.
Binary tasks have a single class decision.
Multiclass tasks have one class decision per evaluated class, which marks that class as positive and every other text as negative.
We write $\mathcal{P}_{c}=\{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{te}}:y^{c}(x)=1\}$ and $\mathcal{N}_{c}=\{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{te}}:y^{c}(x)=0\}$ for the positive and negative test texts of class decision $c$ , with sizes $n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}$ and $n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ .

Probes and scores.
For a feature subset $S\subseteq[d]$ with $[d]=\{1,\dots,d\}$ , we write $\phi_{\ell,S}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}$ for the restriction of $\phi_{\ell}(x)$ to the features in $S$ .
A probe on $S$ is a linear classifier fitted to $\{(\phi_{\ell,S}(x),y^{c}(x)):x\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{tr}}\}$ by the procedure of Section A.1.4, and it returns a real-valued decision function $g_{S}$ .
The score of the probe is its AUROC on the test split, which equals the Mann–Whitney statistic of the decision values (Mann & Whitney, 1947),

 $A_{c,\ell}(S)=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}\,n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{c}}\Bigl(\mathbf{1}\bigl[g_{S}(x)>g_{S}(x^{\prime})\bigr]+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbf{1}\bigl[g_{S}(x)=g_{S}(x^{\prime})\bigr]\Bigr),$ 

(3)

where $g_{S}(x)$ abbreviates $g_{S}(\phi_{\ell,S}(x))$ .
The dense probe uses the full set $[d]$ .
Equation 3 has two properties that the protocol uses.
It depends on the decision values only through their order, so any strictly increasing transformation of $g_{S}$ leaves the score unchanged and no calibration enters the measurement.
It averages over positive and negative texts separately, so the class prior does not enter the score, which keeps one-vs-rest decisions with positive rates near 1% on the same scale as balanced tasks.
A score of $0.5$ is the chance level of any scorer that ignores the input.

Ceiling and threshold.
Let $\Lambda$ be the set of layers probed in the five-seed measurement of Section A.1.6, let $R$ be its set of seeds, and let a superscript $r$ mark the seed of a fit.
The dense-probe reference $C$ of Equation 1, which we call the ceiling, is the largest dense-probe AUROC of a class decision over probed layers and seeds.
The threshold follows from it,

 $C_{c}=\max_{\ell\in\Lambda,\,r\in R}A^{(r)}_{c,\ell}([d]),\qquad\tau_{c}=0.5+0.95\,(C_{c}-0.5).$ 

(4)

The threshold $\tau_{c}$ is the AUROC at which the normalized score $s(k)$ of Equation 1 equals $0.95$ , so a probe qualifies exactly when its AUROC reaches $\tau_{c}$ .
For example, a ceiling of $0.98$ gives $\tau_{c}=0.956$ , and a ceiling of $0.90$ gives $\tau_{c}=0.88$ .
The normalization measures performance above chance, so tasks with different ceilings face the same relative requirement.
The factor $0.95$ is a convention rather than a derived constant, and Section A.1.11 reports the effect of replacing it by $0.90$ or $0.975$ .

Design rationale.
The threshold is relative to the ceiling of the same class decision, so a count never rewards a task merely for being easy to read densely.
An absolute AUROC threshold would conflate readability with sparsity, because a task with a ceiling of $0.91$ and a task with a ceiling of $1.00$ would face requirements of different relative difficulty.

Local count.
At one layer and one seed, the search of Section A.1.5 evaluates a finite family $\mathcal{S}^{(r)}_{c,\ell}$ of candidate subsets.
The local count of Section 2 is the size of the smallest qualifying candidate,

 $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}=\min\bigl\{|S|:S\in\mathcal{S}^{(r)}_{c,\ell},\ A^{(r)}_{c,\ell}(S)\geq\tau_{c}\bigr\},$ 

(5)

and it equals $\infty$ when no candidate qualifies.
We call the candidate that attains this minimum the support of the class decision at that layer and seed.
All counts take values in the extended set $\overline{\mathbb{N}}=\{1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}$ , ordered so that $\infty$ exceeds every finite value.
Section A.1.6 turns the local counts into the task-level $k^{*}$ reported for each task–model cell.

Takeaway for practitioners.
Dense-probe AUROC alone does not reveal how many coordinates carry a task.
In the probe-family control of Table 18 on Gemma-2-9B, intent classification reaches a ceiling of $1.0000$ with a count of 4, while harmfulness detection reaches $0.9785$ with a count of 256.
Two tasks that a dense probe ranks as almost equally readable thus differ by a factor of 64 in the size of their readout, and reporting the ceiling together with $k^{*}$ separates the two quantities.

## A.1.2 Input Representations

Tokenization.
We tokenize each text with the probed model’s own tokenizer and truncate it to at most 768 tokens.
Mathematical-step correctness uses 1024 tokens because each input includes a problem and a partial solution ending at the evaluated step.

Input format.
Base models receive the raw text with the tokenizer’s default special tokens and no chat template.
Instruction-tuned models receive the same text as a single user turn rendered through the model’s chat template, followed by the template’s generation prompt, so the last position is the one at which the model would begin its reply.

Numerics.
We run one forward pass per text in bfloat16 precision, read the hidden states after every probed block of Table 6, and store the pooled vectors of Equation 2 in float32, because the residual activations of the Gemma-3 models exceed the float16 range.

Pooling.
The mean in Equation 2 runs over all attended positions, including special and template tokens, and the last position is the final non-padding token.
Template tokens therefore enter the mean-pooled representation of instruction-tuned models, and the input-format factorial of Section 4 separates their contribution from that of the tuned weights.

Layer convention.
Layer indices are block indices starting at $0$ , and layer $\ell$ denotes the residual stream after block $\ell$ .
The SAE suites in Section A.1.10 use the same layer naming.

## A.1.3 Task Pools, Splits, and Class Decisions

Question.
The measurement compares models on identical inputs, so every task must be one fixed pool of texts whose labels cannot be predicted from leakage or from trivial surface statistics.
This section specifies how we build, split, and balance such pools.

Data.
Each task is a pool of texts per split together with one binary label vector per class decision.
We build every pool once with a fixed seed and reuse it for all 19 models, so differences between models never come from differences in data.
Table 3 lists all 24 pools with their sources, numbers of class decisions, sizes, positive rates, and test splits.

Splits.
Where the source provides labeled training and test splits, we keep them, and where its test split is unlabeled we use the development split.
Otherwise we hold out 20% of the examples for testing, and we choose the held-out unit to prevent leakage.
Mathematical-answer and mathematical-step correctness split by problem, since ProcessBench contains several solutions of the same problem.
PII detection, which flags personally identifiable information, splits by the masked template, since the source re-instantiates one template with many identity values.
Tool selection splits by the request text after replacing every digit string with a placeholder, since the requests are templated.
Harmfulness and jailbreak detection deduplicate prompts and then split stratified by label.
The ten additional tasks on Gemma-2-9B keep their native splits, and we remove duplicate texts within each split and drop test texts that also occur in the training split.

Balance and caps.
Sentiment, relevance, fact checking, routing, and the additional binary tasks are balanced exactly before capping.
Harmfulness detection and code-bug detection keep their natural class ratios.
Mathematical-answer correctness also keeps its natural ratio, which is close to one half because ProcessBench pairs correct and incorrect solutions of the same problem.
PII detection assigns each text at random to the positive or the negative construction described below.
Mathematical-step correctness keeps every erroneous step and at most two correct steps per solution, then subsamples correct steps to twice the number of erroneous ones.
One-vs-rest tasks whose evaluated classes form a subset of the source classes, namely CLINC150, Banking77, tool selection, MASSIVE, and 20 Newsgroups, keep every text of the evaluated classes and fill the remainder of the cap with texts of other classes, so each class decision has a small positive rate.
Language identification, AG News, and Emotion evaluate every class of their pools, so the cap subsamples all classes alike.
For Banking77 and tool selection, whose class frequencies are unequal, we cap the pool so that the rarest evaluated class keeps a positive rate of at least 5%.
We evaluate a class decision only when it has at least five positives in each split for the core tasks and at least 20 for the additional tasks.
Section A.1.8 shows that the smaller class count of the test split sets the precision of every AUROC, which is the reason for this minimum, and in the five-seed measurement the rarer test class holds at least 30 texts in every class decision of the 14 core tasks.

Class decisions.
CLINC150 uses 20 intents sampled uniformly from its 150 in-scope intents.
Banking77 uses its ten most frequent intents.
Tool selection uses the four most frequent functions among conversations whose first assistant turn is a function call, and the input is the user request alone without the tool schema.
Language identification draws on a public 20-language identification corpus assembled from multilingual product reviews, XNLI, and translated STS benchmark sentences.
We sample ten of its languages with English forced into the sample, namely Arabic, Bulgarian, English, French, Greek, Hindi, Japanese, Turkish, Urdu, and Vietnamese.
Among the additional tasks, AG News uses all four topics, Emotion all six emotions, 20 Newsgroups ten sampled groups, and MASSIVE 20 sampled intents of which 15 meet the minimum positive count.

Positive classes.
Routing marks problems drawn from MATH rather than GSM8K, after removing MATH problems with figure code and dropping problems longer than 1200 characters from both sources.
PII detection marks the original texts of the ai4privacy corpus, and its negatives are the same texts with every annotated span replaced by a generic filler, so each text serves as either a positive or a negative and the two classes share domain and register.
Harmfulness detection marks prompts labeled harmful in WildGuardMix.
Jailbreak detection also draws on WildGuardMix but uses only harmful prompts, and it marks the adversarially framed ones against the plainly stated ones, so the label tracks adversarial framing rather than harmful content.
Toxicity marks user turns labeled toxic in ToxicChat.
Sentiment tasks mark positive reviews, subjectivity marks subjective sentences, and the two TweetEval tasks mark offensive and hateful tweets.
Relevance marks the passage selected for a query in MS MARCO, with one selected and one unselected passage per query, each concatenated to the query.
Fact checking marks refuted claims against supported ones, and the probe sees only the claim text without evidence.
Code-bug detection marks defective C functions.
Mathematical-answer correctness marks complete solutions whose final answer is wrong.
Mathematical-step correctness marks the first erroneous step of a ProcessBench solution against correct steps, each rendered together with its problem and the preceding steps.

Control for length.
Raw adversarial prompts in WildGuardMix are several times longer than plainly stated ones, so a probe could separate the jailbreak classes by length alone.
We therefore length-match the jailbreak pool before splitting.
We bin prompts by character length on quantiles of the pooled distribution and pair each positive with a negative of nearly equal length within its bin, keeping only paired prompts.
If length still carried the label after matching, we would expect a classifier that reads only text length to reach an AUROC well away from $0.5$ .
In the matched pool, that length-only classifier reaches an AUROC of 0.482 on the training split and 0.468 on the test split, and the median character lengths of the two classes differ by less than 10%.

Truncation.
We truncate language-identification texts to 256 characters, relevance passages to 800, code functions to 1024, and jailbreak prompts to 2000.
We drop PII texts longer than 400 characters and tool requests longer than 512, so that a positive can never lose its annotated spans to truncation.

Design rationale.
Each construction above removes one alternative explanation of a readable label.
First, fixed pools shared by all 19 models ensure that a difference between two models cannot come from a difference in their data.
Second, the grouped hold-outs split by problem, by masked template, and by digit-normalized request, so a probe cannot score well by recognizing a near-duplicate of a training text.
Third, the PII negatives are the positive texts themselves with the annotated spans replaced, so the two classes share domain, register, and length distribution, and a probe must read the presence of identifying spans rather than the source of the text.
Fourth, the length-matched jailbreak pool removes the strongest surface cue of that task, and the length-only AUROC of 0.482 and 0.468 confirms its removal.
Fifth, jailbreak detection contrasts two groups of harmful prompts, so harmful content cannot explain the label.

Scope.
Routing measures source classification as a proxy for difficulty, since MATH problems use LaTeX notation and GSM8K problems are plain prose, and we report it as such.
Fact checking without evidence can rest on the wording of the claim, and Appendix A.2.3 measures how far a bag-of-words baseline on the raw text reaches on every task of Table 3.

The split column states whether the test pool is the source’s test split, its development split, or a 20% hold-out formed as in Section A.1.3.
The lower block lists the ten additional tasks evaluated only on Gemma-2-9B.

## A.1.4 Probe Fitting

All probes share one fitting procedure, which we denote $\textsc{FitScore}(Z_{\mathrm{tr}},y,Z_{\mathrm{te}},S)$ and use as a subroutine in Algorithm 1, and which returns the test AUROC of a probe on the features in $S$ .

Standardization.
Let $X_{\mathrm{tr}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ hold the training representations of one class decision at one layer, after the subsampling of Section A.1.6.
We standardize every feature $j$ with the mean $\mu_{j}$ and the standard deviation $\sigma_{j}$ of its column over the $n$ training texts,

 $z_{j}=\frac{x_{j}-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}},\qquad\mu_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{ij},\qquad\sigma_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{ij}-\mu_{j})^{2},$ 

(6)

and set $\sigma_{j}=1$ where the column is constant.
The same $\mu$ and $\sigma$ transform the test representations, so no test statistic enters the fit.
For the sparse SAE features of Section A.1.10, we divide by $\sigma_{j}$ without centering, which preserves the sparsity of the features.

Objective.
Given a feature subset $S$ with $|S|=k$ , the probe is a logistic regression with an intercept, an L2 penalty, and class-balanced example weights $\omega_{y}$ ,

 $\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\,b\in\mathbb{R}}\ \tfrac{1}{2}\lVert w\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{y_{i}}\log\bigl(1+\exp(-\tilde{y}_{i}(w^{\top}z_{i,S}+b))\bigr),\qquad\omega_{y}=\frac{n}{2\,n_{y}},$ 

(7)

where $\tilde{y}_{i}=2y_{i}-1\in\{-1,+1\}$ is the signed label, $n_{y}$ is the number of training examples with label $y$ , and $\gamma>0$ is the inverse regularization strength.
We write $\gamma$ rather than the customary $C$ to keep $C$ for the ceiling.
The weights satisfy $n_{1}\omega_{1}=n_{0}\omega_{0}=n/2$ , so both classes carry equal total weight, which matters for the one-vs-rest decisions with positive rates near 1%.
The decision function of the fitted probe is $g_{S}(z)=w^{\top}z_{S}+b$ .
We solve Equation 7 with the primal trust-region Newton solver of LIBLINEAR (Fan et al., 2008) as wrapped by scikit-learn (Pedregosa et al., 2011), with at most 2000 iterations.
LIBLINEAR treats the intercept as an additional constant feature, so the penalty in practice also includes $b^{2}/2$ .
This term is identical for every subset and every model, and it leaves the comparisons unchanged.
With the intercept penalized, the objective is strictly convex in $(w,b)$ , so each L2 fit has a unique minimizer.

Regularization selection.
We choose $\gamma$ from $\Gamma_{2}=\{0.01,0.1,1,10\}$ on a validation portion of the training split.
The validation portion holds 20% of each class, rounded down, drawn with the seed of the fit.
For each $\gamma$ we fit Equation 7 on the remaining 80% and compute the AUROC of the decision function on the validation portion, which we write $\mathrm{AUROC}_{\mathrm{val}}(\gamma)$ .
The selected strength is

 $\hat{\gamma}=\min\Bigl\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{2}:\mathrm{AUROC}_{\mathrm{val}}(\gamma)=\max_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{2}}\mathrm{AUROC}_{\mathrm{val}}(\gamma^{\prime})\Bigr\},$ 

(8)

so ties resolve toward stronger regularization.
We refit on the full training split with $\hat{\gamma}$ , and the returned score is the AUROC of the refitted decision function on the test split.
The dense probe that supplies the ceiling follows the same procedure with $S=[d]$ .

L1 variant.
The L1 path of Algorithm 1 replaces $\tfrac{1}{2}\lVert w\rVert_{2}^{2}$ in Equation 7 by $\lVert w\rVert_{1}$ and fits a probe on all $d$ standardized features jointly at each of the ten strengths

 $\Gamma_{1}=\bigl\{10^{-3+4.5\,i/9}:i=0,1,\dots,9\bigr\},$ 

(9)

which are spaced logarithmically from $10^{-3}$ to $10^{1.5}$ .
LIBLINEAR solves this problem by coordinate descent, again with the intercept as a constant feature.
Each fit yields a support $T(\gamma)=\{j:w_{j}(\gamma)\neq 0\}$ and a test AUROC of that fit’s own decision function.
We drop fits with an empty support.
These fits do not select $\gamma$ on validation data, since each value of $\gamma$ is a separate candidate.

Standardization and the L1 penalty.
Standardization makes the L1 selection independent of the units of each coordinate.
Writing the fitted probe in raw coordinates as $w^{\top}z+b=v^{\top}x+b^{\prime}$ gives $v_{j}=w_{j}/\sigma_{j}$ and $b^{\prime}=b-\sum_{j}w_{j}\mu_{j}/\sigma_{j}$ , and therefore

 $\lVert w\rVert_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{j}\,\lvert v_{j}\rvert.$ 

(10)

The L1 path on standardized features is thus a weighted lasso on raw features whose weights equal the training standard deviations, apart from the penalty on the intercept, which in raw coordinates applies to $b^{\prime}+\sum_{j}v_{j}\mu_{j}$ .
A coordinate with a large raw scale receives no advantage in selection, and a coordinate with a small raw scale receives no penalty for it.

Design rationale.
Three choices keep the probe fit from favoring particular features or tasks.
Training-split standardization with the weighting of Equation 10 prevents coordinates with large activations, which occur in some residual dimensions, from dominating the selection.
Class-balanced weights prevent rare positives from being ignored by the loss, and the class-prior invariance of Equation 3 prevents the positive rate from entering the score.
Validation-based choice of $\gamma$ on training data prevents the regularization strength from being tuned on the test split.

## A.1.5 Support Search at One Layer

Question.
The definition in Equation 5 needs a finite candidate family that contains small qualifying subsets whenever they are easy to find.
Exhaustive search over subsets is infeasible for $d$ in the thousands, so we combine two complementary paths.
The first path ranks features and refits probes on prefixes of the ranking, following sparse probing (Gurnee et al., 2023).
The second path reads supports off the L1 regularization path.
Algorithm 1 states the procedure, and this section defines its components and derives its properties.
Figure 6 shows the recorded curves of both paths and the resulting local count for four tasks on Gemma-2-9B.

Ranking statistic.
We rank the standardized training features by the absolute two-sample Welch $t$ -statistic (Welch, 1947) between the positive and negative examples of the class decision,

 $t_{j}=\frac{\lvert\bar{z}_{1j}-\bar{z}_{0j}\rvert}{\sqrt{s_{1j}^{2}/n_{1}+s_{0j}^{2}/n_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}}},\qquad\varepsilon_{0}=10^{-12},$ 

(11)

where $\bar{z}_{yj}$ and $s^{2}_{yj}$ are the mean and the population variance of feature $j$ among training examples with label $y$ , and $n_{y}$ is their count.
The constant $\varepsilon_{0}$ only guards against division by zero for constant features.
Let $\pi$ be the permutation that sorts features by decreasing $t_{j}$ , and let $\pi_{1:k}$ be its first $k$ entries.
The statistic scores each feature on its own, so the ranking does not favor features that are useful only in combination.
The L1 path selects features jointly and therefore covers that case.

Prefix grid.
The tested prefix sizes are the powers of two up to the representation width,

 $K=\{2^{i}:0\leq i\leq 10,\ 2^{i}\leq d\},$ 

(12)

so $K$ runs from 1 to 1024 for every model with $d\geq 1024$ and stops at 512 for Gemma-3-270M with $d=640$ .
The candidate family is the union of the prefix sets and the nonempty L1 supports,

 $\mathcal{S}^{(r)}_{c,\ell}=\bigl\{\pi_{1:k}:k\in K\bigr\}\ \cup\ \bigl\{T(\gamma):\gamma\in\Gamma_{1},\ T(\gamma)\neq\emptyset\bigr\},$ 

(13)

and it contains at most $|K|+|\Gamma_{1}|=21$ subsets per class decision, layer, and seed.

Algorithm 1 Support search for one class decision at one layer and one seed

 1:

 standardized training features $Z_{\mathrm{tr}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ with labels $y$ , test features $Z_{\mathrm{te}}$ with labels $y_{\mathrm{te}}$ , threshold $\tau_{c}$ from Equation 4, prefix grid $K$ from Equation 12, L1 strengths $\Gamma_{1}$ from Equation 9

 2:

 local count $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}$ and its support $S^{*}$ 

 3:

 $\pi\leftarrow$ feature indices sorted by decreasing $t_{j}$ of Equation 11

 4:

 for $k\in K$ in increasing order do $\triangleright$ ranked-prefix path

 5:

    $A_{\mathrm{rank}}(k)\leftarrow\textsc{FitScore}(Z_{\mathrm{tr}},y,Z_{\mathrm{te}},\pi_{1:k})$ 

 6:

 end for

 7:

 $k_{\mathrm{rank}}\leftarrow\min\{k\in K:A_{\mathrm{rank}}(k)\geq\tau_{c}\}$ $\triangleright$ $\infty$ if the set is empty

 8:

 $k_{\ell_{1}}\leftarrow\infty$ 

 9:

 for $\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}$ do $\triangleright$ L1 path

 10:

    $(w,b)\leftarrow$ L1-penalized fit of Equation 7 on all $d$ features with strength $\gamma$ 

 11:

    $T\leftarrow\{j:w_{j}\neq 0\}$ 

 12:

   if $T\neq\emptyset$ and $\mathrm{AUROC}(Z_{\mathrm{te}}w+b,\ y_{\mathrm{te}})\geq\tau_{c}$ then

 13:

     $k_{\ell_{1}}\leftarrow\min(k_{\ell_{1}},|T|)$ and record $T$ if it attains the minimum

 14:

   end if

 15:

 end for

 16:

 $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}\leftarrow\min(k_{\mathrm{rank}},k_{\ell_{1}})$ 

 17:

 $S^{*}\leftarrow$ the prefix or L1 support attaining $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}$ 

 18:

 return $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}$ , $S^{*}$ 

Cost.
For one class decision, layer, and seed, Algorithm 1 fits one dense probe and up to eleven prefix probes, each with four validation fits and one refit, plus ten L1 fits.
This amounts to at most $5\,(1+|K|)+|\Gamma_{1}|=70$ logistic fits per class decision, layer, and seed.
The dense probe supplies the ceiling of Equation 4, so we record the whole curve once and apply the threshold afterwards.
The threshold therefore never influences which probes we fit.

Logical status of a count.
A finite local count certifies that a qualifying subset of that size exists among the candidates, and $\infty$ records that no candidate qualified.
Neither statement certifies that no smaller subset exists, because the candidate family of Equation 13 is finite.
Lemma 1 in Section A.1.6 proves this and the other elementary properties of the count, and the rest of this section establishes the one property that is not elementary, invariance under changes of units.

Proposition 1 (invariance to coordinate-wise affine maps)

Let $x^{\prime}_{j}=a_{j}x_{j}+b_{j}$ with $a_{j}\neq 0$ for every residual feature $j$ , applied to the training and test splits alike.
In exact arithmetic, and with ties in the ranking broken identically, Algorithm 1 returns the same local count and the same support on $x^{\prime}$ as on $x$ , provided each L1-penalized fit of Equation 7 has a unique solution.

Proof. A coordinate that is constant on the training split standardizes to zero there under both $x$ and $x^{\prime}$ , so both penalties give it a coefficient of exactly zero in every fit and it plays no further role, and we consider the remaining coordinates with $\sigma_{j}>0$ .
For these, Equation 6 gives $\mu^{\prime}_{j}=a_{j}\mu_{j}+b_{j}$ and $\sigma^{\prime}_{j}=\lvert a_{j}\rvert\sigma_{j}$ , so it maps $x^{\prime}_{j}$ to $\operatorname{sign}(a_{j})\,z_{j}$ .
The standardized features therefore change only by coordinate sign flips, which we write as $z^{\prime}=Dz$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\operatorname{sign}(a_{1}),\dots,\operatorname{sign}(a_{d}))$ .
A sign flip leaves $\lvert\bar{z}_{1j}-\bar{z}_{0j}\rvert$ and both class variances unchanged, hence every $t_{j}$ and the ranking $\pi$ .
For any subset $S$ , the map $(w,b)\mapsto(D_{S}w,b)$ is a bijection between parameter vectors on $z$ and on $z^{\prime}$ that preserves every margin $w^{\top}z_{i,S}+b$ and both penalties $\lVert w\rVert_{2}$ and $\lVert w\rVert_{1}$ .
The objective of Equation 7 therefore takes the same value at corresponding points, and the unique minimizer on $z^{\prime}$ is the image of the minimizer on $z$ .
The decision function on every training, validation, and test point is therefore unchanged, and so are every AUROC, the validation choice of Equation 8, and the support of every L1 fit, because $D_{S}$ preserves which coefficients are zero.
 $\square$ 

Proposition 1 states exactly which changes of basis leave $k^{*}$ unchanged.
Rescaling, shifting, or negating a coordinate does not move the count, while mixing coordinates can.
The measurement therefore concerns the model’s own residual coordinates, and the random-rotation control of Table 15 measures how much a basis change moves it.
For SAE features, which Section A.1.10 scales without centering, the invariance holds for pure rescalings with $b_{j}=0$ .

Design rationale.
The two paths cover each other’s blind spots.
A univariate ranking misses features that help only in combination, and the L1 path selects features jointly.
An L1 path can skip sizes when several features enter at one strength, and the prefix grid tests every power of two.
Recording the complete curve before applying the threshold prevents the threshold from steering which probes are fitted.

Remarks.
We make four further observations.
First, the local count is a search result, not a certified minimum.
A finite value states that a qualifying subset of that size exists among the candidates, and $\infty$ states that no candidate qualified.
Second, the prefixes are nested, $\pi_{1:k}\subset\pi_{1:2k}$ , but the prefix curve $A_{\mathrm{rank}}(k)$ need not increase in $k$ , because each prefix probe selects its own regularization strength.
Algorithm 1 takes the smallest qualifying $k$ rather than requiring every larger prefix to qualify.
Third, the grid of Equation 12 has a resolution of one octave above $k=1$ , and the L1 path supplies sizes between grid points, which is why we combine the two paths.
Fourth, the comparison with $\tau_{c}$ in Equation 5 reads test AUROCs, so $k^{*}$ summarizes the test curve over candidate sizes rather than a subset selected on held-out data.
Section A.1.8 quantifies the consequences of this choice.

Takeaway for practitioners.
A sparse-probe count is an upper bound attached to a search procedure, so a study should report the candidate family together with the count.
Combining a univariate ranking with an L1 path costs 70 fits per class decision, layer, and seed, and removing the L1 path changes the task order only slightly on our models (median $\rho=0.99$ in Table 19), so the ranking path alone is a reasonable budget option when only task order matters.

Figure 6: Reading a local count off one probe cell.
Each panel shows Gemma-2-9B on one task at the window layer where seed 1 attains its smallest local count.
The dark curve is the prefix curve $A_{\mathrm{rank}}(k)$ of seed 1, the thin curves are the other four seeds at the same layer, and the hollow diamonds are the L1 path candidates of seed 1 placed at their support sizes, with supports above 1024 lying outside the axis.
The solid horizontal line marks the threshold $\tau_{c}$ of Equation 4, the dotted horizontal line marks the dense ceiling $C_{c}$ over all window layers and seeds, and the dashed vertical line marks the local count of Equation 5, the smallest qualifying size on either path.
The gray vertical line and each title give the task-level count of Table 11, the median over five seeds after neighbor confirmation (Appendix A.1.6).

The prefix curve qualifies at $k^{*}=2$ , layer 17 ties at 2, and the task-level count of 4 is the median over 20 class decisions.
