An L1 support of 6 qualifies before the prefix grid point 8, and neighbor confirmation with layer 20 returns 8.
(c) Sentiment, layer 20, ceiling 0.980, $\tau_{c}=0.956$ .
An L1 support of 71 qualifies before the prefix grid point 128, layers 17 and 24 need 128 for this seed, and the task-level median over seeds is 72.
(d) Harmfulness, layer 20, ceiling 0.979, $\tau_{c}=0.955$ .
An L1 support of 137 qualifies, while layers 17 and 24 need 341 and 342, so neighbor confirmation returns 341.

## A.1.6 Layer Windows, Ceiling, Neighbor Confirmation, and Aggregation

Question.
A local count describes one class decision at one layer and one seed.
The paper reports one number per task and model, so this section specifies how local counts combine, and it shows that the combination inherits the properties of Section A.1.5.
The measurement runs in two stages.
A preliminary sweep locates the layers at which a task is most compactly readable, and a five-seed measurement on a window of those layers produces the reported numbers.
Algorithm 2 states the second stage.

Preliminary sweep.
For every model we first run Algorithm 1 with one seed on the full training split at every layer of the grid in Table 6, for all three representations of Section A.1.9.
In this sweep the ceiling of a class decision is the largest test AUROC of any probe fitted for it, across representations, layers, and search paths, and the threshold follows Equation 4.
The best layer of a task in a representation is the layer with the smallest median local count across class decisions, with ties resolved toward the larger dense AUROC.
The sweep serves three purposes.
It places the window of the second stage, it supplies the SAE supports of Section A.1.10, and it provides the layer-resolved curves behind the depth analyses of the paper.

Layer window.
The five-seed measurement probes a window of four consecutive grid layers per task–model cell.
The window contains the sweep’s best layer in the mean-pooled representation and brackets it, so that the best layer has a grid neighbor on each side wherever the grid allows.
Four layers give Equation 14 three adjacent pairs.
Figure 7 shows, for six tasks on Gemma-2-9B, how the sweep profile, the window, and the five seed values relate.
We hold the window size fixed at four for every cell, because the minimum over more layer pairs tends to be smaller, and cells with different layer counts would not be comparable.
The four-layer window covers 240 of the 266 core task–model cells.
The remaining 26 cells come from follow-up measurements under the same subsample, representation, and number of seeds, on three to five grid layers around the located minimum, which are adjacent grid entries in 18 of these cells and more widely spaced grid entries in five, and in three cells on the full grid.
These cells are sentiment on Gemma-2-9B, Gemma-3-4B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and OLMo-2-7B in both base and instruction-tuned versions, harmfulness detection on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B-IT, Gemma-3-270M-IT, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and both versions of Mistral-7B and OLMo-2-7B, mathematical-step correctness on both versions of Gemma-2-9B, Gemma-3-4B, and Gemma-3-12B, and CLINC150 on Gemma-2-9B-IT.

Seeds and subsamples.
The five-seed measurement uses seeds $R=\{1,\dots,5\}$ in every cell except harmfulness detection on OLMo-2-7B, which uses seeds 0 to 4.
Each seed draws a stratified subsample of the training split that keeps 85% of each class, rounded down, and it also seeds the validation portion of Section A.1.4.
The test split stays fixed across seeds, so seed variation reflects the training data and the fit, not the evaluation set.
Every probe in Algorithm 1, including the dense probe that supplies the ceiling, is fitted on the subsample of its seed, and only the mean-pooled representation of Equation 2 enters this second stage.

Common ceiling.
The ceiling $C_{c}$ of Equation 4 is the largest dense-probe AUROC across the window layers and all five seeds, and the threshold $\tau_{c}$ derived from it applies to every layer and seed of the window.
This common reference prevents a layer or seed with a weak dense probe from receiving an easier threshold.
As a consequence, a layer whose own dense AUROC lies below $\tau_{c}$ reads $\infty$ , which the aggregation treats as a legitimate value.
By Lemma 1(ii), the common ceiling yields counts at least as large as a layer-specific ceiling would, since it can only raise each threshold.

Neighbor confirmation.
For each class decision and seed, we form the larger of the two local counts in every pair of consecutive window layers and keep the smallest of these pairwise maxima,

 $\tilde{k}_{c,r}=\min_{1\leq j<m}\ \max\bigl(k^{*}_{c,\ell_{j},r},\,k^{*}_{c,\ell_{j+1},r}\bigr),\qquad\ell_{1}<\dots<\ell_{m},$ 

(14)

where $\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}$ are the window layers in order.
A size therefore counts only when two consecutive layers both reach it, which removes minima that appear at a single isolated layer.
When only one layer is available, $\tilde{k}_{c,r}$ is that layer’s local count.
For example, local counts of $32$ , $8$ , $16$ , and $\infty$ on four window layers give pairwise maxima of $32$ , $16$ , and $\infty$ , so $\tilde{k}_{c,r}=16$ and the isolated count of $8$ does not enter the result.
Consecutive entries of the probed grid are often several blocks apart, so neighbor confirmation asks for stability across a span of depth rather than across adjacent blocks.

Medians over extended values.
We use one median convention throughout.
For values $u_{1}\leq\dots\leq u_{N}$ in $\overline{\mathbb{N}}$ ,

 $\operatorname{med}(u_{1},\dots,u_{N})=\begin{cases}u_{(N+1)/2},&N\ \text{odd},\\[2.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(u_{N/2}+u_{N/2+1}\bigr),&N\ \text{even},\end{cases}\qquad\tfrac{1}{2}(u+\infty)=\infty.$ 

(15)

A median over an odd number of values is therefore infinite exactly when more than half of them are infinite, and a median over an even number is infinite exactly when at least half are.
An even number of finite values can produce a non-integer median, which we round for display.

Task-level estimator.
Within each seed, the task value is the median of the confirmed counts across class decisions, and the reported cell is the median of these task values across seeds,

 $K_{r}=\operatorname{med}_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\ \tilde{k}_{c,r},\qquad k^{*}=\operatorname{med}_{r\in R}\ K_{r},\qquad\bigl[\min_{r}K_{r},\ \max_{r}K_{r}\bigr],\qquad C=\operatorname{med}_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\ C_{c}.$ 

(16)

The third expression is the seed range, and the fourth is the reported task ceiling.
The median over class decisions makes a multiclass task’s count describe a typical class rather than its hardest class, and per-class counts appear where an analysis needs them.

Properties.
The following lemma collects the elementary properties of the local, confirmed, and task-level counts that the paper relies on.
Each holds for the recorded curves of one class decision.

Lemma 1 (basic properties of the count)

Let the fitted candidates be fixed.

(i) 

Let $k_{\min}(\tau_{c})$ be the smallest $|S|$ over subsets $S\subseteq[d]$ for which some linear classifier supported on $S$ reaches AUROC $\tau_{c}$ on $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{te}}$ . Then $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}\geq k_{\min}(\tau_{c})$ .

(ii) 

Assume $C_{c}>0.5$ , which holds for every measured class decision in the task matrix since the smallest ceiling is 0.62. Let $k^{*}(\alpha)$ denote the local count when the factor $0.95$ in Equation 4 is replaced by $\alpha\in(0,1]$ . Then $k^{*}(\alpha)$ is non-decreasing in $\alpha$ .

(iii) 

Let $k_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq k_{(m)}$ be the ordered local counts of one class decision and seed over $m\geq 2$ window layers. Then the confirmed count satisfies $k_{(2)}\leq\tilde{k}_{c,r}\leq k_{(m)}$ .

(iv) 

The map from the array of local counts $(k^{*}_{c,\ell,r})_{c,\ell,r}$ to the task-level $k^{*}$ of Equation 16 is non-decreasing in every coordinate. Consequently, raising the factor $\alpha$ cannot lower the confirmed count, the per-seed task value, or the task-level $k^{*}$ .

Proof. For (i), if $k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}=\infty$ the claim is immediate.
Otherwise a candidate $S^{*}$ attains the minimum in Equation 5 together with a linear classifier supported on $S^{*}$ , namely the refitted prefix probe or the L1 fit, whose test AUROC reaches $\tau_{c}$ .
This classifier belongs to the set over which $k_{\min}(\tau_{c})$ minimizes, so $|S^{*}|\geq k_{\min}(\tau_{c})$ .
For (ii), the threshold $0.5+\alpha(C_{c}-0.5)$ increases with $\alpha$ by the assumption $C_{c}>0.5$ .
A higher threshold shrinks the set of qualifying candidates in Equation 5, and the minimum over a smaller set cannot decrease.
For (iii), every pairwise maximum in Equation 14 is at least as large as both of its arguments, and two distinct layers contribute these arguments, so every pairwise maximum is at least $k_{(2)}$ , and the minimum over pairs inherits this bound.
The upper bound holds because every pairwise maximum is at most $k_{(m)}$ .
For (iv), the functions $\max$ , $\min$ , and $\operatorname{med}$ on $\overline{\mathbb{N}}$ are each non-decreasing in every argument, since increasing one argument cannot move any order statistic down and the average in Equation 15 is non-decreasing in both terms.
Equations 14 and 16 compose these functions, and a composition of coordinatewise non-decreasing maps is coordinatewise non-decreasing.
The consequence follows from (ii).
 $\square$ 

Three readings of the lemma matter for the paper.
By (i), a finite count certifies that a readout of that size exists and never certifies that no smaller readout exists.
By (ii) and (iv), the threshold variants of Table 19 move every count in a known direction, and those variants test whether the task order survives the move, which the lemma does not decide.
By (iii), the confirmed count never falls below the second-smallest local count, so a single anomalous layer cannot determine it.

Algorithm 2 Task-level $k^{*}$ with seed range from the five-seed layer window

 1:

 window layers $\ell_{1}<\dots<\ell_{m}$ , seeds $R=\{1,\dots,5\}$ , class decisions $\mathcal{C}$ , dense AUROCs $A^{(r)}_{c,\ell}([d])$ and recorded prefix and L1 curves for every $(c,\ell,r)$ 

 2:

 task $k^{*}$ with seed range, task ceiling

 3:

 for $c\in\mathcal{C}$ do

 4:

    $C_{c}\leftarrow\max_{\ell,r}A^{(r)}_{c,\ell}([d])$ and $\tau_{c}\leftarrow 0.5+0.95\,(C_{c}-0.5)$ $\triangleright$ Equation 4

 5:

   for $r\in R$ do

 6:

    for $j=1,\dots,m$ do

 7:

       $k^{*}_{c,\ell_{j},r}\leftarrow$ Algorithm 1 applied to the recorded curves of $(c,\ell_{j},r)$ with threshold $\tau_{c}$ 

 8:

    end for

 9:

     $\tilde{k}_{c,r}\leftarrow\min_{j<m}\max(k^{*}_{c,\ell_{j},r},k^{*}_{c,\ell_{j+1},r})$ $\triangleright$ Equation 14

 10:

   end for

 11:

 end for

 12:

 for $r\in R$ do

 13:

    $K_{r}\leftarrow\operatorname{med}_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\tilde{k}_{c,r}$ $\triangleright$ Equation 15, $\infty$ above every finite value

 14:

 end for

 15:

 return $\operatorname{med}_{r}K_{r}$ , $[\min_{r}K_{r},\max_{r}K_{r}]$ , $\operatorname{med}_{c}C_{c}$ 

Design rationale.
Each aggregation step rules out one way of obtaining a small count by chance.
The common ceiling prevents a layer or seed with a weak dense probe from receiving an easier threshold.
Neighbor confirmation prevents a single fortunate layer from setting the count, which Lemma 1(iii) makes exact.
The fixed window of four layers prevents cells with more layers from receiving smaller counts through a minimum over more pairs.
The fixed test split across seeds isolates training-data variation in the seed range, and the medians over class decisions and seeds prevent one extreme class or one extreme subsample from setting the task value.

Figure 7: Local counts across depth on Gemma-2-9B and the five-seed window inside them.
Each panel shows one task in the mean-pooled representation.
The blue line traces the preliminary single-seed sweep on the full training split over the ten grid layers, giving at each layer the median local count over class decisions under the sweep’s ceiling for each decision.
An open circle at the $\infty$ tick marks a layer where no candidate qualified, which happens at layer 5 for sentiment and for harmfulness detection.
The shaded band covers the layers of the five-seed measurement, which are 24 to 35 for language identification, 9 to 20 for intent classification and banking intent, 13 to 24 for jailbreak detection, 17 to 28 for harmfulness detection, and the five-layer follow-up 13 to 28 for sentiment.
Inside the band, small gold dots give the median local count over class decisions for each seed under the common ceiling of the window, gold diamonds give the median of the five seeds at each layer, and the black line gives the reported count after neighbor confirmation and the medians over class decisions and seeds.
The reported counts read 1, 4, 8, 16, 72, and 341, and the median dense ceilings over class decisions read 1.000, 1.000, 1.000, 0.997, 0.980, and 0.979, in panel order.
The five seeds spread by at most one count at the window layers of language identification, intent classification, and jailbreak detection, and they spread from 61 to 128 at layer 17 for sentiment.
Neighbor confirmation keeps single-layer minima out of the reported count, as at layer 17 for jailbreak detection, where the seed median of 7 sits below the reported 8, and at layer 20 for harmfulness detection, where the seed median of 141 sits below the reported 341.
The sweep and the five-seed medians differ most for harmfulness detection at window layers 17 and 20, where the single-seed sweep reads 149 and 164 and the five-seed medians read 341 and 141.

Readability flag.
We report counts for all task–model pairs and flag task ceilings at or below 0.90 in every table.
Analyses that require reliable readability restrict to pairs above this value, as stated where used.
The flag is a reporting convention and does not alter any count.
A low ceiling makes the threshold easy to reach in absolute terms, so a small count at a low ceiling can describe a weak readout, which is why we do not interpret such counts as task sizes.

Takeaway for practitioners.
A count at a single layer or with a layer-specific ceiling can be made small by the choice of layer alone.
Reporting counts with a common ceiling, neighbor confirmation, and a fixed window size makes counts comparable across models, and the best-single-layer variant of Table 19 shows how much the ordering moves when these safeguards are dropped.

## A.1.7 Worked Example on One Cell

Question.
This section traces the procedure of Sections A.1.5 and A.1.6 through one task–model cell with its actual numbers, so that a reader can check every step of Algorithm 2 against Table 4.
The cell is harmfulness detection on Gemma-2-9B, which has one class decision, a four-layer window, and a reported count of 341 with seed range 332 to 344.

Data.
The window layers are 17, 20, 24, and 28, and the seeds are 1 to 5.
The fixed test split holds 2000 prompts, of which 1039 are harmful.
Each of the 20 layer and seed combinations has one dense probe, eleven prefix probes, and ten L1 fits, as in Algorithm 1.

The last row applies neighbor confirmation of Equation 14 within each seed.
Every local count in this cell comes from the L1 path.

Ceiling and threshold.
The largest dense AUROC over the 20 combinations is $C_{c}=0.9790$ , reached at layer 20 with seed 4, so Equation 4 gives $\tau_{c}=0.5+0.95\times 0.4790=0.9551$ .
The same threshold applies to all 20 combinations, including layer 28, whose own dense AUROCs of 0.9709 to 0.9724 lie 0.007 to 0.008 below the ceiling.
The gap between the ceiling and the threshold is 0.024 AUROC, and Section A.1.8 shows that the test-split standard error of this ceiling is far smaller than that gap.

Local counts.
At layer 20 the L1 path reaches $\tau_{c}$ with 137 to 142 features across the five seeds, and at layers 17, 24, and 28 it needs 332 to 369.
The ranked-prefix path never qualifies with fewer features than the L1 path at any layer or seed of this cell, so every local count in Table 4 is an L1 support size, which is why the counts are not powers of two.

Neighbor confirmation.
For seed 1, the three pairwise maxima of Equation 14 are 341, 342, and 369, namely $\max(341,137)$ , $\max(137,342)$ , and $\max(342,369)$ , and their minimum is 341.
The other seeds give 332, 342, 344, and 339.
The task-level count is the median of these five confirmed counts, 341, with seed range 332 to 344, which matches the reported cell.

What the example shows.
Layer 20 reads the task with fewer than half the features of its neighbors in every seed, so its smaller count is a stable property of that layer and not seed noise.
Neighbor confirmation nevertheless reports 341, because the protocol counts a size only when two adjacent grid layers both reach it, and no neighbor of layer 20 reaches 142.
The best-single-layer variant of Table 19 would report 141 for this cell.
The two readings answer different questions.
The confirmed count describes the size that the task needs across a span of depth, and the single-layer count describes the narrowest layer.
Lemma 1(iii) states the general form of this behavior, since the confirmed count can never fall below the second-smallest local count.
Across the 19 models, the two readings order the 14 core tasks alike at a median Spearman correlation of 0.98 in Table 19, so the choice between them moves absolute counts and not the task order.

## A.1.8 Finite-Sample Behavior of the Count

Question.
The count reads test AUROCs twice, once through the ceiling and once through the comparison with the threshold.
A reader can therefore ask how far the reported count can move away from the count that the same candidates would receive on the population of texts from which the test split is drawn.
This section answers the question in two steps.
A distribution-free bound states the worst case, and a data-dependent standard error measures the actual precision of every ceiling in the task matrix.

Assumption A (sampling of the test split).
Conditional on the training data, the positive test texts $\mathcal{P}_{c}$ are independent draws from one class-conditional distribution, the negative test texts $\mathcal{N}_{c}$ are independent draws from another, and the two samples are independent of each other.

Assumption A holds by construction for the native test splits, and it holds approximately for the grouped hold-outs of Section A.1.3, which sample problems or templates rather than single texts.
Every candidate of Equation 13, every dense probe, and every decision function depend on the training data only, so conditional on the training data the scorers are fixed functions.
Let $\mathcal{G}_{c}$ collect all scorers evaluated for class decision $c$ , namely the dense probes, the prefix probes, and the L1 fits over all window layers and seeds, and let $M=\lvert\mathcal{G}_{c}\rvert$ .
For a scorer $g$ , the population AUROC is

 $\bar{A}(g)=\Pr\bigl[g(X_{1})>g(X_{0})\bigr]+\tfrac{1}{2}\Pr\bigl[g(X_{1})=g(X_{0})\bigr],$ 

(17)

where $X_{1}$ and $X_{0}$ are independent draws from the positive and negative class-conditional distributions, and Equation 3 gives its empirical counterpart $\hat{A}(g)$ on the test split.

Lemma 2 (uniform deviation of test AUROCs)

Under Assumption A, for every $\delta\in(0,1)$ , with probability at least $1-\delta$ over the draw of the test split of class decision $c$ ,

 $\max_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{c}}\bigl\lvert\hat{A}(g)-\bar{A}(g)\bigr\rvert\leq\varepsilon_{\delta},\qquad\varepsilon_{\delta}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n^{\mathrm{te}}_{1}}+\frac{1}{n^{\mathrm{te}}_{0}}\Bigr)\log\frac{2M}{\delta}}.$ 

(18)

Proof. Fix $g$ .
The statistic $\hat{A}(g)$ is a function of $n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}+n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ independent texts, and its expectation is $\bar{A}(g)$ because each summand of Equation 3 has expectation $\bar{A}(g)$ .
Replacing one positive text changes at most $n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ of the $n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ summands, each by at most $1$ , so it changes $\hat{A}(g)$ by at most $1/n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}$ .
Replacing one negative text likewise changes $\hat{A}(g)$ by at most $1/n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ .
The bounded-differences inequality (McDiarmid et al., 1989) then gives, for every $\varepsilon>0$ ,

 $\Pr\bigl[\lvert\hat{A}(g)-\bar{A}(g)\rvert\geq\varepsilon\bigr]\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}(1/n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}})^{2}+n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}(1/n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}})^{2}}\Bigr)=2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{1/n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}+1/n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}}\Bigr).$ 

A union bound over the $M$ scorers and the choice $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\delta}$ bound the probability of the complementary event by $\delta$ .
 $\square$ 

Proposition 2 (the count is sandwiched by population counts)

Let $\varepsilon>0$ and suppose that $\lvert\hat{A}(g)-\bar{A}(g)\rvert\leq\varepsilon$ for every scorer $g\in\mathcal{G}_{c}$ .
For a threshold $t$ , let $\bar{k}(t)$ denote the local count of Equation 5 computed with population AUROCs $\bar{A}$ in place of $\hat{A}$ and with the fixed threshold $t$ , and let $\bar{\tau}_{c}=0.5+0.95\,(\bar{C}_{c}-0.5)$ , where $\bar{C}_{c}$ is the largest population AUROC among the dense probes.
Then every local count of class decision $c$ at every window layer and seed satisfies

 $\bar{k}\bigl(\bar{\tau}_{c}+1.95\,\varepsilon\bigr)\ \geq\ k^{*}_{c,\ell,r}\ \geq\ \bar{k}\bigl(\bar{\tau}_{c}-1.95\,\varepsilon\bigr),$ 

(19)

and by Lemma 1(iv) the same sandwich holds for the confirmed count and, when the premise holds for every $c\in\mathcal{C}$ , for the per-seed task value and the task-level $k^{*}$ .

Proof. The maximum is $1$ -Lipschitz in the sup norm, so the empirical ceiling satisfies $\lvert C_{c}-\bar{C}_{c}\rvert\leq\varepsilon$ and hence $\lvert\tau_{c}-\bar{\tau}_{c}\rvert\leq 0.95\,\varepsilon$ .
If a candidate satisfies $\bar{A}(S)\geq\bar{\tau}_{c}+1.95\,\varepsilon$ , then $\hat{A}(S)\geq\bar{A}(S)-\varepsilon\geq\bar{\tau}_{c}+0.95\,\varepsilon\geq\tau_{c}$ , so it qualifies empirically and the empirical minimum is at most its size.
This gives the left inequality.
If a candidate qualifies empirically, then $\bar{A}(S)\geq\hat{A}(S)-\varepsilon\geq\tau_{c}-\varepsilon\geq\bar{\tau}_{c}-1.95\,\varepsilon$ , so every empirically qualifying candidate also qualifies at the lower population threshold.
This gives the right inequality.
The extension to aggregated counts applies Lemma 1(iv) to the two bounding arrays.
 $\square$ 

What the worst case says.
Lemma 2 supplies a radius $\varepsilon_{\delta}$ that satisfies the premise of Proposition 2 with probability $1-\delta$ , and Proposition 2 then places the reported count between the population counts at two thresholds that differ from $\bar{\tau}_{c}$ by $1.95\,\varepsilon_{\delta}$ .
The relevant comparison is with the gap $0.05\,(C_{c}-0.5)$ between the ceiling and the threshold, which is at most 0.025 AUROC.
At $\delta=0.05$ the radius is 0.08 for the largest and most balanced test splits and 0.36 at the median class decision of the task matrix, where the rarer test class holds 40 texts and $M=440$ scorers enter the union bound.
The worst-case radius therefore exceeds the gap in every class decision, so the distribution-free bound establishes the form of the dependence but not a tight range for any individual count.
Two features of Equation 18 carry over to the tighter analysis below.
The radius is set by the rarer test class, and the number of scorers enters only through $\log M$ .

Data-dependent precision.
The worst case is loose for scorers whose AUROC lies near one, where the variance of Equation 3 is small.
We therefore measure the precision of every ceiling in the task matrix with the standard error of Hanley and McNeil (Hanley & McNeil, 1982), which approximates the sampling variance of an AUROC $A$ from the class counts alone,

 $\widehat{\mathrm{se}}(A)=\sqrt{\frac{A(1-A)+(n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}-1)(Q_{1}-A^{2})+(n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}-1)(Q_{2}-A^{2})}{n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}\,n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}}},$ 

(20)

with $Q_{1}=A/(2-A)$ and $Q_{2}=2A^{2}/(1+A)$ .
We evaluate Equation 20 at the ceiling $C_{c}$ of every class decision of the 14 core tasks on the 19 models, which gives 1025 class decisions, and we compare $1.96\,\widehat{\mathrm{se}}(C_{c})$ with the gap $0.05\,(C_{c}-0.5)$ .
If the gap exceeded this radius, a shift of the ceiling by its plausible sampling error would move the threshold by less than the distance a candidate has to cover, and the count would be insensitive to which test texts were drawn.

Table 5: Test-split precision of the 1025 class-decision ceilings.
The standard error is Equation 20 at the ceiling with the test class counts of the class decision, the worst-case radius is Equation 18 at $\delta=0.05$ , and the gap is $0.05\,(C_{c}-0.5)$ .
Readable class decisions are those with $C_{c}>0.90$ .

Result.
We make two observations on Table 5.
First, the median standard error of a ceiling is 0.0015 AUROC, one sixteenth of the median gap of 0.025, and $1.96\,\widehat{\mathrm{se}}(C_{c})$ lies below the gap in 99.4% of the 941 readable class decisions.
Second, the exceptions concentrate where the ceiling is low, since a ceiling of 0.62 has both a small gap of 0.006 and a large standard error, and such cells already carry the readability flag of Section A.1.6.
The worked example of Section A.1.7 illustrates the typical readable case, with a ceiling of 0.9790 on a test split of 1039 positives and 961 negatives, a standard error of 0.0032 AUROC, and a gap of 0.024 AUROC.

Scope.
Equation 20 is an approximation that treats the decision values as continuous and uses only the class counts, and it describes the ceiling and not the candidate curve, whose points share the same test texts.
Both summaries condition on the training data and therefore describe test-split variation only, while the seed range of Equation 16 describes training-data variation.
The two summaries answer different questions, and we report them separately.

Takeaway for practitioners.
The precision of a sparse-probe count is set by the rarer class of the test split, because both Equation 18 and Equation 20 scale with $1/n_{1}^{\mathrm{te}}$ and $1/n_{0}^{\mathrm{te}}$ .
A relative threshold at 95% of the above-chance ceiling leaves a gap of at most 0.025 AUROC, so a study should check that the standard error of its ceilings is small against that gap, and the check fails first for one-vs-rest decisions with few test positives and for tasks with low ceilings.

## A.1.9 Models and Probed Layers

Table 6 lists the ten base models, their instruction-tuned counterparts, and the layer grid of the preliminary sweep, from which the five-seed window of Section A.1.6 is drawn.
The Gemma-3 grids combine the layers with published Gemma Scope 2 SAEs and an even sweep over depth, giving 10 to 12 layers from block 1 to the last block.
The Gemma-2-9B grid takes every fourth block from 5 to 39 together with the two Gemma Scope layers 20 and 31, and the Llama, Mistral, OLMo, and Qwen grids take every fourth block from 3.
Each base model and its counterpart share the same grid, so the comparisons of Section 4 draw their windows from the same layer set.
Every model provides three representations at each probed layer, namely the mean-pooled residual, the last-position residual, and the SAE encoding of the last-position residual where a suite exists.
The main protocol reads the mean-pooled residual, and Section A.1.10 describes the use of the SAE encoding.

The SAE suite and Features columns name the suite used for the SAE representation and give its dictionary size.

## A.1.10 SAE Feature Protocol

Question.
The correspondence analysis of Section 3.2 compares individual features across models, which requires a basis whose coordinates are meant to be individually interpretable.
This section specifies the dictionaries and the protocol that selects SAE supports.

Encoders.
Each suite provides one SAE per layer that maps a residual vector $h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ to a sparse feature vector $g\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{sae}}}$ with $d_{\mathrm{sae}}\gg d$ .
Gemma Scope (Lieberum et al., 2024), Gemma Scope 2 (McDougall et al., 2025), and Llama Scope (He et al., 2024) use JumpReLU encoders (Rajamanoharan et al., 2024), which keep a pre-activation only above its threshold,

 $u=W_{\mathrm{enc}}h+b_{\mathrm{enc}},\qquad g_{j}=u_{j}\,\mathbf{1}[u_{j}>\theta_{j}],$ 

(21)

with $W_{\mathrm{enc}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{sae}}\times d}$ and $b_{\mathrm{enc}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{sae}}}$ , a threshold $\theta_{j}$ per feature for the two Gemma suites, and a shared scalar threshold for Llama Scope.
Llama Scope stores a dataset-level input normalization and unnormalized decoder columns, and we fold both into $W_{\mathrm{enc}}$ and $b_{\mathrm{enc}}$ so that Equation 21 applies to raw activations.
The folded encoder matches the suite’s reference implementation to within $10^{-6}$ .
Qwen-Scope (Deng et al., 2026) uses a TopK encoder (Gao et al., 2025),

 $g=\operatorname{TopK}_{50}\bigl(\operatorname{ReLU}(W_{\mathrm{enc}}h+b_{\mathrm{enc}})\bigr),$ 

(22)

which keeps the 50 largest pre-activations of each input and sets the rest to zero.

Models.
We use the 16384-feature Gemma Scope release whose average number of active features is closest to 70, the 16384-feature medium-sparsity tier of Gemma Scope 2, the 32768-feature Llama Scope release, and the 65536-feature Qwen-Scope release.
SAEs exist at every probed layer of the eight base models that have a suite.
For the instruction-tuned models used in the intervention experiments of Section 3.2, the Gemma-3 models use the instruction-tuned Gemma Scope 2 releases at every probed layer, and Gemma-2-9B-IT uses the instruction-tuned Gemma Scope releases at layers 9, 20, and 31.
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct has no instruction-tuned suite of its own, so it uses the base-model Llama Scope SAEs at every probed layer.

Procedure.
We encode the last-position residual of every text and store the result as a sparse matrix.
Probes on SAE features follow Sections A.1.4 and A.1.5 without change, except that standardization divides by the training standard deviation without centering, and the dense probe uses all $d_{\mathrm{sae}}$ features.
The prefix grid of Equation 12 stays at 1 to 1024, so a class decision whose SAE probe needs more than 1024 dictionary features reads $\infty$ unless the L1 path finds a smaller support.

Supports for feature correspondence.
The supports compared in Section 3.2 come from the preliminary sweep of Section A.1.6, which fits every probe on the full training split with one seed over the full grid.
For each class decision, the ceiling is that sweep’s ceiling across the three representations, all grid layers, and both search paths.
We choose one layer per task in the SAE representation by neighbor confirmation over class-decision medians, taking the pair of consecutive grid layers with the smallest larger median and the better layer of that pair.
At that layer, the support of each class decision is the smaller of the first qualifying ranked prefix and the smallest qualifying L1 support of Algorithm 1, returned as a set of dictionary indices.
Because the ceiling includes the residual representations, an SAE support qualifies only when the SAE probe reaches 95% of the above-chance AUROC of the best probe in any representation.

Scope.
Class decisions without a qualifying SAE support do not enter the correspondence tests, and Section 3.2 reports the resulting coverage.
Counts in the SAE basis and in the residual basis are not interchangeable, and the paper compares counts only within one basis.
Proposition 1 holds in the SAE basis for pure rescalings, so SAE counts refer to the dictionary’s own features in the same sense that residual counts refer to residual coordinates.

Design rationale.
The SAE ceiling includes the residual representations, so an SAE support must match the best readout available in any representation.
This choice prevents the correspondence analysis from comparing SAE features that read a task much worse than the residual stream does.

## A.1.11 Design Choices and Their Measured Effects

Question.
Each setting in Table 2 is a choice, and a reader can reasonably ask whether a different choice would change the conclusions.
Table 7 states the reason for each choice and the control that measures its effect.
The controls separate two kinds of dependence.
Some choices move every count by construction, and the relevant question is whether the task order survives.
Other choices change what the measurement reads, and the relevant question is how far the counts move.

Estimator of task order.
For the estimator variants, the task value of each model is the median over seeds of the per-seed task values, and infinite values share the largest rank.
The agreement between a variant and the main protocol on one model is the Spearman correlation (Spearman, 1987) of the task-level counts over the tasks that both protocols define,

 $\rho=\frac{\sum_{t}\bigl(\mathrm{rk}_{t}-\overline{\mathrm{rk}}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{rk}^{\prime}_{t}-\overline{\mathrm{rk}^{\prime}}\bigr)}{\sqrt{\sum_{t}\bigl(\mathrm{rk}_{t}-\overline{\mathrm{rk}}\bigr)^{2}\sum_{t}\bigl(\mathrm{rk}^{\prime}_{t}-\overline{\mathrm{rk}^{\prime}}\bigr)^{2}}},$ 

(23)

where $\mathrm{rk}_{t}$ and $\mathrm{rk}^{\prime}_{t}$ are the average ranks of task $t$ under the variant and the main protocol.
We skip a model when fewer than six tasks are defined under both protocols, and Table 19 reports the median and range of $\rho$ over the retained models for each variant.

Null.
If the estimator choices determined the conclusions, we would expect the task order of the 14 core tasks to reshuffle under each variant and $\rho$ to fall well below $1$ .

Result.
We make two observations.
First, the estimator choices move absolute counts but leave the task order nearly intact, which supports the cross-model comparisons of Section 3.
Second, the representation choices move absolute counts substantially, which is why the paper compares counts only within one basis, one input format, and one set of texts.

Table 7: Why each design choice was made, and what changing it does.
Task order is the Spearman correlation of Equation 23 between task-level $k^{*}$ under a variant and under the main protocol, as a median over the 19 models.
Appendix A.2.2 reports every control in full.

Choice

Reason

Measured effect of changing it

Relative threshold at 95%

A fraction of the above-chance ceiling applies the same relative requirement to tasks with different ceilings

90% and 97.5% keep the task order at 0.97 (Table 19)

Two search paths

The ranked path has one-octave resolution and scores features one at a time, and the L1 path selects them jointly

The ranked path alone keeps the order at 0.99 (Table 19)

Neighbor confirmation

A single layer can reach a small count by chance on a fixed test set

The best single layer keeps the order at 0.98 (Table 19), and Table 4 shows a cell where the two readings differ by a factor of 2.4

Linear probe

 $k^{*}$ measures linear readout, which the dense ceiling also measures

A one-hidden-layer MLP gives the same count on 5 of 7 tasks and one grid step apart on 2 (Table 18)

Model coordinates

The residual stream is the basis the model computes in, and Proposition 1 fixes the invariances

Random rotations raise counts by factors of 1.6 and 2.0 on two models, and by at most 20 in one cell (Table 15)

Original wording

The pools are fixed across models, so wording is held constant

Paraphrased test inputs raise counts by 1.5 to 10.8 on six cells (Table 16)

Class-balanced loss

One-vs-rest decisions have positive rates near 1%

Not varied, and held identical for every model and task

Shuffled labels

A chance reference for the search itself

The best AUROC at any $k$ stays at or below 0.577 on seven tasks of Gemma-2-9B (Table 18)

Confounds and the elements that rule them out.
The paragraphs headed Design rationale in this appendix organize the same design from the opposite direction, by naming each alternative explanation of a small or a large count together with the design element that removes it and the evidence that the removal works.
They cover data leakage and surface cues in the task pools, the common ceiling, neighbor confirmation, the fixed window, seed variation, test-split reuse, label noise, joint feature selection, units, basis, and the probe family of the linear readout.

Takeaway for practitioners.
The estimator choices move absolute counts but not the task order, so task rankings obtained with this protocol transfer across reasonable estimator settings.
Absolute counts move with the basis, the input format, and the wording of the inputs, so counts from two studies are comparable only when these three are the same.

## A.1.12 Selection Effects and Statistical Units

Test-set use.
Training data determine standardization, feature ranking, and the validation choice of regularization.
The reported count nevertheless reads the test curve to identify qualifying supports and layers, and the common ceiling is itself a maximum over test AUROCs.
The count is therefore a descriptive measurement on a fixed evaluation set, and Proposition 2 states how far it can lie from the corresponding population count for a given deviation of the test AUROCs.
Two selection effects act in opposite directions.
Taking the ceiling as a maximum over the 20 dense fits of a four-layer window raises the threshold, since $\mathbb{E}\max_{g}\hat{A}(g)\geq\max_{g}\bar{A}(g)$ , which pushes counts up.
Taking the minimum over candidates and layer pairs favors a fortunate fit, since $\mathbb{E}\min_{g}\hat{A}(g)\leq\min_{g}\bar{A}(g)$ for any fixed collection, which pushes counts down.
We do not claim that the two effects cancel.
Table 5 shows that the sampling error of the ceiling is small against the threshold gap in 99.4% of readable class decisions, neighbor confirmation requires two layers to agree, and the seed range reports how far the count moves when the training data change.
The test splits hold 472 to 3000 texts, and the shuffled-label control in Table 7 shows that the search does not produce high AUROCs from label noise.

Statistical units.
Table 8 states the unit and the meaning of every uncertainty summary and missingness convention in the paper.
Seed ranges, bootstrap intervals, and held-out prediction errors summarize different sources of variation, and we never combine them.

Table 8: Uncertainty and missingness conventions. Each quantity is interpreted at its actual unit of analysis, and the paper never combines quantities with different units.

## A.1.13 Estimator Variants Across Experiments

Later experiments reuse the probe fitting of Section A.1.4 but adapt the representation, the ceiling, or the aggregation to their own question.
Table 9 lists these variants so that a common symbol does not suggest interchangeable readings.
We compare counts only within one row of the table.
In every row, neighboring layers are adjacent entries in the probed layer set of the cell, not necessarily consecutive transformer blocks.
Lemma 1(i) and (ii) hold in every row, because each variant still takes a minimum over a finite candidate family against a threshold, and Lemma 1(iv) holds in every row whose aggregation composes minima, maxima, and medians.

## A.1.14 Software and Compute

Activation extraction uses the Hugging Face Transformers implementations of all models and runs on single GPUs.
Probe fitting runs on CPU, because each fit is a small convex problem and the cost lies in their number rather than their size.
At most 70 fits per class decision, layer, and seed give at most 1400 fits per class decision in the five-seed measurement of a four-layer window.
The main protocol draws its randomness from the fixed seed that builds every task pool and from the five measurement seeds, which fix the training subsample and the validation portion.
The only other source of variation is the L1 solver, whose coordinate order is not seeded and whose cap of 2000 iterations can leave an approximate solution, so an L1 support can differ between runs when the problem has several minimizers or the solver stops before convergence.

## A.1.15 Scope of the Measurement

The ceiling and $k^{*}$ describe what linear probes of the stated family can read from a frozen representation under the stated protocol.
Five consequences follow.
First, by Lemma 1(i) a finite $k^{*}$ upper-bounds the smallest linear support that reaches the threshold, and $\infty$ records that the search found none, not that none exists.
Second, the ceiling is a linear-probe reference and not an upper bound on nonlinear predictors or on what fine-tuning could reach.
Third, $k^{*}$ depends on the feature basis, the pooling position, the input format, the wording of the inputs, the data, the threshold, and the search, and Section A.1.11 reports how much each tested change moves it.
Fourth, Section A.1.8 describes variation over test texts under Assumption A and not variation over task definitions or data sources.
Fifth, a feature that predicts a label need not affect the model’s behavior, and the intervention experiments of Section 3.2 address that question separately.
The paper therefore compares counts only within a specified protocol and treats agreement across models as agreement in measured linear readouts rather than as evidence of shared computation.

## A.1.16 Recommendations for Measuring Readout Sparsity

The protocol and its controls suggest a reporting standard for studies that measure how many features a linear readout needs.
Each item below follows from a result stated in this appendix.

1. 

Report the dense ceiling and the sparse count together for every cell, since two tasks with nearly equal ceilings can differ in count by a factor of 64 (Section A.1.1).

2. 

Define the threshold relative to the ceiling above chance, and report the task order under at least one alternative threshold (Lemma 1(ii) and Table 19).

3. 

Take the dense ceiling as a common reference over all layers and seeds that enter the count, so that no layer or seed receives an easier threshold (Section A.1.6).

4. 

Require agreement of two neighboring layers before a size counts, and hold the number of probed layers fixed across cells (Lemma 1(iii) and Table 4).

5. 

Combine a univariate ranking with a joint selection method, and state the candidate family, since the count is an upper bound attached to that family (Lemma 1(i)).

6. 

Repeat the measurement over training subsamples on a fixed test split, report the seed range, and keep it separate from test-split uncertainty (Section A.1.8).

7. 

Require a minimum number of test positives for every class decision, and check the standard error of each dense ceiling against the gap between ceiling and threshold, since the size of the rarer test class sets the precision of every AUROC (Table 5).

8. 

Flag low ceilings and do not interpret the counts of such cells as task sizes (Section A.1.6).

9. 

Compare counts only within one basis, one input format, and one set of texts, and test the basis with a random rotation (Proposition 1 and Table 15).

10. 

Remove surface cues from the task pools and verify their removal with a cue-only classifier, as the length-matched jailbreak pool does (Section A.1.3).

## A.2 Details of the Cross-Model Agreement Experiments

This appendix gives the procedures, estimators, controls, and per-cell readings behind Section 3.
Every experiment reuses the measurement protocol of Appendix A.1 unless a paragraph states a change.
The appendix follows the order of the main text.
Section A.2.1 compares counts and task orders across models and tests whether the text alone predicts the shared order, and Section A.2.2 measures how far each protocol choice moves them.
Section A.2.3 compares the models with a bag-of-words reading of the raw text.
Sections A.2.4 and A.2.5 test whether selected SAE features correspond across models and compose within a model, and Section A.2.6 tests whether selected features matter for the model’s own behavior.
Table 10 maps every claim of Section 3 to its evidence.

The last column names the statistic and the reference value against which the main text reads each claim.

Design rationale.
Every experiment in this appendix answers a specific alternative explanation of a main-text finding, and its paragraph headed Design rationale names that alternative, the design element that rules it out, and the evidence that it does.
The design elements of these controls share one principle.
Each control holds fixed everything except the factor under suspicion, so that any change in the reading can be attributed to that factor alone.

Notation.
We write $\mathcal{M}$ for a set of models and $\mathcal{T}$ for a set of tasks.
For a model $m$ and a task $t$ , $k^{*}_{t,m}$ denotes the task-level count of Algorithm 2 and $C_{t,m}$ the task ceiling, namely the median of the class-decision ceilings $C_{c}$ of Equation 4.
A task is readable in model $m$ when $C_{t,m}>0.90$ , the readability flag of Appendix A.1.6.
Every count in this appendix is unrounded unless a caption says otherwise.

## A.2.1 Task Ordering Across Models

Question.
This section asks whether two models that differ in lab, scale, and training data assign similar counts to the same task, and whether they order the tasks alike.

Data and models.
The comparison uses the 14 core tasks of Table 3 on the ten base models of Table 6, and a second pass adds the nine instruction-tuned models.
Every model reads the same text pools, so any difference between two models comes from the models and not from the data.
The ten base models come from five labs and form $\binom{10}{2}=45$ pairs.
Six of the ten base models are Gemma models, so 15 pairs share a lab and 30 cross labs.
The 19 models of the second pass form 171 pairs, of which 69 share a lab and 102 cross labs.

Estimators.
We summarize agreement within a task by the cross-model spread ratio

 $\sigma_{t}=\frac{\max_{m\in\mathcal{M}}k^{*}_{t,m}}{\min_{m\in\mathcal{M}}k^{*}_{t,m}},$ 

(24)

computed over the models in which task $t$ is readable.
A ratio of 1 means identical counts, and a ratio of 2 means that the widest model needs one more step of the power-of-two grid of Equation 12 than the narrowest.
We summarize agreement across tasks by the Spearman correlation (Spearman, 1987) between the count vectors of two models $A$ and $B$ over a shared task set $\mathcal{T}_{AB}$ ,

 $\rho(A,B)=\frac{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{AB}}\bigl(r^{A}_{t}-\bar{r}^{A}\bigr)\bigl(r^{B}_{t}-\bar{r}^{B}\bigr)}{\sqrt{\sum_{t}\bigl(r^{A}_{t}-\bar{r}^{A}\bigr)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{t}\bigl(r^{B}_{t}-\bar{r}^{B}\bigr)^{2}}},$ 

(25)

where $r^{A}_{t}$ is the rank of $k^{*}_{t,A}$ among the counts of model $A$ on $\mathcal{T}_{AB}$ , tied values receive the average of their ranks, and $\bar{r}^{A}$ is the mean of these ranks over the shared task set.

Treatment of infinite counts.
A cell with $k^{*}=\infty$ has no finite size, and the task set $\mathcal{T}_{AB}$ admits three definitions.
The all treatment keeps every task and gives every infinite cell the largest tied rank of its model, which matches how Figure 1b places such cells.
The finite treatment drops a task when either model reads infinite on it.
The gated treatment additionally drops a task when either model’s ceiling falls below 0.90, which leaves only cells where a count is interpretable.
The main-text summary of Section 3 uses a fourth and stricter set, namely the nine tasks readable in all ten base models, so that every pair compares the same nine tasks.

Design rationale.
Two summaries answer two different doubts.
The spread ratio asks whether counts agree in absolute size within a task, and the rank correlation asks whether models agree on which tasks are narrow, which survives any uniform rescaling of a model’s counts.
Infinite and low-ceiling cells carry no interpretable size, and the three treatments bracket how we rank them, from keeping every cell to keeping only interpretable ones.
Splitting pairs by lab tests whether the agreement comes from shared training recipes, which would show as a same-lab advantage in the medians of Table 12.
The permutation test addresses the alternative that nine tasks with ties agree by chance, and the text-level control addresses the alternative that every model reads one order off the text, which would leave no agreement once the text-side order is removed.

Remark (invariance of the order statistic).
Let $g$ be any strictly increasing map on $[1,\infty]$ with $g(\infty)=\infty$ , and replace every count $k^{*}_{t,m}$ by $g(k^{*}_{t,m})$ in every model.
Then every correlation $\rho(A,B)$ of Equation 25 is unchanged under each of the three treatments, because a strictly increasing map preserves the order of any two counts and preserves equality, so it leaves every rank $r^{A}_{t}$ of every task unchanged, including the average ranks of ties and the tied maximal rank of infinite cells, and Equation 25 depends on the counts only through these ranks.

The invariance of the order statistic has three consequences for the analysis.
First, reporting $\log k^{*}$ instead of $k^{*}$ changes no correlation.
Second, an estimator change that multiplies every count of a model by a common factor leaves that model’s order correlation with the main protocol at exactly 1, so the estimator controls of Section A.2.2 measure task-specific distortion and not uniform rescaling.
Third, rounding is not strictly increasing because it can merge distinct values, which is why every correlation in this paper uses unrounded counts.

Permutation test.
The null hypothesis is that the ten models order the nine tasks independently of one another.
We shuffle the task labels within every model, which keeps each model’s ties, recompute the median of Equation 25 over the 30 cross-lab pairs, and repeat this $10^{5}$ times with seed 0.
A shuffled model enters all nine of its pairs, so the null distribution carries the dependence between pairs that share a model.
The test rejects only independence, since two instruments could agree through a shared text-derived order, which the text-level control below measures.

Count ranges and ceilings.
Across the ten base models, language identification has a median $k^{*}$ of 1, tool selection and routing require one or two features, and harmfulness detection ranges from 153 to 391.
Among the nine tasks readable in every model, $\sigma_{t}$ is at most 3.2, except for PII detection, which ranges from 1 to 8.
Below the readability flag, mathematical correctness ranges from 8 to 981 among the seven models with finite readings.
Ceilings differ by at most 0.05 across models on every readable task.
Table 11 lists the five tasks outside the common readable subset.

Rounding in Figure 2a.
A cell’s $k^{*}$ is a median over class decisions and then over seeds, so a cell with an even number of contributing values can land halfway between two grid points.
Six of the 90 cells in Figure 2a do.
Tool selection reads 1.5 on Gemma-3-12B, Mistral-7B, and OLMo-2-7B, intent classification reads 4.5 on Qwen3-8B, and banking intent reads 15.5 on Gemma-3-4B and 17.5 on Llama-3.1-8B.
The figure rounds these to the nearest integer and resolves a tie to the even value, so tool selection displays 2 and intent classification displays 4.
Every correlation in this paper uses the unrounded values, as the invariance of the order statistic requires.

Result for the main-text summary.
For the nine readable tasks, Equation 25 over the 30 cross-lab pairs gives a median of 0.9294 and a range of 0.7812 to 1.0000.
The permutation null has a median of $-0.004$ , a 99th percentile of 0.21, and a maximum of 0.42, so no permutation reaches the observed median and $p<10^{-5}$ .
The 15 same-lab pairs and all 45 pairs give the same result, at null 99th percentiles of 0.31 and 0.18.
This summary uses the same nine tasks for every pair, and the pairwise treatments below form a separate summary over all 14 tasks.

Result for the pairwise treatments.
We make two observations on Table 12.
First, the three treatments agree to within 0.05 in every column, so the stability of the order does not depend on how we handle unreadable cells.
Second, the same-lab and cross-lab ranges overlap substantially, and the medians show no consistent same-lab advantage across treatments.
Adding the nine instruction-tuned models leaves every median between 0.889 and 0.929.

Text-level control.
We run the probing pipeline of Appendix A.2.3 on TF-IDF features of the raw text and read, for each of the nine tasks, the prefix score $s(k)$ of Equation 30 at $k=4,8,16,64,256$ and the text-only ceiling.
With any one of these six readings as the control $z$ , we compute for every pair of models $(A,B)$ the partial rank correlation of their two $k^{*}$ orders,

 $\rho_{AB\cdot z}=\frac{\rho_{AB}-\rho_{Az}\,\rho_{Bz}}{\sqrt{\left(1-\rho_{Az}^{2}\right)\left(1-\rho_{Bz}^{2}\right)}},$ 

(26)

where each $\rho$ is the Spearman correlation of Equation 25 between two of the three task orders over the nine tasks (Table 13).
Each reading predicts part of every model’s order, with a median correlation of $-0.51$ to $-0.71$ across the ten models, because tasks that the text decides with few terms also admit sparse readouts.
After control, the median cross-lab partial correlation is 0.86 to 0.92, against 0.93 without control, and its lowest value of 0.860 comes with $s(256)$ as the control.
The agreement thus rests mostly on the part of the order that the text does not predict, such as the ranking of the seven tasks whose $s(16)$ lies between 0.88 and 1.00.

Rows are ordered by the median $k^{*}$ over the ten base models, rounded for display. The first summary row is the median over models of the Spearman correlation between each model’s $k^{*}$ and the column, and the second is the median over the 30 cross-lab pairs of the partial rank correlation of Equation 26 with the column as the control, against 0.929 without control.

Scope.
The correlations of Table 12 are measured over a fixed population of model pairs.
They establish that the tested models order the tasks alike under one measurement protocol, and Section A.2.2 measures how far that protocol can move the order.
The text-level control uses one text representation, and a richer text-only baseline could explain more of the order.

Finding.
Readout sparsity orders tasks alike across models and labs.
On the nine readable tasks the median cross-lab Spearman correlation is 0.93 and the lowest is 0.78, and same-lab pairs show no advantage in any treatment.
Controlling for text-side readings leaves the median at 0.86 to 0.92.
Sparse probing has so far characterized one model at a time (Gurnee et al., 2023), and the task order it yields is largely shared across ten base models from five labs.

Implication.
For ranking tasks by readout sparsity, one reference model can stand in for models of other labs, up to the residual disagreement that the lowest pair quantifies.
Absolute counts carry no such guarantee, as the protocol controls of the next section show.

## A.2.2 Protocol and Estimator Controls

Question.
The count of a task in one model depends on the representation basis, the input format, the wording of the inputs, and four estimator choices.
This section asks, for each of these, how far the choice moves the absolute count, the ceiling, and the task order.
Table 14 summarizes the answers, and the paragraphs below give each control in full.

Two tasks do not define an order, so the chat-template row reports none.

Random rotations of the feature basis.

Question.
A small $k^{*}$ has two possible sources.
The model may place task information in few residual coordinates, or a strong discriminative direction may be readable from few coordinates of any basis.
The rotation control separates the two.

Procedure.
For each rotation $r\in\{0,1,2\}$ we draw a $d\times d$ matrix with independent standard normal entries from a generator seeded with $1000+r$ and take the orthogonal factor $Q_{r}$ of its QR decomposition.
We multiply the training and test activations of the task’s neighbor-confirmed sweep layer by $Q_{r}$ , then rerun the full search of Algorithm 1, including standardization, with one seed on the full training split.
The unrotated reference uses the same layer, seed, and split, so its counts differ from the five-seed values of Table 11.
The control covers 22 cells on each of Gemma-2-9B and Gemma-3-4B, namely the 20 class decisions of intent classification together with the binary tasks harmfulness detection and mathematical-step correctness.

Remark (a rotation preserves linear readability).
Let $Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ be invertible.
The set of affine decision functions $\{x\mapsto w^{\top}x+b:w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ b\in\mathbb{R}\}$ equals the set $\{x\mapsto w^{\prime\top}Qx+b:w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ b\in\mathbb{R}\}$ , since the choice $w^{\prime}=Q^{-\top}w$ gives $w^{\prime\top}Qx=w^{\top}x$ and the choice $w=Q^{\top}w^{\prime}$ gives the converse.
The largest test AUROC that any dense linear probe attains is therefore the same on $x$ and on $Qx$ , because AUROC depends only on the values of the decision function on the test points.

This remark concerns the hypothesis class and not the fitted probe, because per-coordinate standardization and the penalty of Equation 7 are not rotation invariant.
A change in the fitted ceiling therefore measures the fitting procedure, and a change in $k^{*}$ measures how the discriminative direction distributes over coordinates.
Proposition 1 gives the complementary statement that coordinate-wise affine maps such as standardization change neither quantity.

Design rationale.
An orthogonal map is the one change that provably preserves linear readability while destroying the coordinate system, so any change in $k^{*}$ under it isolates the role of the model’s basis.
Fixing the 20-fold criterion before the run prevents an after-the-fact threshold from turning a modest effect into a claim of a privileged basis.

Null prediction and criterion.
If the model’s coordinates carried no special role, a random rotation would leave the counts unchanged.
If the task information sat on a few coordinates of the model’s basis, a rotation would spread it and raise the counts sharply.
As a heuristic, a unit direction concentrated on a few coordinates acquires small components on nearly all coordinates after a random rotation, which is the mechanism behind the second prediction.
Before running the control we fixed a 20-fold rise as the criterion for a privileged basis.

Estimator.
For a cell $c$ with unrotated count $k_{c}$ and rotated counts $k_{c,r}$ , the cell factor is $\phi_{c}=\operatorname{median}_{r}k_{c,r}/k_{c}$ .
We summarize a model by the median of $\phi_{c}$ over its 22 cells and test whether the cells rise more often than they fall with a two-sided sign test,

 $p_{\mathrm{sign}}=\min\Bigl\{1,\ 2\sum_{i=0}^{\min(n_{+},n_{-})}\binom{n}{i}2^{-n}\Bigr\},\qquad n=n_{+}+n_{-},$ 

(27)

where $n_{+}$ and $n_{-}$ count the cells whose factor $\phi_{c}$ exceeds or falls below 1, and the cells with $\phi_{c}=1$ , whose median rotated count equals the original, drop out.

Table 15: Effect of a random rotation on $k^{*}$ .
Entries are medians over the three rotations, and the intent row is additionally a median over the 20 class decisions.
The factor column is the median over cells of each cell’s own ratio, which is why it does not equal the ratio of the two preceding columns.

Result.
Rotation raises $k^{*}$ by a median factor of 1.59 on Gemma-2-9B, with an interquartile range of 1.00 to 2.00 and a maximum of 4.0.
There, 14 of the 22 cells rise while 3 fall and 5 stay put, and Equation 27 gives $p=0.013$ .
On Gemma-3-4B the median factor is 2.00, the interquartile range is 1.68 to 4.00, the maximum is 20.0, and 18 cells rise against 1 falling and 3 unchanged, giving $p=8\times 10^{-5}$ .
The rise is real and an order of magnitude short of the criterion we had fixed, and we report it against that criterion.
Taking the median over the three rotations in each cell, ceilings fall by at most 0.0002 on Gemma-2-9B and 0.0078 on Gemma-3-4B, and the worst single rotation lowers a ceiling by 0.0032 and 0.0131, as the remark on rotations leads one to expect.
Rank correlations between rotated and original counts over the 22 cells are 0.720 with $p=1.6\times 10^{-4}$ and 0.606 with $p=0.0028$ .
In both models the rotated intent decisions, at 8 and 12 features, stay far below the two wide tasks, at 142 to 251.
The two wide tasks exchange places on Gemma-3-4B, where step checking rises by 1.67 and harmfulness by 1.02, so the size of the rise has no consistent relation to encoding width.
The rise stays modest because the largest rotated count is 251 out of $d=3584$ , so a few hundred random mixtures still span a discriminative direction that lies in a high-variance subspace.

Scope.
The basis contributes a factor of one to four to the typical cell and up to twenty in the most affected cell, and it leaves the separation between narrow and wide tasks intact.
The result applies to one seed, one layer per task, and three rotations on two models.

Chat template.

Section 4 reports the four-cell design that crosses base and instruction-tuned weights of Gemma-2-9B with plain and chat-formatted inputs on harmfulness detection and fact checking.
Appendix A.3.2 gives the conditional effects, the interaction formula, the seed ranges, and the common-layer controls, and Table 14 summarizes only their implication for measurement sensitivity.
The design covers two tasks, so Table 14 reports no task order for this control.

Paraphrase.

Question.
This control asks whether a count describes a task itself or the particular wording of the test texts on which the count was originally measured.

Procedure.
We paraphrase a seeded subsample of at most 1500 texts from each task’s test split with Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 at temperature 0.7, discard refusals and overly short generations, and retain about 99% of the items.
Probes train on the original training activations and evaluate on the original and the paraphrased test sets, with Gemma-2-9B as the instrument.
The evaluation sets hold 1499 intent texts, 472 jailbreak prompts, 855 sentiment sentences, and 1498 harmfulness prompts, and the original arm evaluates on test sets of the same sizes.
The control uses one seed, the full training split, and neighbor confirmation over all ten grid layers.
A second run paraphrases the training split as well, capped near 3000 texts per task, with 2988, 1900, 2991, and 2997 training texts, and adds an original-text baseline with the same training sizes.

Estimator.
Write $k^{*}_{a\to b}$ for the count when probes train on distribution $a$ and test on distribution $b$ , with $o$ the original texts, $o^{\prime}$ the size-matched original subset, and $p$ the paraphrases.
The expansion factor of Table 16 then decomposes exactly into three ratios,

 $\frac{k^{*}_{o\to p}}{k^{*}_{o\to o}}=\underbrace{\frac{k^{*}_{o^{\prime}\to o^{\prime}}}{k^{*}_{o\to o}}}_{\text{sample size}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{k^{*}_{p\to p}}{k^{*}_{o^{\prime}\to o^{\prime}}}}_{\text{intrinsic width}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{k^{*}_{o\to p}}{k^{*}_{p\to p}}}_{\text{distribution shift}},$ 

(28)

which holds as an identity because the intermediate counts cancel.
The first ratio isolates the smaller training set, the second measures how much wider the paraphrased distribution is when read on its own terms, and the third measures the extra cost of reading across the two distributions.

Design rationale.
A single cross-distribution comparison confounds the three effects of Equation 28, and the four train and test combinations separate them.
The size-matched original arm removes the smaller paraphrased training set as an explanation, and the paraphrased-to-paraphrased arm measures the width of the new distribution without any transfer across distributions.

Null prediction.
If a count described only the task, paraphrasing would leave all three ratios near 1.

Result.
We make two observations on Tables 16 and 17.
First, the paraphrased distribution is intrinsically wider on five of the six cells, by 1.2 to 5.6, and reading across distributions adds a further 1.5 to 1.9 on the three binary tasks and 0.8 to 1.1 on the intents.
Second, jailbreak detection expands the most in both parts, which fits the reading that jailbreak detection partly rests on surface wording.
The sample-size ratio stays at one grid step or less in every cell.
Ceilings stay at or above 0.947 everywhere, and the order of the four tasks holds in all four train and test combinations, while within the intent task two class decisions exchange places and ties at 8 appear.

Scope.
The result holds for one paraphrasing model, one paraphrase seed, and one instrument, Gemma-2-9B.
It shows that absolute counts belong to a fixed set of texts, which is why the paper compares counts across models only on identical text pools.

Probe family and probe capacity.

Question.
Two standard objections apply to any sparse-probe count such as $k^{*}$ .
A nonlinear probe might read the same class decision from fewer features, which would make $k^{*}$ a property of linear readability rather than of the representation, and a finite count might reflect probe capacity rather than signal in the activations.

Procedure.
We reuse the same feature ranking, the same grid of $k$ , and the same validation split, and replace the linear probe with a one-hidden-layer MLP in one arm and permute the training labels in the other.
The MLP has 32 or 128 hidden units and an $\ell_{2}$ penalty of $10^{-3}$ or $10^{-1}$ , chosen on the validation split at every $k$ exactly as the linear probe chooses $\gamma$ , and it trains with early stopping.
The control uses Gemma-2-9B, one seed, the full training split, and one layer per task.

Design rationale.
Both arms reuse the ranking, the grid, and the validation split of the linear arm, so a difference can come only from the probe family or from the labels.

Null prediction.
If the search could manufacture a finite count from noise, the permuted-label arm would reach AUROCs near the thresholds of the real tasks.
If linearity of the probe limited the count, the MLP arm would reach the same threshold with fewer features.

The last column is the highest test AUROC reached at any $k$ on the grid after permuting the training labels.
This control uses one seed, the full training split, and one layer per task, so its absolute values differ from the five-seed values of Table 11.

Result.
Five of the seven tasks return the same count under both probe families.
Harmfulness improves by one grid step and fact checking worsens by one, and each grid step doubles $k$ , so neither move exceeds the resolution of the search.
Shuffled labels reach at most 0.577 anywhere on the grid, far below every threshold of the real tasks, so the search does not manufacture a finite $k^{*}$ .
Both arms share their data and their selection rule, which makes the comparison internal to this table.

Scope.
The comparison covers one model, one layer per task, and two MLP widths, and it omits the seed averaging, the subsampling, and the neighbor confirmation of the main protocol.

Estimator variants.

Procedure.
We vary one estimator choice at a time and correlate the resulting task order against the main protocol by Equation 25, separately in each of the 19 models.
The threshold variants replace the factor 0.95 of Equation 4 by 0.90 or 0.975.
The search variant drops the L1 path of Algorithm 1.
The layer variant replaces the neighbor-confirmed count of Equation 14 by the smallest local count at any single layer.
Infinite counts take the tied maximal rank.

Null prediction.
By Lemma 1(ii), the threshold variants move every count in a known direction, and by the invariance of the order statistic a uniform move leaves the order intact.
The correlations therefore test whether the moves are uniform enough across tasks to preserve the order.

Table 19: Task order under estimator variants.
Each entry is a Spearman correlation against the main protocol over the 14 core tasks, summarized over the 19 models.

Variant
Median
Range

Threshold at 90% of the ceiling
 $0.97$ 
 $0.93$ to $0.99$ 

Threshold at 97.5% of the ceiling
 $0.97$ 
 $0.91$ to $0.99$

 $0.99$ 
 $0.91$ to $1.00$ 

Best single layer, no neighbor confirmation
 $0.98$ 
 $0.93$ to $0.99$ 

Result.
Every variant keeps the task order at a median of 0.97 or more over the 19 models, and the lowest single model reaches 0.91.
The estimator choices therefore move absolute counts but leave the task order nearly intact, while the representation and input changes of the upper block of Table 14 move absolute counts by larger factors of 1.5 to 20.

Finding.
The controls separate two kinds of dependence on the protocol.
Estimator choices keep the task order at a median correlation of 0.97 or more.
Changes of basis and wording move absolute counts by factors of 1.5 to 20, while ceilings move by at most 0.03 and the order of the tested cells holds.
The count is therefore a property of a task read in a fixed basis on a fixed set of texts, and its rank among tasks is the part that transfers across models.

Implication.
A reported $k^{*}$ should state its basis, its input format, and its text pool, and comparisons of absolute counts should hold all three fixed.
Among the tested factors, wording and basis move counts the most, so they deserve the first control in any new study.

## A.2.3 Bag-of-Words Input Baseline

Question.
This baseline asks whether a sparse readout merely restates that a task is easy to decide from its raw text alone, with no learned representation.

Procedure.
We run the entire probing pipeline on TF-IDF features of the raw text over all 24 tasks, with unigrams and bigrams, sublinear term frequency, and a vocabulary capped at 30000 terms.
The baseline uses the same dense probe, the same prefix grid, the same L1 path, and the same threshold as the model side, and Gemma-2-9B serves as the model-side instrument.

Design rationale.
Running the identical pipeline on the raw text gives the model a reference instrument with no learned representation.
A task whose model-side reading merely reflected how easily its text decides it would show the same ceiling and the same prefix curve on both instruments.

Estimators.
Let $A_{t}(k)$ denote the best test AUROC at $k$ features for task $t$ in one representation, and let $C_{t}$ be that representation’s own ceiling.
Two crossing points summarize a curve,

 $k^{\mathrm{first}}_{t}=\min\{k:A_{t}(k)\geq\tau_{t}\},\qquad k^{\mathrm{stable}}_{t}=\min\{k:A_{t}(k^{\prime})\geq\tau_{t}\ \text{for all}\ k^{\prime}\geq k\},$ 

(29)

and they coincide exactly when the curve never falls back below $\tau_{t}$ after its first crossing.
The robust statistic is the prefix score, the running maximum of the normalized AUROC,

 $\bar{s}_{t}(k)=\max_{k^{\prime}\leq k}\ \frac{A_{t}(k^{\prime})-0.5}{C_{t}-0.5},$ 

(30)

which is non-decreasing in $k$ by construction and moves only when a new point exceeds all earlier points on the curve.
We compare the two representations at a fixed $k$ of 64 or 256 with a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test (Wilcoxon, 1945) over the 24 tasks on the per-task differences of prefix scores $\delta_{t}=\bar{s}^{\mathrm{model}}_{t}(k)-\bar{s}^{\mathrm{text}}_{t}(k)$ , dropping tasks with $\delta_{t}=0$ .

The ceiling axis.
Input-side and model-side ceilings correlate at $\rho=0.950$ with $p=1.3\times 10^{-12}$ over the 24 tasks.
The model exceeds the input baseline by a median of 0.026 AUROC.
The largest gains are $+0.153$ on hate speech, $+0.133$ on relevance, and $+0.130$ on fact checking, and the only negative value is $-0.033$ on emotion.
Ceilings move by at most 0.0024 between independent runs, so this comparison rests on a stable statistic.
Readability is therefore mostly a property of the text, and the ceiling axis measures what the text decides at least as much as what the model contributes.

Why we report no input-side $k^{*}$ .
TF-IDF accuracy is flat and non-monotone near the threshold, so $k^{\mathrm{first}}_{t}$ is decided by differences of order 0.001.
In 14 of the 24 tasks, $k^{\mathrm{first}}_{t}\neq k^{\mathrm{stable}}_{t}$ .
Rerunning the same pipeline with a different task list moves fact checking from 5591 features to 512 while its ceiling moves from 0.7885 to 0.7870, and moves harmfulness from 5705 to 8233 at an unchanged ceiling.
Fact checking crosses at 512 by $+0.0012$ and falls back by $-0.0066$ at 1024.
Every per-task ratio between input-side and model-side counts therefore rests on a crossing point that the data do not identify, and we report no such ratio.

Table 20: Fraction of its own ceiling each representation reaches within $k$ features.
Entries are medians over the 24 tasks of the prefix score of Equation 30, the model is Gemma-2-9B, and the $p$ value comes from a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test over tasks.

Features
Bag of words
Model
Model higher
Wilcoxon $p$

Result.
The model reaches more of its own ceiling within 64 features, and the difference is no longer significant at 256.
The comparison favors the model in one respect and the text in another.
The model selects from 3584 residual coordinates while the baseline selects from up to 30000 terms, so a larger pool of candidates serves the baseline.
Each residual coordinate, however, already aggregates the whole input, while each term reads one surface pattern.
The table therefore describes the two representations under a common search and does not isolate the model’s contribution.

Scope.
The same bag-of-words readings serve as the text-level control of the cross-model task order in Appendix A.2.1, where they predict part of each model’s order and leave the median cross-lab partial correlation at 0.86 to 0.92 against 0.93 without control.

Finding.
Dense readability is mostly a property of the text.
Input-side and model-side ceilings correlate at 0.950, and the model adds a median of 0.026 AUROC.
The model’s advantage lies in sparsity, since within 64 features it reaches 0.991 of its own ceiling against 0.919 for the text.

Implication.
A probing study should report a text-only baseline under the same pipeline, since a high ceiling alone says little about what the model contributes.
On flat, non-monotone curves such as those of the TF-IDF baseline, the first crossing point of a threshold is not identified, and the running-maximum prefix score of Equation 30 is the statistic to report instead.

## A.2.4 Cross-Model Feature Matching

Question.
Equal counts in two models leave open whether the two models use corresponding features.
This section asks whether the SAE features that each model selects for a class decision vary alike across individual inputs, beyond what their shared label explains.

Table 21: Where feature correspondence can be tested and what it shows.
The upper block reports the three tasks the matching test covers.

The double dagger marks tool selection, whose four class decisions give a leave-one-out dictionary of a median 10 features, so its recovery reflects the power of the protocol rather than a negative result.
The lower block lists the remaining core tasks with the number of the eight models that hold a support.
Sections A.2.4 and A.2.5 give the per-pair and per-model readings.
