Data and models.
Matching runs on the eight base models with an available SAE suite, namely Gemma-3-270M, Gemma-3-1B, Gemma-3-4B, Gemma-2-9B, Gemma-3-12B, Gemma-3-27B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Qwen3-8B.
They give 28 pairs, of which 13 cross labs and 15 pair two Gemma models.
The shared inputs are the tasks’ test pools, which contain 3000 texts each for intent classification and language identification and 840 texts for tool selection.

Supports.
Supports come from the SAE protocol of Appendix A.1.10, read at the last token.
A class decision holds a support only when some subset of SAE features within the search reaches 95% of that decision’s best probe in any representation at the task’s neighbor-confirmed SAE layer.
A class decision enters a pair only when both models hold a support for it.
The number of tests per pair is therefore the size of that intersection, which ranges from 11 to 19 on intent classification.

Estimator.
For a model pair $(A,B)$ and class decision $c$ with binary labels $y\in\{0,1\}^{n}$ , let $X^{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k_{A}}$ and $X^{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k_{B}}$ contain the selected features on the $n$ shared test inputs, and let $n_{v}$ be the number of inputs with label $v$ .
Shared label responses can by themselves induce activation agreement between two models, so we compare response profiles within each class.
We remove each feature’s mean within each class and scale by the resulting standard deviation,

 $\widetilde{X}^{A}_{ij}=X^{A}_{ij}-\frac{1}{n_{y_{i}}}\sum_{u:y_{u}=y_{i}}X^{A}_{uj},\qquad P^{A}_{ij}=\frac{\widetilde{X}^{A}_{ij}}{\operatorname{sd}(\widetilde{X}^{A}_{:j})},\qquad R=\frac{|(P^{A})^{\top}P^{B}|}{n},$ 

(31)

where $\operatorname{sd}$ uses the denominator $n$ , the absolute value acts entrywise, and a zero standard deviation is replaced by one so that a constant centered column stays at zero.
With $q=\min(k_{A},k_{B})$ and $\mathcal{M}_{q}$ the one-to-one matchings of cardinality $q$ between the columns of $P^{A}$ and $P^{B}$ , the statistic is

 $T(A,B;c)=\max_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{q}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{(i,j)\in M}R_{ij}.$ 

(32)

The Hungarian algorithm (Kuhn, 1955) solves this rectangular assignment exactly.

Lemma 3 (what the matching statistic ignores)

The following hold for Equations 31 and 32.

1. 

For any functions $f_{j}:\{0,1\}\to\mathbb{R}$ , replacing $X^{A}_{ij}$ by $X^{A}_{ij}+f_{j}(y_{i})$ leaves $P^{A}$ unchanged.

2. 

For any nonzero scalars $a_{j}$ , replacing $X^{A}_{:j}$ by $a_{j}X^{A}_{:j}$ leaves the $j$ th row of $R$ unchanged.

3. 

Every entry of $R$ lies in $[0,1]$ , so $T(A,B;c)\in[0,1]$ , and $T(A,B;c)=T(B,A;c)$ .

Proof. For the first claim, the added term is constant within each class, so the within-class centering of Equation 31 removes it exactly.
For the second claim, scaling by $a_{j}$ scales the centered column and its standard deviation by $a_{j}$ and $|a_{j}|$ , so $P^{A}_{:j}$ changes at most in sign, and the absolute value removes the sign.
For the third claim, every nonconstant column of $P^{A}$ has mean zero and $\lVert P^{A}_{:j}\rVert_{2}^{2}=n$ , so $R_{ij}$ is an absolute Pearson correlation and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality bounds it by 1.
A constant column gives $R_{ij}=0$ .
Transposing $R$ maps matchings of $(A,B)$ onto matchings of $(B,A)$ with the same value.
 $\square$ 

Lemma 3 states precisely what a high matching score can and cannot reflect.
The first claim shows that agreement in class means, however large, contributes nothing, so a high score requires agreement in how responses vary among inputs of the same class.
The second claim shows that the statistic ignores the scale and the sign of each selected feature, so a high score does not imply identical activation signs or identical computations in the two models.

Design rationale.
Two models that both read a label correlate on every input merely because both respond to that label.
The statistic removes this source by construction, as Lemma 3 shows, and the null removes the next source, namely that any features correlated with the label might agree within class as well.
Drawing the null from unselected features of the same model keeps the dictionary, the layer, and the inputs fixed, so the comparison isolates the selection itself.

Null.
Two selected sets can agree within class merely because both contain features correlated with the label, so the null must match label correlation.
For each selected feature $j$ of model $B$ , let $\lambda_{j}=|\operatorname{corr}(X^{B}_{:j},y)|$ be its absolute point-biserial correlation with the label on the shared inputs.
We form a candidate list $\mathcal{N}_{j}$ from the 50 nonconstant, unselected features of $B$ whose label correlations lie closest to $\lambda_{j}$ .
Each null draw $b$ samples one replacement uniformly from each list $\mathcal{N}_{j}$ , independently across $j$ , and recomputes Equation 32 with model $A$ ’s profiles unchanged.
Independent sampling can draw the same replacement for two selected features, so a null set is a collection with multiplicity.
The construction preserves the number of columns and approximately preserves their label correlations.
With 1000 draws $T^{(1)},\dots,T^{(1000)}$ , we define the exceedance indicator and the standardized score,

 $I_{ABc}=\mathbf{1}\bigl\{T(A,B;c)>Q_{0.99}(T^{(1)},\ldots,T^{(1000)})\bigr\},\qquad z_{ABc}=\frac{T(A,B;c)-\overline{T}_{\rm null}}{\operatorname{sd}(T_{\rm null})+10^{-12}},$ 

(33)

where $Q_{0.99}$ is the empirical 0.99 quantile with linear interpolation and the exceedance is strict.

Null prediction.
If the selected features of $B$ corresponded to those of $A$ no better than label-matched unselected features do, $T(A,B;c)$ would be exchangeable with the null draws up to the approximation in the matching of label correlations.
The pass rate would then sit near the nominal 1%.
This 1% is a nominal tail expectation for the resampling construction and not a separately calibrated error rate.

Algorithm 3 Cross-model correspondence with a label-correlation-matched null

 1:

 ordered models $A,B$ , selected supports, aligned test inputs and binary labels

 2:

 Verify that the label vectors agree and retain class decisions with supports on both sides

 3:

 for each retained class decision $c$ do

 4:

   Center and scale the selected profiles and compute $T(A,B;c)$ by Equation 32

 5:

   Form the 50-feature candidate lists $\mathcal{N}_{j}$ from model $B$ ’s nonconstant unselected features

 6:

   Seed the generator with the run seed and the CRC32 checksum of the ordered pair and the class name

 7:

   for $b=1,\ldots,1000$ do

 8:

    Sample one replacement per selected $B$ feature independently, keeping multiplicities

 9:

    Recenter, rescale, and solve the rectangular assignment to obtain $T^{(b)}$ 

 10:

   end for

 11:

   Record $I_{ABc}$ , $z_{ABc}$ , and both support sizes by Equation 33

 12:

 end for

Aggregation.
For a task with eligible comparisons $\mathcal{E}$ , each one model pair on one class decision with supports on both sides, the pooled pass rate and the pair-level pass rate are

 $\widehat{p}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{(A,B,c)\in\mathcal{E}}I_{ABc},\qquad\widehat{p}_{AB}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}_{AB}|}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{AB}}I_{ABc},$ 

(34)

where $\mathcal{C}_{AB}$ is the set of class decisions eligible for the pair $(A,B)$ .
The pooled rate weights every test equally, and a median of $\widehat{p}_{AB}$ over pairs weights every pair equally.
The order of the model list fixes which side supplies replacements, so reversing that order can change the null and the reported rates even though the observed score is symmetric by Lemma 3.
Seeding each pair and class decision separately ensures that adding a model does not change the random draws of existing pairs.

Dependence.
Tests that share a model or a class decision share data, so the tests in $\mathcal{E}$ are not independent.
We therefore read $\widehat{p}$ as a descriptive rate against the nominal 1% and do not convert it into a single $p$ value.
The test labels enter both the centering and the null construction, so the procedure describes correspondence on the shared evaluation pool.

Result by task.
Intent classification exceeds the null in 56.2% of 447 tests at a median $z$ of $+3.08$ , with 84% of all $z$ scores positive.
Language identification reaches 48.9% of 280 at $+3.71$ , and tool selection reaches 34.3% of 105 at $+1.68$ .
The 13 cross-lab pairs match at median rates of 0.50, 0.30, and 0.50 on the three tasks, with ranges of 0.36 to 0.69, 0.20 to 0.50, and 0.00 to 0.75, so one cross-lab pair matches on no tool-selection decision.
The strongest cross-lab pair on intent classification is Qwen3-8B with Gemma-3-27B at a match rate of 0.69, and the highest median $z$ of the whole table is $+4.66$ on Gemma-3-12B with Llama-3.1-8B at a rate of 0.68.
Figure 8 lists all 28 pairs.

Figure 8: Match rate for every model pair on intent classification.
Each bar is the fraction of that pair’s shared intents whose one-to-one feature match exceeds the label-correlation-matched null’s 99th percentile.
The dashed line marks the 1% rate the null allows, and every pair clears it.
Dots mark cross-lab pairs.
Pairs containing Gemma-3-270M occupy most of the lower half.

Degenerate comparisons.
Language identification needs one further split, because at least one support has size 1 in 245 of its 280 tests, and both supports have size 1 in 189 of them.
With $q=1$ the statistic reduces to one absolute correlation when both supports have size 1, and to the largest absolute correlation between the single feature and the other side’s features otherwise, and in either case it must still exceed the null threshold to pass.
Seven of the ten language decisions have single-feature supports in most eligible model pairs.
Its 35 tests with larger supports reach 60.0% at a median $z$ of $+4.92$ , and the 66 such tests of tool selection reach 30.3% at $+1.48$ .

Result by class decision.
Match rates in Table 22 vary far more across the 19 intents, from 0.14 to 1.00, than across the 28 pairs, from 0.18 to 0.76.
Figure 9 shows the distribution of the standardized scores of all 832 tests and their relation to the mean support size.

Subsampling control for large supports.

Question.
One-to-one matching may become conservative as supports grow, since the average of Equation 32 runs over more matched pairs and a large assignment has more ways to fall short of the null.
This control asks whether the low scores of large supports reflect that property of the statistic or an absence of correspondence.

Procedure.
We cut the second model’s support to 20 features at random, redraw five times, keep the first model’s support whole, and draw the null at the reduced size.

Null prediction.
If large supports failed only because of the conservativeness of the statistic, reduction to 20 features would restore their pass rates to those of the three converging intents.

The upper group matches at full size and the lower group, the four intents with supports of 50 or more, does not.

Result.
We make two observations on Table 23.
First, the three converging intents still exceed the null in 57% of their reduced tests, so reduction preserves a real signal.
Second, the four intents with large supports reach only 12 to 27%, so their low rate is not an artifact of support size alone, although that rate still exceeds the nominal 1% and is consistent with partial correspondence.
The deep negative scores do disappear, so their magnitude measures the conservativeness of the statistic on large sets and carries no evidence against correspondence.

Model scale.

We group the 28 pairs by the smallest model they contain and take the median of $\widehat{p}_{AB}$ in each group, separately for each of the three tasks.

A pair enters a task’s column only when that task has at least one test for it.

Only intent classification rises with model scale, from 0.36 to 0.63, and the ten pairs among models of 7B and above give the same median of 0.63.
Language identification peaks in the middle group, at 0.65, tool selection falls and then rises, and the three groups overlap in their ranges, so these readings support no scale threshold and no general scale trend.

Coverage.

Only intent classification, language identification, and tool selection provide at least 100 tests each among the 14 core tasks.
The remaining tasks lack enough SAE supports or comparisons with more than one feature on each side.
Routing has one class decision with supports of one or two features, so its 21 pairwise tests are single, mostly degenerate comparisons, and we report its coverage without a match rate.
Sentiment returns zero testable cells rather than a negative result.
Only Qwen3-8B records a support for it, with 32 features, while the other seven models place their neighbor-confirmed SAE layer at layer 1, 3, or 5 and reach no qualifying subset there.
The five tasks listed last in Table 21 hold an SAE support on none of the eight models, and their residual-basis ceilings of 0.62 to 0.89 in Table 11 show that the models read them poorly in every basis we have.
The correspondence test therefore applies to readable tasks with SAE supports in at least two models.

Finding.
Selected SAE features vary alike across models among inputs of the same class.
After class means are removed and against a null matched in label correlation, 56.2% of intent tests pass where the null allows 1%, and the 13 cross-lab pairs pass at a median rate of 0.50.
Correspondence varies more across class decisions, from 0.14 to 1.00, than across model pairs, from 0.18 to 0.76.
Earlier comparisons of units or features across networks match them by activation correlation on shared inputs (Li et al., 2015; Lan et al., 2024), and such correlations include the agreement that a shared label induces.
The present test removes that agreement exactly, by Lemma 3, before any feature is matched.

Implication.
Cross-model feature matching should remove class means and compare against a label-correlation-matched null, otherwise two models that merely read the same label appear to share features.
Agreement under this test concerns measured responses on shared inputs and does not by itself establish a shared computation across the two models.

## A.2.5 Leave-One-Out Dictionary Test

Question.
Within one model, the class decisions of a multiclass task each have their own SAE support.
This test asks whether those supports draw on a shared family dictionary or whether each class decision carries private features that no other class decision uses.

Procedure.
For a class decision $i$ with support $S_{i}$ , we take the union of the other class decisions’ supports as a dictionary and refit $i$ ’s probe on those features alone,

 $U_{-i}=\bigcup_{j\neq i}S_{j},\qquad\mathrm{rec}_{i}=\frac{A_{i}(U_{-i})-0.5}{C_{i}-0.5},\qquad\mathrm{own}_{i}=\frac{|S_{i}\cap U_{-i}|}{|S_{i}|},$ 

(35)

where $A_{i}(U)$ is the test AUROC of the probe of Section A.1.4 restricted to $U$ and $C_{i}$ is the ceiling of class decision $i$ .
A recovery of 1 means that a class decision that contributed nothing to the dictionary is still fully expressible in it.
Own overlap separates composition from plain inclusion, since a class decision whose support already lies in the dictionary recovers trivially.
The null replaces $U_{-i}$ by a random set of $|U_{-i}|$ SAE features, drawn with five seeds.
The width of the expression is the smallest prefix of the dictionary, ranked by the $t$ -statistic of Equation 11 within it, that clears the threshold $\tau_{i}$ .
The test uses the SAE layers 28 of Gemma-2-9B, 23 of Llama-3.1-8B, and 23 of Qwen3-8B.

Design rationale.
Leaving the class decision out of its own dictionary prevents trivial recovery, and own overlap measures how much trivial recovery remains.
The random dictionary of equal size separates composition from the generic power of many features.

Criteria and null prediction.
Before running the test we fixed two criteria, a median recovery of at least 0.9 and recovery above the random null.
If each class decision relied on private features, recovery from $U_{-i}$ would stay near the random null once own overlap is low.

Recovery, the null, and own overlap are medians over class decisions, and the minimum is the lowest recovery of any class decision.

Result.
On intent classification, 18 of 20, 17 of 19, and 17 of 18 class decisions reach a recovery of 0.9 on Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-2-9B, and Qwen3-8B.
All 57 class decisions across the three models exceed their own random control, with Wilcoxon $p$ values of $1.9\times 10^{-6}$ , $3.8\times 10^{-6}$ , and $7.6\times 10^{-6}$ in the same order.
Own overlap stays between 0.11 and 0.24, so 76 to 89% of a held-out support lies outside the dictionary and is nonetheless expressible within it.
Language identification reproduces the result on three models at an own overlap of at most 0.08.

Composability and minimality.
Among the class decisions for which some prefix of the dictionary clears the threshold, which are 11 of 20, 14 of 19, and 14 of 18 on the three models, reading a held-out intent from the dictionary takes a median of 64 to 128 features, while the per-model medians of the intents’ own shortest supports are 12 to 22, and the median over class decisions of the per-decision ratio is 3.8 to 5.2.
A shared dictionary can express every member of the family, and each member also has a shorter private support.
The quantity $k^{*}$ measures the second of these.

Scope.
The test has power only when a task has enough class decisions to build a large dictionary.
Tool selection has four class decisions, so its leave-one-out dictionary holds a median of 3 to 22 features and no class decision recovers fully.
The number of class decisions sets the dictionary size and the dictionary size sets the power of the test, so this reading describes the protocol on tasks with few class decisions and not a negative result.
The test also needs a basis in which random subsets are insufficient.
Run on residual coordinates it returns a recovery of 0.997 against a random control of 0.994, because a few hundred of the 3584 coordinates already read an intent, and the comparison between dictionary and random control loses all resolution.

Finding.
The class decisions of a family compose from a shared family dictionary and also keep short private supports.
A held-out intent recovers a median of 0.98 of its performance from the other intents’ features on each of three models with an own overlap of at most 0.24, yet its own support is 3.8 to 5.2 times shorter than the dictionary readout.

Implication.
A shared family dictionary and a minimal per-decision readout are distinct quantities, and the count $k^{*}$ measures only the second of the two.

## A.2.6 Behavioral Interventions

Question.
A feature that predicts a label need not affect what the model does.
This section asks whether removing a selected support changes the behavior that the class decision describes, and whether equally large edits in other directions leave the behavior intact.

Editing the residual stream.

Every SAE intervention edits the output of one transformer block at every token position, for the prompt and for each generated token.
For a removed set $J$ of SAE features, a scale $\alpha>0$ , and a residual vector $h$ , the treatment is

 $h\ \mapsto\ h-\alpha\,\Delta_{J}(h),\qquad\Delta_{J}(h)=\sum_{j\in J}g_{j}(h)\,W_{\mathrm{dec},j},$ 

(36)

where $g_{j}(h)$ is the JumpReLU activation of Equation 21 recomputed from the current residual vector and $W_{\mathrm{dec},j}$ is the decoder column of feature $j$ .
At $\alpha=1$ the edit removes exactly the reconstruction that the features of $J$ contribute and leaves the reconstruction of every other feature and the reconstruction error in place.
Frequency-matched controls replace $J$ by unselected features drawn without replacement, one per removed feature, from its 50 nearest neighbors in activation frequency on the training encodings, with three independent draws.
The equal-norm control subtracts a fixed random unit direction $u$ scaled at each position to the size of the treatment,

 $h\ \mapsto\ h-\alpha\,\lVert\Delta_{J}(h)\rVert_{2}\,u,$ 

(37)

so it matches the size of the treatment edit at every token position and differs from it only in direction.
Generation is greedy through the model’s chat template with at most 48 new tokens.
Every condition uses 200 prompts per class from the task’s held-out split.

Reply language.

Estimator.
The decision variable is a one-vs-rest language identification class decision for a language $L$ , and the behavior is the script of the model’s reply.
We read

 $\mathrm{gap}_{L}=P(\text{reply in }L\mid\text{input in }L)-P(\text{reply in }L\mid\text{input not in }L),$ 

(38)

and estimate each probability by its empirical frequency over the 200 prompts of the corresponding class.
The 95% interval of the gap is the 2.5 to 97.5 percentile range of 10000 parametric bootstrap replicates (Efron, 1992), each of which redraws the two class frequencies independently from binomial distributions with the observed rates and 200 trials.
A treatment separates from a control when the two intervals do not overlap.
A reply counts as being in $L$ when it has at least 8 alphabetic characters and at least 0.3 of them fall in $L$ ’s script.

Design choices.
The gap needs no second language classifier as long as the script identifies the language, so we keep only script-unique languages.
This excludes Arabic and Urdu, which share a script, and the four Latin-script languages of the pool.
A gap near zero means that the target-language reply probabilities are similar under the two input conditions, and it does not by itself imply that the model can no longer produce the language.
The layer comes from the protocol’s own neighbor confirmation, giving layer 31 of Gemma-2-9B-IT at 74% of depth, layer 40 of Gemma-3-27B-IT at 65%, and layer 19 of Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct at 59%.
On Gemma-2-9B-IT the removed set equals the full protocol’s $k^{*}$ support at that layer for all four languages.

Design rationale.
The two kinds of control answer different doubts.
Frequency-matched features ask whether removing any equally active features disrupts the reply, and the equal-norm direction asks whether an edit of equal size in an arbitrary direction does.
The gap of Equation 38 compares inputs in $L$ with inputs in other languages, so it measures the adaptation of reply language to input language rather than the ability to produce $L$ at all.

Null prediction.
If the selected features only correlated with the input language and did not drive the reply language, removing them would move the gap of Equation 38 no more than removing frequency-matched features or subtracting an equal-norm random direction does.

yes

Same-script replies.
Script detection cannot see a switch to a language that shares the script, which inflates negatives.
Ablating the Bulgarian support on Gemma-3-27B-IT makes the model reply in Russian, so the Cyrillic readout still scores the reply as Bulgarian.
We keep the main readout unchanged for comparability across runs and record two diagnostics beside it.
The first is the rate of Cyrillic letters used in Russian but not in Bulgarian, namely Unicode U+044B, U+044D, and U+0451, in Bulgarian replies.
The second is the rate of kana-free replies for Japanese inputs.
Greek needs no correction because its script is unique to it.
Devanagari is shared with Marathi and Nepali, and the Hindi readout applies without that correction.
The correction does not affect the Gemma-2-9B-IT positives, where the model switches to fluent English in Latin script and the readout scores it correctly.
Sample replies after each ablation remain coherent, so the intervention redirects the reply language rather than breaking the model’s generation.

Dose response.

Question.
Scaling the SAE edit of Equation 36 by $\alpha$ separates removing this direction from removing enough of anything, because at each scale the treatment and its equal-norm control of Equation 37 differ only in direction and not in size.

Result.
Counting every cell of Table 27 that has a matched noise control gives 28.
Four of them sit at the 16-fold scale, where the noise control itself collapses, and we set them aside.
Of the remaining 24, the treatment falls more than 0.10 below its noise control in 15, and the two move together in 9.
The original-magnitude column of the 27B model reproduces exactly across two independent runs.

Why the edit size explains the 27B readings.
Collapse correlates with the absolute size of the edit at $\rho=+0.73$ with $p=0.001$ over 16 cells, so the amount removed is a live explanation that the dose curve has to settle.
The supports at layer 40 of Gemma-3-27B-IT carry 0.24 to 0.59% of the residual norm, against 1.96 to 6.73% at layer 31 of Gemma-2-9B-IT, so the same ablation removes between 4 and 28 times less there depending on the language.
At two or four times that magnitude all four languages collapse while 11 of the 12 matched noise controls do not move, which shows that the direction rather than the magnitude carries the effect.
SAE reconstruction cosines also differ across these layers, at 0.674 for Gemma-2-9B-IT layer 31, 0.352 for Gemma-3-27B-IT layer 40, and 0.729 for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct layer 19, and a poorly reconstructing layer weakens the link between an SAE support and the direction the model uses.
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct is read through SAEs trained on the base model, which adds a second reason for its smaller effects at the original magnitude.
Three of the removed features on Gemma-2-9B-IT also fire on inputs in other languages, so a support is not always exclusive to its own class decision.

Refusal.

Estimator.
The second behavior uses the harmfulness task with 200 held-out prompts per class.
A reply counts as a refusal when it contains one of 22 fixed phrases such as “I can’t”, “I’m sorry”, or “as an AI”.
We read the gap between the refusal rate on harmful prompts and the refusal rate on benign prompts, with the same bootstrap interval as Equation 38.
The harmful refusal rate alone would miss one of the two ways in which an edit can erase the distinction between the two classes.
OLMo-2-7B-IT takes the second way, where harmful refusal rises from 0.705 to 0.840 while benign refusal rises from 0.075 to 0.585, which only the gap registers.

Interventions.
The SAE arm removes the 64 features with the largest two-sample $t$ -statistics between harmful and benign prompts on the training encodings at layer 20 of Gemma-2-9B-IT, with the edit of Equation 36 and the same controls as the reply-language arm.
The LEACE arm (Belrose et al., 2023) applies to the residual vector $h$ the rank-one affine edit

 $h\ \mapsto\ h-\bigl((h-\mu)^{\top}b\bigr)\,a,$ 

(39)

with $\mu$ , $a$ , and $b$ fitted on the training activations of the intervened layer so that the label becomes linearly unreadable there, and applies it at every position.
Its controls are norm-matched random rank-one directions in configurations 2, 3, 5, and 6, and shuffled-label edits in configurations 4 and 7, the latter once at their natural size and once rescaled to the Frobenius change of the treatment.
Configurations differ in the layer and in whether the edit is fitted on the activation of the last token or the mean of the last three tokens, which matters because the edit applies at every position.

Control collapse is the mean reduction in gap across that arm’s random controls.
Configurations vary in edit type, layer, and activation pooling.
A configuration is separated when its treated interval overlaps no control interval.

Result.
The untreated gap is $+0.565$ on Gemma-2-9B-IT and $+0.630$ on OLMo-2-7B-IT.
Configuration 1 has frequency-matched controls at $+0.600$ , $+0.625$ , and $+0.630$ and an equal-norm control at $+0.550$ , and its treated interval of $[0.195,0.355]$ overlaps none of them.
Configuration 2 produces the smallest collapse of the seven, and its interval is too wide to separate.
Configuration 6 overlaps on its control side rather than its treatment side, since one of its three random controls collapses further than the treatment, and the same layer and intervention fitted on three tokens returns controls at $+0.615$ , $+0.620$ , and $+0.620$ .
We report one configuration per model and intervention in the main text and list all seven here, because the count of fitted configurations belongs beside the ones we report.

Two controls on the size of the edit.
On Gemma-2-9B-IT a shuffled-label edit rescaled to the same Frobenius change as the LEACE edit leaves the gap between $+0.535$ and $+0.605$ , so an equally large edit that carries no label information does not move the behavior.
On OLMo-2-7B-IT the LEACE edit is smaller than its random-direction controls, at 0.184 against 0.277 to 0.294, and collapses the gap further, so a larger edit is neither necessary nor sufficient.
Generations after the LEACE edit remain fluent and become evasive rather than degenerate.

Sufficient and nonexclusive readouts.

Definitions.
Call a coordinate set $S$ sufficient at level $a$ when a probe restricted to $S$ reaches test AUROC $a$ .
Pinning $S$ replaces every coordinate in $S$ by its training mean on both splits, and we then refit a dense probe on all $d$ coordinates.
Call $S$ exclusive when pinning it drives the refitted dense AUROC toward chance.

Remark (pinning removes only what the complement cannot restore).
Let $\bar{S}=[d]\setminus S$ .
The refitted dense probe after pinning $S$ can realize every decision function that a probe on $\bar{S}$ alone can realize, because after pinning every coordinate in $S$ is constant across inputs, so a weight on it only shifts the intercept, and setting those weights to zero recovers every linear function of $\bar{S}$ .
Its best attainable test AUROC is therefore at least that of the best probe on $\bar{S}$ .

This remark predicts that pinning a sufficient set leaves dense readability intact whenever the complement is itself sufficient, which is the question the experiment answers.

Procedure.
We rank the mean-pooled residual coordinates of the harmfulness task by $|t|$ of Equation 11 on the training split, at layer 24 of Gemma-2-9B-IT, layer 15 of OLMo-2-7B-IT, and layer 11 of Mistral-7B-Instruct.
For $n\in\{16,51,64,128,256\}$ we pin the top $n$ coordinates on both splits and refit a dense probe, and we also fit a probe on the top $n$ coordinates alone.
This experiment uses a standardized logistic probe with inverse strength 1, no class weighting, and the full training split, so its values are comparable within Table 29 and not with the main protocol.

Design rationale.
Refitting after pinning asks the strongest version of the necessity question, namely whether any linear readout survives the removal, rather than whether one particular probe breaks.

Each cell gives the test AUROC of a probe on the top $n$ coordinates alone, followed by the refitted dense AUROC after pinning those coordinates to their training means.
The last row gives the dense AUROC with nothing pinned.

Result.
The top 256 coordinates alone reach 0.963 to 0.982 across the three models, and pinning them leaves the refitted dense AUROC within 0.0011 of the untouched probe in every model.
Pinning the same coordinates during generation also changes no behavior at any $n$ up to 256 in the three families.
Both the selected coordinates and their complements are therefore sufficient, and the residual representation is highly redundant.
The count $k^{*}$ names a minimally sufficient readout and not a necessary set.
This also explains why the rank-one LEACE edit of Equation 39 changes behavior where coordinate pinning does not, since LEACE removes a direction that spans all coordinates and drives linear readability to chance, from 0.95 to 0.46 and from 0.988 to 0.494.
We do not report iterative nullspace projection as an encoding width, because erasing 64 directions that way only lowers AUROC from 0.98 to between 0.80 and 0.83 in the three families and measures the inefficiency of that method rather than a property of the representation.

Finding.
Selected features matter for behavior in the direction the probe identifies.
On Gemma-2-9B-IT, removing one to four features collapses the reply-language gap for Japanese, Greek, and Hindi from 0.88 to 0.99 to at most 0.015, while matched controls leave it near baseline.
The dose sweep shows that the direction and not the size of the edit carries the effect.
The residual coordinates, by contrast, form sufficient but nonexclusive readouts, since pinning the top 256 coordinates leaves dense readability within 0.0011 of its untouched value.

Implication.
A test of necessity in a redundant basis needs a direction-level erasure or a feature-level ablation with matched controls, since coordinate pinning cannot remove information that the complement also carries.
Reporting every fitted configuration beside the reported one, as Table 28 does, lets a reader judge how selective the reported effects are.

Scope of the interventions.

The refusal arm covers one task and reads refusal from a phrase list.
Mistral-7B-Instruct enters the pinning experiments but not the SAE and LEACE refusal arms, because its baseline refusal rate of 0.110 leaves little refusal to collapse.
Both arms intervene inside one model at a time.
They establish that a model’s own behavior depends on its selected features, and they make no claim that a matched pair of supports across two models implements one computation.

## A.2.7 Summary of Findings and Open Questions

The experiments of this appendix support five statements, each under the protocol of Appendix A.1 and on the ten base models and nine instruction-tuned models stated above.

1. 

Readout sparsity orders tasks alike across the ten base models, at a median cross-lab Spearman correlation of 0.93 on nine readable tasks that remains 0.86 to 0.92 after controlling for any one of six text-side readings, and this order survives changes of threshold, search path, and layer rule at median correlations of 0.97 to 0.99.

2. 

Absolute counts depend on the basis and on the wording of the texts by factors of 1.5 to 20, while ceilings move by at most 0.03, so only counts measured in one basis on one text pool are comparable.

3. 

Dense ceilings are largely fixed by the text, at a correlation of 0.950 with a bag-of-words instrument, and the models’ contribution shows in sparsity at small feature counts.

4. 

Selected SAE features correspond across models beyond class means and label correlation on three tasks, at pass rates of 34.3 to 56.2% against a nominal 1%.

5. 

Selected language features drive reply-language adaptation in Gemma-2-9B-IT, and selected residual coordinates are sufficient but not necessary for harmfulness readability.

Four questions remain open under the present evidence.
First, text-side readings predict part of each model’s task order but not the agreement between models, and whether a richer text-only baseline would explain more of that agreement remains open.
Second, correspondence varies strongly across class decisions, and support size explains its extremes but not its middle range.
Third, the correspondence test compares responses and not mechanisms, so whether corresponding features implement one computation across models requires interventions in both models at once.
Fourth, the correspondence and intervention results cover the tasks for which SAE supports exist, and the reconstruction quality of the SAE at the intervened layer, 0.35 to 0.73 in cosine, bounds how directly an SAE support reflects the direction the model uses.

## A.3 Details of the Training Experiments

Section 4 asks how training changes the sparsity of task readouts.
This appendix answers three questions in turn.
First, which tasks change their counts between a released base model and its instruction-tuned counterpart under the respective deployment formats.
Second, how model weights and input format contribute to those changes and whether the two contributions interact.
Third, whether supervision for a target produces a sparse readout of that target in a controlled setting where the answer to every query is known.
The real-model comparisons in Sections A.3.1 and A.3.2 reuse the estimator of Appendix A.1 without change.
The controlled synthetic experiments use their own readout protocol, which Section A.3.4 states and contrasts with the main estimator.
Table 30 maps every claim of Section 4 to the evidence and the statistic that support it.

Each row names the claim, the place in this appendix that reports it, and the statistic that carries it.
Seed ranges in the last column are minima and maxima over five seed-level task counts, not confidence intervals.

Each experiment in Sections A.3.1 to A.3.4 also states its design rationale, meaning the alternative explanation it rules out, the design element that rules it out, and the evidence that the element works.

Notation.
We write $k^{*}_{t,m,s}$ for the task-level count of Algorithm 2 on task $t$ , model pair $m$ , and setting $s$ .
The setting is a pair of weights and input format, with $s=\mathrm{base/plain}$ for the base checkpoint on raw text and $s=\mathrm{IT/chat}$ for the instruction-tuned checkpoint on its chat template.
Every count in Sections A.3.1 and A.3.2 uses the ceiling $C_{c}$ and threshold $\tau_{c}$ of Equation 4, the local counts of Equation 5, and the confirmed counts $\tilde{k}_{c,r}$ of Equation 14.
We call the synthetic readout of Section A.3.4 $k^{\mathrm{syn}}$ to keep it apart from these counts.

## A.3.1 Paired Deployment Comparisons

Question.
Does instruction-tuned deployment change the count of every task by a similar factor, or does it act selectively on some tasks and leave others unchanged?

Models.
The nine pairs comprise the six Gemma sizes and the Llama, Mistral, and OLMo base and instruction-tuned pairs listed in Table 6.
Qwen contributes a base checkpoint to the main matrix but no matched instruction-tuned checkpoint to this analysis.
Each pair shares its layer grid, so both members of a pair draw their windows from the same layers.
No model is trained for this comparison, which compares released checkpoints together with their deployment formats.

Data and gate.
Both members of a pair read the same 14 task pools of Table 3.
Within each pair, a task enters only if both ceilings exceed 0.90 and both counts are finite.
We write $\mathcal{T}_{m}$ for the tasks that pass this gate, and the gate leaves nine or ten tasks per pair, as the Tasks column of Table 31 lists, rather than one common subset across pairs.

Design rationale.
A count can fall after instruction tuning because the task becomes less readable, because every readout shrinks by a common factor, or because the deployment change acts on some tasks more than others.
The gate requires a ceiling above 0.90 on both sides, so a reported ratio compares two readouts of a reliably readable task rather than a readout with a lost signal.
Both members of a pair read identical task pools on the same layer grid, so data and depth sampling cannot differ between them.
The ratio is computed per task within one pair, so a common rescaling would appear as equal ratios across tasks, which the per-task table tests directly.

Estimator.
For a model pair $m$ and a task $t\in\mathcal{T}_{m}$ , the deployment reduction and its Spearman rank association with the base count over the gated tasks are

 $R_{t,m}=\frac{k^{*}_{t,m,\mathrm{base/plain}}}{k^{*}_{t,m,\mathrm{IT/chat}}},\qquad\rho_{m}=\operatorname{Spearman}_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{m}}\!\left(k^{*}_{t,m,\mathrm{base/plain}},\,R_{t,m}\right).$ 

(40)

A ratio above one means that the deployed instruction-tuned setting admits a smaller readout than the base setting on plain text.
The counts are medians over five probe seeds, and a seed range is the minimum and maximum of the five seed-level task counts.

Null reference.
If deployment rescaled every count by one common factor, every ratio $R_{t,m}$ would equal that factor and the task profile would keep its shape.
The statistic $\rho_{m}$ does not test this null directly, because the base count enters both of its arguments in Equation 40.
The following proposition makes this coupling precise under an independence assumption.

Proposition 3 (coupling of the selectivity statistic)

Let $X=\log k^{*}_{\mathrm{base/plain}}$ and $Y=\log k^{*}_{\mathrm{IT/chat}}$ be random over tasks with finite variances $\sigma_{X}^{2}>0$ and $\sigma_{Y}^{2}$ , and let $X$ and $Y$ be independent.
Then the Pearson correlation of $X$ with $\log R=X-Y$ equals $\sigma_{X}/\sqrt{\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}}>0$ .

Proof. Independence gives $\operatorname{Cov}(X,X-Y)=\operatorname{Var}(X)=\sigma_{X}^{2}$ and $\operatorname{Var}(X-Y)=\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}$ .
Dividing the covariance by the standard deviations $\sigma_{X}$ and $\sqrt{\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{Y}^{2}}$ gives the stated value.
 $\square$ 

Proposition 3 shows that a positive association between base counts and ratios arises even when the instruction-tuned counts carry no information about the base counts.
A positive $\rho_{m}$ is therefore a descriptive summary of the selectivity pattern and not an independent test of it.
The evidence for selectivity rests instead on the per-task counts and their seed ranges in Table 32, where the ratio is exactly 1.00 on five tasks and far above one on two.
The nine correlations $\rho_{m}$ also share their tasks across the model pairs, so they are dependent summaries.

The five tasks with base counts of at most 8 keep the same count after deployment, and their seed ranges overlap.

Seed interpretation.
Each count uses five 85% training subsamples, the shared ceiling, and neighbor confirmation from Appendix A.1.6.
The seed ranges summarize variation across seed-level task medians on a fixed test split.
Non-overlap of two ranges indicates that no seed reverses the base and instruction-tuned comparison, and it is not a calibrated significance test.

Scope.
The comparison changes weights and input format together, as deployment does.
It describes released checkpoints and does not isolate any stage of their post-training recipes.
Section A.3.2 separates the two changes on one model, Gemma-2-9B, in a factorial design.

Finding.
Instruction-tuned deployment reshapes the sparsity profile across tasks rather than rescaling it.
On Gemma-2-9B, the five tasks with base counts of at most 8 keep ratios of exactly 1.00, while harmfulness and fact checking shrink by factors of 7.26 and 6.33 with both ceilings above 0.90.
Within one task and one model family under one protocol, the harmfulness reduction rises from 1.1 at 270M parameters to 8.5 at 12B and stays at 7.5 at 27B.

Implication.
A single summary of how instruction tuning changes representations can hide opposite behavior across tasks, so base and instruction-tuned comparisons should be reported per task.
The size of the harmfulness reduction also depends on the model family and scale, so a result on one pair does not transfer to another pair without measurement.

## A.3.2 Weights and Format as a Factorial Design

Question.
Does the deployment reduction of Section A.3.1 on Gemma-2-9B come from the instruction-tuned weights, from the chat format, or from their combination?

Conditions.
Let $w\in\{0,1\}$ indicate base and instruction-tuned weights of Gemma-2-9B, and let $f\in\{0,1\}$ indicate plain text and chat format.
The four conditions cross the two factors.
Both chat conditions render the input through the chat template of the Gemma-2-9B-IT tokenizer, followed by its generation prompt, so the base checkpoint receives the template of its instruction-tuned counterpart.
Both plain conditions receive the raw text with the tokenizer’s default special tokens, as in Appendix A.1.2.
All four conditions share the task pools, mean pooling over attended positions, the 768-token limit, the five seeds, and the probe settings of Appendix A.1.

Tasks.
The design measures harmfulness detection and fact checking, the two tasks with the largest deployment reductions in Table 32.
Intent classification on CLINC150 serves as the floor control of the design, because its counts of 2 or 4 lie near the bottom of the size grid.

Design rationale.
Deployment changes two things at once, the weights and the input format, so a base/IT difference alone cannot say which of them moves the count.
Crossing the two factors turns one comparison into four cells, and the interaction ratio of Equation 43 then separates additive from joint action on the log-count scale.
Both chat cells use one template string, so the format factor means the same thing under either weight setting.
Three further elements close the remaining alternatives.
The shared-layer recomputation rules out a shift of the located window, the per-cell ceilings rule out a change of readability, and the floor control shows where the grid resolution makes ratios unreliable.

Estimator.
Write the four task-level counts as $k_{wf}$ .
The conditional reduction factors of the weights and of the format, each at a fixed level of the other, and the deployment reduction, are

 $R_{W}(f)=\frac{k_{0f}}{k_{1f}},\qquad R_{F}(w)=\frac{k_{w0}}{k_{w1}},\qquad R_{\mathrm{deploy}}=\frac{k_{00}}{k_{11}}.$ 

(41)

The deployment ratio factors along either path through the design,

 $R_{\mathrm{deploy}}=R_{W}(0)\,R_{F}(1)=R_{F}(0)\,R_{W}(1).$ 

(42)

This identity holds whether or not the factors interact, so it carries no evidence about independence.
To quantify interaction on the multiplicative scale, we define

 $I=\frac{R_{W}(1)}{R_{W}(0)}=\frac{R_{F}(1)}{R_{F}(0)}=\frac{k_{01}k_{10}}{k_{00}k_{11}},\qquad\delta=\log I.$ 

(43)

The value $I=1$ means that each factor reduces the count by the same factor under either level of the other, so the solo reductions multiply to the deployment reduction.

Proposition 4 (saturated log-linear decomposition)

Let the four task-level counts $k_{wf}$ be finite and positive.
Then there are unique real coefficients $\mu,\alpha,\beta,\delta$ with

 $\log k_{wf}=\mu-\alpha\,w-\beta\,f-\delta\,wf\qquad\text{for all }w,f\in\{0,1\},$ 

(44)

namely $\mu=\log k_{00}$ , $\alpha=\log R_{W}(0)$ , $\beta=\log R_{F}(0)$ , and $\delta=\log I$ .
Moreover, the deployment reduction factors into the two solo reductions and the interaction,

 $R_{\mathrm{deploy}}=R_{W}(0)\,R_{F}(0)\,I.$ 

(45)

Proof. Equation 44 imposes four linear equations on four unknowns.
Setting $w=f=0$ gives $\mu=\log k_{00}$ .
Setting $(w,f)=(1,0)$ gives $\alpha=\log k_{00}-\log k_{10}=\log R_{W}(0)$ , and $(w,f)=(0,1)$ gives $\beta=\log R_{F}(0)$ in the same way.
Setting $w=f=1$ gives $\log k_{11}=\mu-\alpha-\beta-\delta$ , which rearranges to $\delta=\log k_{01}+\log k_{10}-\log k_{00}-\log k_{11}=\log I$ .
The four equations have a unique solution because their coefficient matrix is lower triangular with a nonzero diagonal.
For Equation 45, the product $R_{W}(0)R_{F}(0)I$ equals $(k_{00}/k_{10})(k_{00}/k_{01})(k_{01}k_{10})/(k_{00}k_{11})=k_{00}/k_{11}$ .
 $\square$ 

The sign convention makes $\alpha$ and $\beta$ positive when the solo changes reduce the count.
With this convention, a positive $\delta$ means that the joint change reduces the count by more than the product of the solo reductions, which we call complementarity.
The decomposition is saturated, so it describes the four medians exactly and has no residual from which to estimate uncertainty.

Null prediction.
If the two factors acted separably on the log-count scale, we would expect $I=1$ , and the deployment reduction would equal the product of the two solo reductions.
If the reduction came from the weights alone, we would expect $R_{F}(0)=R_{F}(1)=1$ .

Table 34: Seed ranges and ceilings for the four-cell design. Brackets give the minimum and maximum over five seed-level task counts, not confidence intervals. Restricting to the shared layers reproduces every median, with layers $17,20,24,28$ for harmfulness and $20,24,28$ for fact checking.

Result.
The observed counts give $I\approx 1.08$ for harmfulness and $I\approx 4.87$ for fact checking.
Harmfulness therefore admits an approximately separable description, in which the format alone takes the count from 341 to 135 and the weights alone take it from 341 to 128.
Fact checking instead shows strong complementarity, since each solo change leaves the count between 217 and 227, while the joint change takes it to 40.
Neither task supports the reading that the weights alone carry the reduction, because the format alone lowers both counts.
The ceilings stay between 0.979 and 0.986 on harmfulness and between 0.909 and 0.927 on fact checking, so the count changes do not come from changes in linear readability.
These ratios are arithmetic contrasts of the eight measured medians in Table 34 and not fitted causal effects with estimated uncertainty.

Layer control.
The four conditions draw their windows from the same layer grid, but the located windows can differ across conditions.
Restricting all four conditions to their shared layers, which are 17, 20, 24, and 28 for harmfulness and 20, 24, and 28 for fact checking, reproduces every median in Table 34.
The factorial contrasts therefore do not come from a shift of the window.

Floor control.
The intent control has counts of 4, 2, 2, and 2 on the available grids and 4, 4, 4, and 2 on the shared layers 13, 17, and 20.
Counts at the bottom of the size grid change by whole octaves, so a single grid step changes the ratio by a factor of two.
This resolution leaves too little room for a stable multiplicative decomposition, and the control does not carry the factorial conclusion.

Scope.
The design uses one model family and one chat template, so it establishes that weights and format jointly shape readout sparsity on Gemma-2-9B and does not estimate a universal template effect.
The mean over attended positions includes template tokens in the chat conditions, so the format factor combines a change of context with a change of pooled positions.

Finding.
Weights and input format both reduce readout counts on Gemma-2-9B, and the way they combine depends on the task.
Harmfulness is close to separable with $I\approx 1.08$ , while fact checking needs both changes together with $I\approx 4.87$ , so either change alone leaves its count between 217 and 227 near the base count of 253 and the joint change takes it to 40.

Implication.
A base and instruction-tuned comparison run on different input formats measures the joint effect of weights and format.
Attributing a readout change to training therefore requires holding the chat template fixed.
On fact checking the weight change barely moves the count on plain inputs and reduces it strongly on chat inputs, so the chosen template decides the conclusion.

## A.3.3 Synthetic World and Training Objective

Question.
Does supervision for a target produce a sparse readout of that target, beyond the presence of the target’s input information in the representation?
Answering this question requires tasks whose answers are known exactly and whose supervision we control.
We therefore build a synthetic world of 32 binary factors in which every query has a known true answer, and we vary only whether the training objective rewards predicting that answer.

Generative model.
Each example draws a latent factor vector $z\in\{0,1\}^{32}$ with independent fair coordinates, $z_{j}\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)$ .
A uniformly random permutation $\sigma$ of $\{1,\dots,32\}$ orders the observations.
The observation for factor $j$ is a single token $o(j,z_{j})$ that names the factor and its value, so the vocabulary holds 64 observation tokens.
A training sequence is

 $x=\bigl(\langle\mathrm{bos}\rangle,\ o(\sigma_{1},z_{\sigma_{1}}),\dots,o(\sigma_{32},z_{\sigma_{32}}),\ \langle\mathrm{sep}\rangle,\ q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{12},a_{12}\bigr),$ 

(46)

where $q_{1},\dots,q_{12}$ are distinct query tokens drawn uniformly without replacement from a pool of 50 and each $a_{i}$ is one of two answer tokens.
The sequence has $1+32+1+24=58$ tokens, and the vocabulary has $4+64+50=118$ tokens, counting the beginning, separator, and two answer tokens.
A measurement sequence stops at a single query token, which gives a prefix of 35 tokens.

Query pool.
The pool contains 32 factor queries with answers $z_{j}$ and 18 aggregate queries, namely one target and one control instance for each of nine function families.
For a factor subset $S$ , the three function families are threshold, conjunction, and parity,

 $g_{\mathrm{thr},S}(z)=\mathbf{1}\!\left\{\sum_{j\in S}z_{j}\geq|S|/2\right\},\quad g_{\mathrm{and},S}(z)=\prod_{j\in S}z_{j},\quad g_{\mathrm{xor},S}(z)=\Bigl(\sum_{j\in S}z_{j}\Bigr)\bmod 2.$ 

(47)

Threshold subsets have sizes 2, 4, 8, and 16, conjunction subsets have sizes 2 and 4, and parity subsets have sizes 2, 4, and 8.
The threshold uses at least half of the factors and so includes ties.
Table 35 lists every subset.
The two eight-factor threshold instances share factors 16 to 19 by design, which forms the overlap control of Section A.3.4.
Other control subsets also intersect target subsets of different families, for example the sixteen-factor threshold control contains both conjunction targets.

Table 35: Factor subsets of the 18 aggregate queries.
Factors are numbered from 0 to 31.
Target and control instances of a family use subsets of equal size.
The eight-factor threshold pair shares factors 16 to 19, which forms the overlap control of Section A.3.4.

Lemma 4 (matched true-label distributions)

For every family, the true label of the target instance and the true label of the control instance have the same distribution, namely

 $\Pr[g_{\mathrm{thr},S}=1]=2^{-|S|}\sum_{i\geq|S|/2}\binom{|S|}{i},\qquad\Pr[g_{\mathrm{and},S}=1]=2^{-|S|},\qquad\Pr[g_{\mathrm{xor},S}=1]=\tfrac{1}{2}.$ 

(48)

Proof. The sum $\sum_{j\in S}z_{j}$ of independent fair bits follows a binomial distribution with $|S|$ trials and success probability one half, whatever the identity of the factors in $S$ .
Each family’s label is a function of this sum alone, so its distribution depends on $S$ only through $|S|$ .
Target and control subsets of a family have equal sizes by Table 35.
The three expressions of Equation 48 follow from the binomial probabilities of the sum, and the parity case uses the fact that a binomial count with success probability one half is even with probability one half for every $|S|\geq 1$ .
 $\square$ 

Lemma 4 rules out one alternative explanation of any target and control difference.
The probes of the target and control instances of one family face the same class balance and the same chance level, so a difference in their counts cannot come from the label distribution.

Design rationale.
The design pairs every supervised target with a control that differs from it in one respect only, whether training rewards predicting the aggregate.
Four elements make this contrast clean.
First, control answers are fresh fair bits, so the objective gives no reason to compute the control aggregate, while the control’s input factors stay in the sequence and every factor is itself a supervised query that the model must retain up to the query position.
Second, target and control subsets have equal sizes within each family, so Lemma 4 equalizes their class balance and chance level.
Third, the eight-factor threshold control shares half of its factors with a trained target, which tests whether training a target narrows readouts of anything computed from its inputs.
Fourth, each observation appears at a uniformly random position, so no factor can be read from a fixed position, and every probe uses fresh sequences, so a probe cannot recognize training examples.
The contrast sits within one model, one layer grid, and one probe protocol, which removes differences between models from every target and control comparison.

Implication for experimental design.
Pairing a supervised target with an independent-answer control over the same kind of inputs converts the question of whether training shapes a readout into a within-model contrast with a known null.
The same construction applies to any setting in which the true answer can be computed but its supervision can be withheld.

Target and control supervision.
Factor queries and target aggregates receive their correct answers during training.
Control aggregates instead receive a fresh fair bit $\xi$ for every occurrence, drawn independently of the factors and of every other answer.
The control’s input factors remain in the sequence, and every factor is itself a supervised factor query.
Probes always use the true aggregate value $g_{q}(z)$ as their label, including for controls.
The design thus holds the input information fixed and varies only whether the objective rewards computing the aggregate.

Objective.
Let $\ell_{\theta}(x_{<i})\in\mathbb{R}^{118}$ be the output logits of the model with parameters $\theta$ before position $i$ and let $\mathcal{A}(x)$ be the 12 answer positions of Equation 46.
Training minimizes the expected cross-entropy over the full vocabulary of 118 tokens at the 12 answer positions only,

 $\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\theta)$ 
 $\displaystyle=-\,\mathbb{E}_{x}\,\frac{1}{12}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}(x)}\log\operatorname{softmax}\bigl(\ell_{\theta}(x_{<i})\bigr)_{x_{i}},$ 

(49)

 $\displaystyle x_{i}$ 
 $\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle a_{g_{q}(z)}\rangle,&q\text{ a factor or target query},\\
\langle a_{\xi}\rangle,&q\text{ a control query},\end{cases}$ 

where $q$ is the query that precedes position $i$ and $\langle a_{0}\rangle,\langle a_{1}\rangle$ are the two answer tokens.
Each update estimates the expectation with 256 freshly generated sequences, so no sequence is seen twice.

Proposition 5 (what the objective rewards)

First, the prefix before any answer position determines $z$ , so the answer to every factor and target query is a deterministic function of the prefix.
Second, for every control query the conditional distribution of the answer given the prefix is uniform on the two answer tokens, so the expected loss at that position is at least $\log 2$ , with equality exactly when the model assigns probability one half to each answer token.
Third, the infimum of $\mathcal{L}$ over all predictors equals $\tfrac{9}{50}\log 2$ , the expected fraction of control positions times $\log 2$ .

Proof. Each factor appears in exactly one observation token, and that token names its value, so the 32 observations determine $z$ .
Every factor and target answer is $g_{q}(z)$ and is therefore a function of the prefix.
A control answer $\xi$ is independent of the prefix, because it is drawn independently of $z$ , of the permutation, of the queries, and of the other answers.
The expected loss at a control position is the cross-entropy between the uniform distribution on the two answer tokens and the model’s distribution, which is at least the entropy $\log 2$ and equals it exactly when the model puts probability one half on each answer token, since any mass on other tokens only increases the loss.
At factor and target positions, a predictor that places probability tending to one on the correct token drives the loss toward zero.
Each of the 12 query slots holds one of the 50 queries uniformly at random, and 9 of the 50 are controls, so a fraction $9/50$ of answer positions carries the irreducible loss $\log 2$ in expectation, and the two facts together give the infimum $\tfrac{9}{50}\log 2$ .
 $\square$ 

Proposition 5 states exactly what the two conditions of the design differ in.
The objective rewards computing $g_{q}(z)$ at the query position for targets, while for controls every function of the input is equally good.
Proposition 5 does not imply a one-coordinate readout, since the transformer output passes through a final layer normalization and a vocabulary projection, and the GRU output through a vocabulary projection.
The single-feature result for targets in Section A.3.4 is therefore an empirical finding and not a consequence of the objective.

Algorithm 4 Generation and training of one controlled synthetic model

 1:

 architecture and width from Table 36, training seed $s$ , number of updates $T$ 

 2:

 Initialize parameters with seed $s$ and the data generator with seed $1000+s$ 

 3:

 for $t=1,\dots,T$ do

 4:

   for each of 256 sequences do

 5:

    Draw $z\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)^{32}$ and a uniform permutation $\sigma$ 

 6:

    Write $\langle\mathrm{bos}\rangle$ , the observations $o(\sigma_{j},z_{\sigma_{j}})$ for $j=1,\dots,32$ , and $\langle\mathrm{sep}\rangle$ 

 7:

    Draw 12 distinct queries uniformly from the pool of 50

 8:

    for each query $q$ do

 9:

      Append $q$ and then $\langle a_{g_{q}(z)}\rangle$ if $q$ is a factor or target, or $\langle a_{\xi}\rangle$ with a fresh $\xi\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)$ if $q$ is a control

 10:

    end for

 11:

   end for

 12:

   Compute Equation 49 on the batch and take one AdamW step with the scheduled learning rate

 13:

 end for

Algorithm 5 Readout measurement for one query of one trained model

 1:

 trained model, query $q$ , measurement seed $777+q$ 

 2:

 Draw 6,000 fresh examples, each ending at the query token $q$ with no answer token

 3:

 Label each example with the true value $g_{q}(z)$ , also when $q$ is a control

 4:

 Record the residual stream, or the GRU hidden state, at the query position after the embedding and after every block or recurrent layer

 5:

 Use the first 4,000 examples for training and the last 2,000 for testing

 6:

 Fit the probe suite of Appendix A.1.5 with probe seed 0 on the raw coordinates

 7:

 Repeat the fit on a PCA basis fitted to the training examples only

 8:

 Read $k^{\mathrm{syn}}_{q}$ from Equation 50

Table 36: Architecture and optimization of the controlled synthetic models. The main 20-model grid uses these settings. Section A.3.4 covers the auxiliary width, duration, and trajectory runs.

Component

Setting

Transformer grid

Depth $\{2,4\}$ , width $\{64,128,256\}$ , seeds $\{0,1,2\}$ , giving 18 models

Transformer block

Pre-layer normalization, 4 attention heads, causal mask, GELU MLP of width $4d$ , learned positions, no dropout

Transformer output

Final layer normalization and an untied vocabulary projection without bias

Initialization

Transformer linear and embedding weights normal with standard deviation 0.02 and zero biases, with attention input projections, layer normalization, and the GRU at their library defaults

Recurrent grid

Two-layer GRU, width 256, seeds $\{0,1\}$ , giving 2 models

Optimizer

AdamW (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2017), learning rate $3\times 10^{-4}$ , weight decay 0.01, batch size 256

Schedule

500-step linear warmup multiplied by cosine decay, 30,000 updates

Sequence

58 training tokens, 35-token measurement prefix ending at the query

Probe data

6,000 fresh examples per query, the first 4,000 for training and the last 2,000 for testing

PCA

Fit on probe training features only, retaining $\min(256,d)$ components

Probe grid

 $1,2,4,\ldots,256$ , capped at the feature width, with the shared L1 path

## A.3.4 Synthetic Readout, Coverage, and Controls

Question.
In models trained on the objective of Equation 49, how many coordinates does a readout of each aggregate need, and does the answer differ between targets and controls?

Representation.
The readout uses the residual stream at the query position, where the model must produce the answer.
Layer zero denotes the embedding output and layer $\ell\geq 1$ the output of block or recurrent layer $\ell$ , which differs from the block indexing of Appendix A.1.2.
The transformer residual is read before the final layer normalization, and the GRU readout uses the hidden state of each recurrent layer.
The raw basis uses the model’s own coordinates.
The PCA basis centers the training features and rotates them onto their principal axes, and since every main-grid width is at most 256, it keeps all $d$ components and is a full-rank orthogonal change of basis.

Estimator.
Let $C_{q\ell}$ be the dense AUROC for query $q$ at layer $\ell$ , and let $\widetilde{A}_{q\ell}(k)$ be the median AUROC over the ranked-prefix and L1 candidates of size $k$ at that layer.
The synthetic readout is

 $k^{\mathrm{syn}}_{q}=\min_{\ell:\,C_{q\ell}>0.90}\ \min\left\{k:\widetilde{A}_{q\ell}(k)\geq 0.5+0.95\,(C_{q\ell}-0.5)\right\}.$ 

(50)

A query whose dense AUROC never exceeds 0.90 at any layer is recorded as unreadable, which is distinct from a large finite count.
Table 37 states every difference from the main estimator.

Table 37: Synthetic readout versus the main estimator.
The two estimators share standardization, feature ranking, the probe objective, the regularization grid, and the L1 path of Appendix A.1.5.

Component

Main estimator

Synthetic readout

Position

Mean over attended positions

Query position

Seeds

Five probe seeds on 85% subsamples

One probe seed on all 4,000 training examples

Ceiling

Common maximum over window layers and seeds

Layer-specific dense AUROC

Candidates of one size

Any qualifying candidate

Median AUROC over candidates of that size

Layers

Neighbor confirmation over a four-layer window

Minimum over layers that pass the gate

Gate

Ceiling below 0.90 flagged

Layers at or below 0.90 excluded

The median over candidates of one size makes a size qualify only when its typical candidate reaches the threshold.
The minimum over layers without neighbor confirmation is the less conservative choice, and the target counts of one sit at the floor of the grid, where neighbor confirmation cannot lower a count.
Variation across the 20 models comes from training seeds, widths, depths, and architectures rather than from probe seeds, since every model uses one probe seed.

Design rationale.
The readout position, the gate, and the candidate rule each address one alternative reading.
The query position is where the model must produce the answer, and the answer token is excluded from the prefix, so the probe reads what the model has computed before the answer appears and not the answer itself.
The gate separates a readout that is absent from a readout that is wide, so an aggregate the model never computes cannot masquerade as a large count.
The median over candidates of one size prevents a single fortunate candidate from setting a small count.
The PCA basis is a data-dependent rotation of the full space, so agreement between the two bases shows that the target and control separation does not come from alignment with the model’s own coordinates.

Null predictions.
If supervision played no role in readout sparsity, target and control instances of one family would show the same distribution of counts, since Lemma 4 matches their labels and their inputs carry the same information.
If a control’s count simply reflected the number of factors it depends on, the count would grow in proportion to $|S|$ across families.
If training a target narrowed the readout of any aggregate computed over its inputs, the overlapping eight-factor threshold control would read narrower than the other readable controls.

Figure 10: Synthetic readout $k^{\mathrm{syn}}$ for every target and control in the 20 main-grid models.
Each marker is one model.
Within each query family, gold diamonds show the supervised target, blue circles show the control that shares the factor count but answers a coin, and black bars mark the median over models.
(a) Raw basis.
Every target reads at $k^{\mathrm{syn}}=1$ in all 20 models, and the control medians are 37, 53.5, 60.5, 52.5, 30, and 29 for thr2, thr4, thr8, thr16, and2, and and4.
(b) PCA basis.
Every target again reads at 1 in all 20 models, and the control medians are 31.5, 48, 64, 32, 30, and 16.
The xor2 control passes the readability gate in none of the 20 models in either basis and appears as open markers in the band at the top.
No control reaches a count of one in any model.

Result.
Every readable target in the seven learned families has $k^{\mathrm{syn}}=1$ in all 20 main models and in both bases.
Figure 10 shows every one of these counts.
Readable controls span family medians of 29 to 60.5 in the raw basis and 16 to 64 in the PCA basis, and no control reaches a count of one in any model.
The target and control distributions therefore do not overlap, which rejects the first null prediction.
Four- and eight-factor parity targets fail the readability gate, as do the parity controls, and we keep them apart from finite wide readouts.

Idealized factor coordinates.
The second null prediction has an exact form in an idealized representation whose 32 coordinates are the binary factors themselves.

Proposition 6 (exact recovery needs every factor)

Let the representation be $z\in\{0,1\}^{32}$ with independent fair coordinates, let $|S|\geq 2$ , and let $g$ be $g_{\mathrm{thr},S}$ or $g_{\mathrm{and},S}$ .
A linear score on a coordinate set $T$ reaches population AUROC one for $g$ only if $S\subseteq T$ , and $\sum_{j\in S}z_{j}$ reaches it.

Proof. Let $T$ miss some $j\in S$ .
For the threshold, fix the other coordinates of $S$ so that exactly $\lceil|S|/2\rceil-1$ of them equal one, which is possible because $0\leq\lceil|S|/2\rceil-1\leq|S|-1$ .
The label then equals $z_{j}$ .
For the conjunction, fix the other coordinates of $S$ at one, and the label again equals $z_{j}$ .
Fix every coordinate outside $S$ arbitrarily.
The two inputs that differ only in $z_{j}$ have positive probability, opposite labels, and equal scores, since the score ignores coordinate $j$ .
A tie between a positive and a negative contributes one half rather than one to the AUROC, so the AUROC falls strictly below one.
For the converse, both labels are nondecreasing threshold functions of $\sum_{j\in S}z_{j}$ , so every positive has a strictly larger sum than every negative.
 $\square$ 

Proposition 6 concerns exact recovery, while $k^{\mathrm{syn}}$ asks for 95% of the above-chance ceiling, so it motivates the second null prediction without implying it.
The measurements do not follow this idealized picture.
Across the 120 readable controls of the threshold and conjunction families, the Spearman correlation (Spearman, 1987) between $|S|$ and the count is 0.289 in the raw basis and 0.347 in the PCA basis, with log-log slopes of 0.31 and 0.35 rather than one.
The two-sided $p$ -values are 0.0014 and 0.0001, but the 120 observations share models and families, so these values describe an association and do not test independent units.
Control counts thus depend on the number of input factors only weakly, and the trained models do not store controls as sums over separate factor coordinates.

Overlapping inputs.
The target threshold over factors 12 to 19 and the control threshold over factors 16 to 23 share four of eight factors.
The control’s median count is 60.5 in the raw basis and 64 in the PCA basis, with a raw range of 14 to 102 over the 20 models.
The other readable controls have a median of 38 in the raw basis and 32 in the PCA basis.
A two-sided Mann–Whitney test (Mann & Whitney, 1947) comparing the overlapping control with the other controls gives $p=0.099$ in the raw basis and $p=0.005$ in the PCA basis, with the overlapping control reading wider.
Training the target therefore does not narrow the readout of an untrained aggregate over half of its inputs, which rejects the third null prediction.
The wider reading in the PCA basis, with $p=0.005$ , rests on one pair of instances, and we do not interpret its direction further.

Width-limited and longer runs.
Six additional two-layer transformers of widths 16 and 32, three two-layer width-128 transformers with 1,500 updates, and two four-layer transformers of widths 128 and 256 trained for 150,000 updates serve as capacity and duration diagnostics.
They are excluded from the 20-model main grid.
At width 16, six or seven of the nine targets read at $k^{\mathrm{syn}}=1$ and the remaining two or three are unreadable, and at width 32 the split is seven and two.
Limited width therefore removes some readouts entirely, and every target that stays readable keeps a count of one.
In the 150,000-step runs, answer accuracy on a fixed evaluation set of 512 held-out sequences, logged every 2,000 updates, stays between 0.459 and 0.535 for four-factor parity and between 0.471 and 0.529 for eight-factor parity across all 75 logged evaluations, against a chance level of one half, while two-factor parity and the eight-factor threshold end at 1.000 and never fall below 0.959 after the first 2,000 updates.
The dense ceilings of the parity targets stay between 0.500 and 0.521 over the two runs, and those of the parity controls reach at most 0.527.
After these runs, the overlapping eight-factor threshold control reads 26 at width 128 and 51 at width 256, in the range of the main grid, so five times more training does not narrow an unsupervised readout.
These results concern the tested architectures and optimization and do not establish that the parity targets are unlearnable.

Training trajectory.
Table 39 reports the query-position counts at all six saved checkpoints of the four-layer width-256 transformer with training seed 0.
The readable threshold target already has a one-feature readout at the earliest checkpoint after 500 updates.
The two-factor parity target is unreadable at 500 and 1,000 updates and reads at a count of one from 2,000 updates on.
Neither tracked target shows a wide readout at one checkpoint that narrows at a later one.
This sampling cannot exclude a wider transient readout between checkpoints.
