Readout sparsity and information.
A single-feature readout of a target might suggest that training discards information.
The minimum expected cross-entropy of any decoder of a target $Y$ from a representation $V$ is $H(Y\mid V)=H(Y)-I(Y;V)$ , which depends on $V$ only through the information it retains.
Mutual information is invariant to invertible maps of $V$ , while readout sparsity depends on the basis.
The next proposition makes the second half exact.

Proposition 7 (information is basis-free and readout sparsity is not)

Let $Y\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)$ , let $U=(2Y-1,\,N)$ with $N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})$ independent of $Y$ , and let $V=QU$ with the orthogonal map $Q$ whose rows are $(1,1)/\sqrt{2}$ and $(1,-1)/\sqrt{2}$ .
Define the population count of a representation as the smallest number of its coordinates on which some linear score reaches the threshold $0.5+0.95\,(C-0.5)$ , where $C$ is the best population AUROC of a linear score on all coordinates.
Then $I(Y;V)=I(Y;U)$ , the population count of $U$ is one, and the population count of $V$ is two whenever $\sigma>\sqrt{2}/\Phi^{-1}(0.975)$ , where $\Phi$ is the standard normal distribution function.

Proof. The map $Q$ is invertible, so $V$ and $U$ generate the same information about $Y$ and $I(Y;V)=I(Y;U)$ .
The first coordinate of $U$ equals $2Y-1$ and separates the classes perfectly, so $C=1$ for both representations, since $V$ determines $U$ linearly.
The threshold is then $0.975$ , and the population count of $U$ is one.
Each coordinate of $V$ equals $(2Y-1\pm N)/\sqrt{2}$ .
A linear score on one coordinate either preserves or reverses the order of that coordinate, and the reversal yields an AUROC of at most one half, so the best single-coordinate AUROC is the probability that a positive exceeds an independent negative.
The difference of a positive and a negative value equals $(2+N_{1}\mp N_{2})/\sqrt{2}$ with independent copies $N_{1},N_{2}$ , which is normal with mean $\sqrt{2}$ and variance $\sigma^{2}$ , since $N_{2}$ and $-N_{2}$ have the same distribution.
The AUROC of either coordinate is therefore $\Phi(\sqrt{2}/\sigma)$ , which falls below $0.975$ exactly when $\sigma>\sqrt{2}/\Phi^{-1}(0.975)$ .
The sum of the two coordinates of $V$ equals $\sqrt{2}\,(2Y-1)$ and reaches AUROC one, so the population count of $V$ is two.
 $\square$ 

Proposition 7 shows that a basis change can move a readout count while leaving the retained information untouched.
The agreement of the raw and PCA bases in Table 38 is therefore an empirical finding about these two bases rather than a consequence of the definition, and it does not establish invariance to arbitrary changes of basis.
The single-feature readouts characterize how the target is accessible under the tested bases and probe protocol.
They do not establish compression of information or a gradual narrowing of the readout during training.

Scope.
The synthetic experiments establish the target and control separation for threshold, conjunction, and two-factor parity functions of independent binary factors, in transformers and GRUs of width 64 to 256 trained on the objective of Equation 49.
The absolute control counts depend on the basis, which Table 38 measures for the raw and PCA bases.
The experiments do not model the continuous and correlated features of natural language, and they do not explain the intermediate counts between one and several hundred that Section 3 reports for pretrained models.

Finding.
In 20 of 20 trained models and in both tested bases, every readable supervised aggregate admits a one-feature readout, while readable controls over inputs of the same size need family medians of 29 to 60.5 features in the raw basis.
Sharing half of its factors with a trained target does not narrow a control, which reads 60.5 and 64 in the raw and PCA bases.
Threshold targets over 2, 4, 8, and 16 factors all read at one feature in both bases, so the number of input dependencies does not set the readout size of a learned target in these models.

Implication.
In this controlled setting, a sparse readout marks the variables that the objective rewarded computing, and linear readability alone marks only that the information is present.
A dense probe with a high ceiling therefore cannot distinguish a computed target from a readable consequence of its inputs, while the pair of ceiling and count can.
The basis construction of Proposition 7 adds a caution for interpretation, since a count describes access in a chosen basis and a small count does not by itself indicate that information has been discarded.

## A.3.5 Summary of Findings and Open Questions

The training experiments support four findings, each under the conditions stated above.

1. 

Instruction-tuned deployment changes readout counts selectively across tasks, leaving counts of at most 8 unchanged on Gemma-2-9B and reducing the two widest readable readouts, fact checking and harmfulness, by factors of 6.33 and 7.26 (Table 32).

2. 

The harmfulness reduction depends on the model, rising from 1.1 to 8.5 across Gemma sizes and ranging from 1.20 to 6.67 across the other three labs (Tables 31 and 33).

3. 

Weights and input format both contribute, with an interaction ratio of about 1.08 on harmfulness and about 4.87 on fact checking (Equation 43).

4. 

In controlled synthetic models, supervision concentrates each learned target into a one-feature readout, while matched readable controls need tens of features, and sharing half of its inputs with a trained target does not narrow a control (Table 38).

The following questions remain open and are stated as testable conditions.

1. 

Whether the saturated decomposition yields similar interaction ratios on model families other than Gemma-2-9B and on chat templates other than its own.

2. 

Which stage of post-training produces the selective reduction, which requires checkpoints between the base and the released instruction-tuned model.

3. 

Why sentiment keeps a ratio of 1.12 on Gemma-2-9B despite a base count of 72, unlike the other wide readouts on that model, harmfulness and fact checking.

4. 

Whether four- and eight-factor parity becomes learnable under other architectures or optimizers, and whether it then also reads at one feature at the query position.

5. 

Which mechanism produces the intermediate counts of pretrained models, which the synthetic setting, with counts of one or of tens, does not reproduce.

## A.4 Details of the Learning and Maintenance Experiments

This appendix specifies the experiments of Section 5 at the level needed to recompute every number in that section.
The section makes three predictive claims and two intervention claims, and each claim rests on a distinct estimator with its own unit of analysis.
Table 40 maps every claim to the subsection that defines its estimator, the display that reports it, and the statistic that carries it.
The task pools and the probe family come from Appendix A.1, and every reference count $k^{*}_{t}$ and ceiling $C_{t}$ below is the task-level output of Algorithm 2 on Gemma-2-9B unless a subsection states otherwise.
We keep three procedures apart throughout.
Initial learning adapts a pretrained student, recovery adapts a backbone that sequential training has already changed, and frozen probing fits new linear probes without changing any backbone weight.

Shared notation.
We index students by $m$ , tasks by $t$ , class decisions by $c$ , and realized training-set sizes by $n$ .
For a student $m$ adapted on task $t$ with $n$ labels, $A^{\mathrm{stu}}_{tmc}(n,r)$ denotes the test AUROC of the adapted model for class decision $c$ and training seed $r$ , evaluated on the fixed test split of Table 3.
The reference quantities $k^{*}_{t}$ and $C_{t}$ carry no student index, since they come from the frozen Gemma-2-9B measurement.
All logarithms of label counts and feature counts are base 10.

Negative losses denote improvements, and we retain them.

## A.4.2 Student Adaptation and Label Sources

Question.
The label-budget analysis needs, for every student and task, a learning curve that relates the number of labels to held-out AUROC.
This subsection specifies how we produce those curves.

Models.
The eight students are Gemma-3-270M, Gemma-3-1B, Gemma-3-4B, Llama-3.2-1B, Llama-3.2-3B, Qwen3-0.6B-Base, Qwen3-1.7B-Base, and OLMo-2-0425-1B.
Each run starts from the pretrained checkpoint with a newly initialized binary classification head and newly initialized adapters.
The head reads the final non-padding position of the last layer, following the sequence-classification convention of each architecture.
LoRA (Hu et al., 2021) adapters with the settings of Table 41 attach to every attention and MLP projection that exists in the architecture, namely the query, key, value, and output projections and the gate, up, and down projections.

Data.
Each class decision defines a binary problem on the task pool of Table 3.
Up to three class decisions per multiclass task enter the adaptation sweep, so these learning curves need not cover every decision that enters the reference $k^{*}_{t}$ .
For a requested budget $n$ and a training split with $n_{\mathrm{tr}}$ texts of which a fraction $\pi$ is positive, we write $\bar{n}=\min\{n,n_{\mathrm{tr}}\}$ and draw without replacement

 $n_{+}=\max\bigl\{1,\ \min\{\operatorname{round}(\bar{n}\pi),\ n_{\mathrm{tr}}\pi,\ \bar{n}-1\}\bigr\},\qquad n_{-}=\min\{\bar{n}-n_{+},\ n_{\mathrm{tr}}(1-\pi)\},$ 

(51)

positive and negative texts, so every subset keeps the pool’s class ratio and contains both classes.
A requested budget above the pool size maps to the full pool, and we collapse requested budgets that map to the same realized size.
Budgets and seeds are independent runs, not successive extensions of one adapter, and every run initializes its head and adapters from the same seeded draw, with the pretrained weights and the random state restored from an in-process per-seed cache so that repeated runs of one seed start from identical initial states.

Label sources.
Main runs train on ground-truth labels.
Hard-teacher runs replace the ground-truth training labels with the predictions of a frozen Gemma-2-9B probe, and soft-teacher runs train on the same probe’s predicted positive-class probabilities $p_{i}$ with the loss

 $\mathcal{L}_{\rm soft}(\theta)=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[p_{i}\log q_{\theta}(1\mid x_{i})+(1-p_{i})\log q_{\theta}(0\mid x_{i})\Bigr],$ 

(52)

where $q_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)$ is the student’s softmax output.
Stratified subsampling for soft-label runs uses the hard labels $\mathbf{1}[p_{i}>0.5]$ .
Before training, we verify that the teacher labels align text by text with the student’s training pool.
Every run evaluates against the original ground-truth test labels of the fixed test split, so all three label sources share one evaluation target.

Procedure.
Table 41 lists the settings of the three adaptation procedures.
All procedures use AdamW (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2017) with a constant learning rate, batch size 16, and no validation-based early stopping.
The number of epochs adapts to the realized training size,

 $E(n)=\max\Bigl\{3,\ \min\bigl\{50,\ \lfloor 3000/n\rfloor\bigr\}\Bigr\},$ 

(53)

so a run on 16 labels takes 50 epochs and a run on 4096 labels takes three.
Trainable parameters are stored in float32, and the forward pass uses bfloat16 autocasting on GPU.
Students receive plain text without a chat template, truncated at the token limits of Table 41.

Table 41: Adaptation settings of the three procedures.
Sequential updates train all backbone weights, whereas initial learning and recovery train rank-16 LoRA adapters and a head.
The recovery label grid of four budgets from 16 to 1024 is coarser than the eight-point initial-learning grid, and Section A.4.7 states how this difference enters the comparison.

Design rationale.
Three choices keep the learning curves comparable across budgets.
First, every budget is an independent run from the pretrained checkpoint, so points on one curve do not share an adapter and a poor early run cannot propagate to later budgets.
Second, curves are keyed by the realized training size, so a budget above the pool size never appears at a label count the student did not receive.
Third, stratified sampling by Equation 51 holds the class ratio fixed, so a change in AUROC along a curve reflects the number of labels and not a change in prevalence.

Scope.
Initial learning truncates inputs at 768 tokens for the five non-Gemma students and for the Gemma-3 students on the ten additional tasks, and at 512 tokens for the Gemma-3 students on the 14 core tasks, whose runs predate the change of the default limit.
Sequential updates and recovery truncate at 512 tokens.
The recovery comparison of Section A.4.7 therefore uses one limit on both sides for Gemma-3-1B and Gemma-3-4B, and for Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B it compares two complete procedures that also differ in this limit, so it does not isolate the change of backbone state alone.
These limits are separate from the frozen-probe limits of Appendix A.1.2.

## A.4.3 Label Requirements, Censoring, and the Complete Fit Panel

Question.
Do tasks with wider readouts in the frozen reference model require more labels for students to learn?
Answering this requires a label requirement that is comparable across tasks with different attainable AUROC, a treatment of curves that saturate before their first point, and a regression that separates student-specific offsets from the shared dependence on $k^{*}_{t}$ .

Definition 1 (learning curve and label requirement)

Let $n_{1}<\dots<n_{J}$ be the realized training sizes observed for student $m$ on task $t$ .
The learning curve is the median over the training seeds and the evaluated class decisions at each of these realized training sizes,

 $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j})=\operatorname{median}_{c,\,r}\,A^{\mathrm{stu}}_{tmc}(n_{j},r),$ 

(54)

keyed by the realized size rather than by a requested budget that may exceed the pool.
The label threshold applies the normalization of Equation 4 to the curve’s own endpoint,

 $\tau_{tm}=0.5+0.95\left[\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})-0.5\right].$ 

(55)

If $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{1})\geq\tau_{tm}$ , the requirement is left-censored, and we record only $N^{*}_{tm}\leq n_{1}$ .
Otherwise let $j$ be the first index with $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j+1})\geq\tau_{tm}$ , and define the weight $w$ and the requirement by

 $w=\frac{\tau_{tm}-\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j})}{\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j+1})-\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j})},\qquad\log_{10}N^{*}_{tm}=(1-w)\log_{10}n_{j}+w\log_{10}n_{j+1}.$ 

(56)

Equivalently, $N^{*}_{tm}=n_{j}^{\,1-w}\,n_{j+1}^{\,w}$ is the geometric interpolation between the two bracketing sizes.
The reader takes the first crossing on the observed curve and imposes no monotonicity.

Remark (existence and bracketing).
If $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})>0.5$ , then $N^{*}_{tm}$ is either left-censored or finite with $n_{j}<N^{*}_{tm}\leq n_{j+1}$ for the first crossing bracket $(n_{j},n_{j+1})$ .
The threshold satisfies $\tau_{tm}=\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})-0.05\,[\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})-0.5]<\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})$ , so the endpoint itself reaches $\tau_{tm}$ and a first crossing exists.
If the crossing occurs at $n_{1}$ , the value is left-censored.
Otherwise $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j})<\tau_{tm}\leq\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{j+1})$ by the choice of $j$ , which gives $0<w\leq 1$ in Equation 56, and the geometric interpolation then lies in the half-open bracket $(n_{j},n_{j+1}]$ .

Every uncensored requirement is therefore finite and lies within one grid step of an observed crossing.
The endpoint $\bar{A}_{tm}(n_{J})$ is an empirical reference, so extending the grid to larger budgets can change both $\tau_{tm}$ and $N^{*}_{tm}$ .
The requirement therefore measures how quickly a curve approaches its own largest-budget performance, not the number of labels needed for a fixed absolute AUROC.

Remark (reference point).
Reading $N^{*}$ against the running maximum of each learning curve instead of its endpoint changes 31 of the 109 requirements and moves the shared exponent from 0.65 $[0.44,0.91]$ to 0.63 $[0.40,0.90]$ .
One requirement changes by an order of magnitude, CLINC150 on Gemma-3-1B from 28 to 295, where an early spike raises the reference.
The two references trade one sensitivity for another, and we report the endpoint reference as primary and this variant as a sensitivity analysis.
On the shared four-point grid of Section A.4.7 every recovery and initial-learning curve is monotone in the label count, so the two references coincide there.

Censoring.
Left-censored observations carry only an upper bound $N^{*}_{tm}\leq 16$ , and a least-squares fit cannot use a bound as a response value.
We exclude them from the regression and display them in Table 42, so a reader can see which tasks saturate early.
Eighteen of the 19 censored cells occur at $k^{*}_{t}\leq 2$ , and every censored requirement lies below every included one, since an uncensored requirement exceeds 16 by construction.
The exclusion therefore removes information from the low end of the $k^{*}$ range rather than adding points at an imputed value.

Design rationale.
The relative threshold of Equation 55 removes differences in attainable AUROC between tasks and students, so a hard task with a low endpoint does not receive a large requirement merely because it never reaches a fixed absolute target.
The ceiling gate $C_{t}>0.90$ admits only tasks whose reference count describes a reliably readable readout, as defined in Appendix A.1.6.
Student intercepts absorb every student-level offset, which Proposition 8 below makes precise.

Estimator.
We relate the requirement of task $t$ in student $m$ to the reference count by

 $\log_{10}N^{*}_{t,m}=a_{m}+b\log_{10}k^{*}_{t}+e_{t,m},$ 

(57)

where $a_{m}$ is a student-specific intercept, $b$ is the shared exponent, and $e_{t,m}$ is a residual.
The panel $\mathcal{P}$ contains every pair with $C_{t}>0.90$ , $0<k^{*}_{t}<\infty$ , and a finite uncensored $N^{*}_{tm}$ , which yields 109 observations across eight students and 16 tasks.
We estimate $(a_{1},\dots,a_{8},b)$ by ordinary least squares on the design matrix whose first column holds $\log_{10}k^{*}_{t}$ and whose remaining eight columns are student indicators, without a separate global intercept.

Proposition 8 (the shared exponent pools within-student slopes)

Let $x_{tm}=\log_{10}k^{*}_{t}$ and $y_{tm}=\log_{10}N^{*}_{tm}$ , let $\mathcal{T}_{m}$ be the tasks of student $m$ in $\mathcal{P}$ , and let $\bar{x}_{m},\bar{y}_{m}$ be their means over $\mathcal{T}_{m}$ .
Write $S^{xx}_{m}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{m}}(x_{tm}-\bar{x}_{m})^{2}$ for the within-student sum of squares, write $S^{xy}_{m}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{m}}(x_{tm}-\bar{x}_{m})(y_{tm}-\bar{y}_{m})$ for the within-student cross product, and assume $\sum_{m}S^{xx}_{m}>0$ .
Then the shared least-squares exponent of Equation 57 fitted on the panel $\mathcal{P}$ is

 $\widehat{b}=\frac{\sum_{m}S^{xy}_{m}}{\sum_{m}S^{xx}_{m}}=\sum_{m:\,S^{xx}_{m}>0}\frac{S^{xx}_{m}}{\sum_{m^{\prime}}S^{xx}_{m^{\prime}}}\ \widehat{b}_{m},\qquad\widehat{b}_{m}=\frac{S^{xy}_{m}}{S^{xx}_{m}},$ 

(58)

where $\widehat{b}_{m}$ is the slope of a separate regression for student $m$ , and $\widehat{a}_{m}=\bar{y}_{m}-\widehat{b}\,\bar{x}_{m}$ .

Proof. For fixed $b$ , the objective $\sum_{m}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{m}}(y_{tm}-a_{m}-bx_{tm})^{2}$ separates over students, and each $a_{m}$ minimizes its own block at $a_{m}=\bar{y}_{m}-b\bar{x}_{m}$ .
Substituting this minimizer leaves $\sum_{m}\sum_{t}\bigl[(y_{tm}-\bar{y}_{m})-b(x_{tm}-\bar{x}_{m})\bigr]^{2}$ , a convex quadratic in $b$ whose stationarity condition is $\sum_{m}S^{xy}_{m}=b\sum_{m}S^{xx}_{m}$ .
The second equality in Equation 58 follows by writing $S^{xy}_{m}=S^{xx}_{m}\widehat{b}_{m}$ for every student with $S^{xx}_{m}>0$ .
 $\square$ 

Proposition 8 connects the two fits we report.
The shared exponent is a convex combination of the separate student slopes of Table 43, weighted by how widely each student’s tasks spread in $\log k^{*}$ .
The shared exponent therefore lies between the smallest and the largest separate slope, 0.454 and 0.700.
The fit identifies the exponent only from variation of $k^{*}_{t}$ across tasks within each student, so a student-level offset such as model size or family enters $a_{m}$ and never $b$ .

Result.
Table 42 lists the contributing tasks together with the left-censored cells that the regression excludes, and the resulting panel contains 109 observations.
Figure 11 shows the learning curves of one student with their thresholds and crossings, and the full 109-observation panel with the fitted parallel lines.
Seven of the eight separate slopes lie between 0.629 and 0.700, which shows that the shared exponent does not average over students with opposite trends.
Llama-3.2-3B has the flattest relationship at 0.454, and we keep it in the primary fit.
Its slope is sensitive to the CLINC150 observation, whose curve crosses its threshold close to the smallest budget in several other students.

Scope.
The fit uses 16 of the 24 tasks, namely those with $C_{t}>0.90$ and at least one uncensored requirement.
The exponent describes how requirements scale across tasks within this range of $k^{*}_{t}$ , from 1 to 341, and it makes no claim about extrapolation beyond it.

Finding.
Across eight students from four families, the label requirement grows as a power of the frozen Gemma-2-9B reference model’s count over 16 tasks, with a shared exponent of 0.65 that seven of the eight separate student fits reproduce to within 0.06.

Implication.
A single measurement of $k^{*}$ on one reference model supplies a relative ranking of annotation needs across tasks before any student is trained.

## A.4.4 Task-Block Uncertainty and Held-Out Prediction

Question.
How precisely do the 109 observations determine the shared exponent, and how accurately does the fit predict the requirement of a task it has not seen?

Unit of resampling.
Observations from different students share tasks and reference counts, so the 109 rows are not independent draws.
We resample whole task blocks, which is a block bootstrap (Efron, 1992) with the task as the block.
Let $\mathcal{B}_{t}=\{(t,m)\in\mathcal{P}\}$ be the block of task $t$ and $T=16$ the number of tasks.
Each bootstrap replicate draws task identities $t^{(1)},\dots,t^{(T)}$ uniformly with replacement and fits Equation 57 to the concatenation $\mathcal{B}_{t^{(1)}}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{B}_{t^{(T)}}$ taken as a multiset, so a task drawn twice contributes its block twice.
We use 10,000 replicates with seed 0 and skip replicates with fewer than three distinct tasks, since the slope is not estimable from fewer.
The reported interval is the 2.5th to 97.5th percentile of the retained slopes $\widehat{b}^{(1)},\dots,\widehat{b}^{(B)}$ ,

 $\mathrm{CI}_{0.95}(b)=\bigl[\,Q_{0.025}(\{\widehat{b}^{(i)}\}),\ Q_{0.975}(\{\widehat{b}^{(i)}\})\,\bigr].$ 

(59)

Held-out error.
For each task $t$ , we refit Equation 57 on $\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{t}$ and predict every row of $\mathcal{B}_{t}$ .
Writing $\widehat{y}^{(-t)}_{tm}$ for this leave-one-task-out prediction, the error summary is

 $e_{0.9}=Q_{0.9}\!\left(\left\{\bigl|\log_{10}N^{*}_{tm}-\widehat{y}^{(-t)}_{tm}\bigr|\right\}_{(t,m)\in\mathcal{P}}\right),\qquad F_{0.9}=10^{e_{0.9}}.$ 

(60)

Remark (multiplicative reading of the held-out error).
For any observation with $\bigl|\log_{10}N^{*}_{tm}-\widehat{y}^{(-t)}_{tm}\bigr|\leq e_{0.9}$ , exponentiating the two inequalities with base 10 shows that the prediction $\widehat{N}^{(-t)}_{tm}=10^{\widehat{y}^{(-t)}_{tm}}$ satisfies $\widehat{N}^{(-t)}_{tm}/F_{0.9}\leq N^{*}_{tm}\leq F_{0.9}\,\widehat{N}^{(-t)}_{tm}$ , and by the definition of the empirical 0.9 quantile at least 90% of the observations in $\mathcal{P}$ satisfy the premise.

By this remark, the reported factor of 5.3 means that at least nine of ten held-out requirements fall within a factor of 5.3 of their leave-one-task-out prediction in either direction.

Algorithm 7 Task-block bootstrap and leave-one-task-out prediction

 1:

 panel $\mathcal{P}$ from Algorithm 6 with task blocks $\mathcal{B}_{t}$ 

 2:

 Fit the shared slope and student intercepts by least squares

 3:

 for $i=1,\ldots,10000$ do

 4:

   Draw $T$ task identities with replacement

 5:

   if at least three distinct tasks are drawn then

 6:

    Concatenate their blocks, retaining repeated blocks

 7:

    Refit Equation 57 and retain its slope $\widehat{b}^{(i)}$ 

 8:

   end if

 9:

 end for

 10:

 Report the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of the retained slopes

 11:

 for each task $t$ do

 12:

   Fit on $\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{t}$ 

 13:

   Predict every row of $\mathcal{B}_{t}$ and retain its absolute log error

 14:

 end for

 15:

 Report the pooled 90th-percentile error $e_{0.9}$ and the factor $10^{e_{0.9}}$ 

Table 44: Shared fit and task-level uncertainty. The percentile interval uses 10,000 task-block bootstrap draws with multiplicities retained. Held-out error leaves whole task blocks out.

Quantity

Corresponding multiplicative error
5.335

Design rationale.
Resampling rows would treat eight observations of one task as eight independent pieces of evidence, although they share one reference count and one set of texts.
The task block is the unit that varies when a new task is added, so it is the unit we resample.
Leave-one-task-out prediction answers the practical question of how well the fit anticipates the requirement of a task it has not seen, which an in-sample residual cannot answer.

Null and result.
If requirements did not depend on the reference count, the task-block interval would cover zero.
The interval $[0.44,0.91]$ excludes zero and also excludes one, so requirements grow with $k^{*}_{t}$ but more slowly than in proportion to it.
The held-out factor of 5.3 states the precision of individual predictions and is the relevant quantity for any use of the fit as a budget planner.

Scope.
The held-out error is a pooled quantile over observations, so tasks with more eligible students contribute more errors.
It is neither a calibrated conformal interval nor a coverage guarantee for arbitrary unseen tasks.
The bootstrap conditions on the observed task collection, the measured reference counts, and the fitted student curves, and it does not propagate the seed variation of $k^{*}_{t}$ reported in Appendix A.1.6.
Retaining multiplicities is essential for a valid block bootstrap, since collapsing a draw to its distinct tasks changes the sampling distribution of the replicate.

Finding.
The exponent interval excludes both zero and one, so requirements grow with the count but sublinearly.
Individual forecasts carry a held-out factor of 5.3.

Implication.
The fit supports planning at the level of orders of magnitude and task rankings, and a practitioner should budget with the factor of 5.3 in mind rather than with the point prediction alone.

## A.4.5 Incremental Predictive Value and Teacher Controls

Question.
Does $k^{*}_{t}$ carry information about student performance beyond transferability scores and difficulty proxies, and does the teacher’s internal count matter once its labels are fixed?

Estimator.
The fixed-budget analysis relates $k^{*}_{t}$ to student AUROC at 256 labels after accounting for a control variable.
Let $r_{x}$ and $r_{y}$ be average-rank vectors over tasks, and let $Z$ contain an intercept and the ranked controls.
The partial rank correlation is

 $e_{x}=r_{x}-ZZ^{+}r_{x},\quad e_{y}=r_{y}-ZZ^{+}r_{y},\qquad\rho_{xy\cdot Z}=\operatorname{Corr}(e_{x},e_{y}),$ 

(61)

where $Z^{+}$ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse, so $ZZ^{+}$ projects onto the column space of $Z$ .
The residuals $e_{x}$ and $e_{y}$ are therefore the parts of the two rank vectors that the controls do not explain linearly.
The permutation diagnostic permutes $e_{x}$ 10,000 times with seed 0 and reports the fraction of permutations whose absolute correlation reaches the observed one.
This diagnostic tests association under the stated residual construction and is not a general test of conditional independence.

Controls.
The controls are LogME (You et al., 2021), the H-score (Bao et al., 2019), label entropy, the bag-of-words input ceiling of Appendix A.2.3, and the student base-model ceiling where available.
With ten high-ceiling tasks, we add controls one at a time, and multi-control fits serve only as sensitivity analyses because they leave little residual variation.
The counts in this analysis come from the preliminary single-seed sweep of Appendix A.1.6, not from the five-seed counts of the primary exponent fit, and the analysis reads the requested 256-label runs, a budget below the pool size of all ten tasks.
Table 45 therefore reports a separate control analysis rather than a second fit of Equation 57.

The residual-permutation $p$ values are not adjusted for multiplicity. These controls use the single-seed counts of the preliminary sweep and the requested 256-label runs, as specified in Appendix A.4.5, which also reports the $N^{*}$ -level control.

Design rationale.
Rank-based partial correlations test whether $k^{*}_{t}$ adds information beyond each transferability score without assuming linear relations with AUROC.
The teacher swap uses a natural experiment in which two teachers disagree in their internal counts but agree in their labels, which separates the path through labels from the path through representation sparsity.

Result.
Given LogME, $k^{*}_{t}$ keeps a partial correlation between $-0.668$ and $-0.763$ with student AUROC in all three students, whereas LogME given $k^{*}_{t}$ falls to between $+0.012$ and $+0.140$ .
Given the H-score, both predictors keep substantial partial correlations, so the H-score and $k^{*}_{t}$ carry complementary information about student AUROC at 256 labels.
Given the bag-of-words ceiling of Appendix A.2.3, $k^{*}_{t}$ keeps a partial correlation between $-0.547$ and $-0.670$ , with residual-permutation $p$ values of 0.032 to 0.096, although the two predictors correlate at $-0.80$ over the ten tasks.
The same control on the 109 observations of Equation 57 leaves a partial rank correlation of $+0.51$ between $k^{*}_{t}$ and $N^{*}$ after removing each student’s mean, with $p<10^{-4}$ , and adding the bag-of-words ceiling as a covariate moves the shared exponent from 0.65 to 0.55 while $R^{2}$ changes from 0.525 to 0.528.
On the ten tasks of the fixed-budget analysis the bag-of-words ceiling exceeds 0.98 on all but fact checking, sentiment, and harmfulness, which limits this control there.

Table 46: What each learning control tests. Distinct controls address distinct alternative explanations, and no single control identifies the cause of the relation between counts and requirements.

Teacher controls.
For harmfulness and intent classification, probes on base and instruction-tuned Gemma-2-9B produce nearly identical labels despite different internal counts.
Substituting one teacher’s hard labels for the other’s changes student AUROC by at most 0.005 over the tested budgets, and soft-label curves remain close at larger budgets.
These controls change the teacher’s labels and cannot change its representation sparsity alone, so they test the consequences of label substitution rather than a surgical intervention on sparsity.
Our class-mean approximation to the STS transferability score is likewise a simplified variant and not a reproduction of the original procedure.

Finding.
Given LogME, the count keeps a partial correlation of $-0.668$ to $-0.763$ with student AUROC in all three tested students, whereas LogME given the count retains at most $+0.140$ .
Given the bag-of-words ceiling, the count keeps $-0.547$ to $-0.670$ at fixed budget and $+0.51$ with $N^{*}$ on the full panel of 109 observations across eight students.

Implication.
Readout sparsity is not a restatement of a transferability score or of text decidability, and in all three students it complements the H-score rather than replacing it.

## A.4.6 Multi-Task Budget Replay

Question.
Under one total budget for several tasks, does allocation in proportion to predicted requirements reach a joint performance target with fewer labels than uniform allocation?

Data.
The replay includes the nine tasks whose Gemma-2-9B ceilings exceed 0.90, namely language identification, tool selection, routing, intent, PII detection, jailbreak detection, banking intent, harmfulness detection, and fact checking.
For each student and task, $a_{t}(n)$ is the median test accuracy over seeds and class decisions at realized size $n$ , linearly interpolated in $\log n$ between measured sizes and held constant beyond the smallest and largest sizes.

Estimator.
For a total budget $B$ and nonnegative weights $w_{t}$ , each policy assigns

 $b_{t}(B)=B\frac{w_{t}}{\sum_{u}w_{u}},\qquad U(B)=\prod_{t}a_{t}\!\left(b_{t}(B)\right),$ 

(62)

and its cost for a target $U^{\star}$ is the smallest total budget with $U(B)\geq U^{\star}$ .
We report every policy’s cost as a ratio to the uniform policy’s cost in the same setting.
The policies differ only in their weights,

 $w^{\mathrm{uni}}_{t}=1,\qquad w^{\mathrm{pilot}}_{t}=\max\{10^{-6},\,1-a_{t}(16)\},\qquad w^{k}_{t}=k^{*}_{t},\qquad w^{N}_{t}=10^{\,\widehat{\alpha}^{(-t)}}\bigl(k^{*}_{t}\bigr)^{\widehat{\beta}^{(-t)}},$ 

(63)

where $(\widehat{\alpha}^{(-t)},\widehat{\beta}^{(-t)})$ is a least-squares fit of $\log_{10}N^{*}$ on $\log_{10}k^{*}$ over the other pipeline tasks of the same student, and a student leaves the predicted-requirement policy when any of its tasks has fewer than four other tasks to fit on, a condition that no tested pipeline triggers.
The requirement in this fit comes from accuracy curves with chance level 0.5, not from the AUROC curves of Equation 57.

Remark (monotone utility and exact search).
If every curve $a_{t}$ is nondecreasing and nonnegative and every $w_{t}>0$ , then $U(B)$ is nondecreasing in $B$ , because each $b_{t}(B)$ increases linearly in $B$ , log-linear interpolation of a nondecreasing sequence is nondecreasing, and a product of nonnegative nondecreasing functions is nondecreasing.
The set $\{B:U(B)\geq U^{\star}\}$ is then an interval $[B^{\star},10^{6}]$ , and geometric bisection on $[10^{2},10^{6}]$ evaluates $U$ at the geometric midpoint $\sqrt{\ell h}$ of the current bracket $[\ell,h]$ , keeps the half that contains $B^{\star}$ , and halves $\log(h/\ell)$ at every step.
After 60 halvings the returned budget lies within a factor of $(10^{4})^{2^{-60}}$ above $B^{\star}$ , whenever the target is reachable within the interval and not already reached at its lower end.

The measured curves are unsmoothed and can be locally nonmonotone, in which case the bisection is a numerical policy evaluation rather than a certified minimum.
A replay on the running-maximum envelope of each curve, which satisfies the premise of this remark, serves as a sensitivity analysis.

Remark (why the count rule overspends).
If one fit with exponent $\widehat{\beta}$ served all tasks, the predicted-requirement weights would equal $w^{N}_{t}\propto(k^{*}_{t})^{\widehat{\beta}}$ , since the student intercept cancels in Equation 62.
The raw-count rule $w^{k}_{t}=k^{*}_{t}$ then corresponds to an exponent of one.
Whenever the fitted exponent lies below one, as the AUROC-based exponent of Equation 57 does, the raw-count rule concentrates more labels on the widest-readout tasks than their requirements warrant.
The leave-one-task-out fits make this correspondence approximate, since each task receives its own fitted pair.

Design rationale.
The replay evaluates every policy on the same measured learning curves, so differences between policies come from allocation alone and not from new training noise.
Predicted requirements for a task come from fits that exclude that task, so no task’s own measured requirement enters its weight.
The pipeline admits only the nine tasks with $C_{t}>0.90$ , and it excludes relevance, mathematical-step correctness, code-bug detection, and mathematical-answer correctness because their Gemma-2-9B reference ceilings fall below this gate.

Null.
Uniform allocation is the null policy, and a rule that carries useful allocation information should reach every joint target in every student at a cost ratio below one.

Table 47: Budget allocation replay relative to uniform allocation. Ratios below one use fewer labels under the replay accounting. Only settings in which the uniform, pilot, count, and predicted-requirement policies all reach the target enter the 18-setting summary, and the oracle policy is excluded because its exact requirements are undefined for left-censored tasks.

Result.
The predicted-requirement rule costs a median of 1.03 times the uniform budget and is cheaper in 6 of 18 settings, so it does not consistently save labels.
The raw-count rule costs between 2.29 and 13.02 times the uniform budget and is cheaper in none of the 18 settings, consistent with the remark above.
The pilot rule costs a median of 0.66 times the uniform budget and is cheaper than uniform allocation in all 18 settings under the stated accounting.

Scope.
We evaluate targets $U^{\star}\in\{0.50,0.60,0.70\}$ , and the 18 settings comprise eight students at each of the first two targets and two students at the third, since the other students have at least one policy that cannot reach the target within $10^{6}$ labels.
The pilot rule sets its weights from 16-label runs, but the replay neither adds the pilot’s own labels and training runs to $B$ nor enforces $b_{t}\geq 16$ , so its ratio describes this accounting convention rather than an end-to-end budget.
The product in Equation 62 is a replay utility and not an observed probability of joint success, since we estimate no dependence among task errors.
The result concerns proportional allocation, and other allocation policies that use count information remain untested.
Predicting where a learning curve reaches its criterion differs from locating where additional labels most improve a joint objective, and the proportional policies of Equation 63 use only the former.

Finding.
Allocating labels in proportion to predicted requirements reaches the three joint targets at a median of 1.03 times the uniform budget over 18 settings, and allocating in proportion to raw reference counts costs 2.29 to 13.02 times the uniform budget in the same settings.

Implication.
Forecasting where each learning curve reaches its criterion does not by itself specify where the next label is most valuable to the joint target.
The boundary of usefulness is precise, since proportional rules built on counts or predicted requirements do not save labels in the tested pipelines, while a 16-label pilot does in all 18 settings under the stated accounting.

## A.4.7 Retention, Readability, and Recovery under Continual Learning

Question.
When sequential training disrupts an earlier task’s classification head, does the backbone still carry linearly readable information about that task, and how many labels does recovery need compared with initial learning on the pretrained backbone?

Models and data.
The common chain trains harmfulness detection, then sentiment, then jailbreak detection, with three chain seeds in each of Gemma-3-1B, Gemma-3-4B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Qwen3-8B.
Expanded Gemma-3-1B experiments change the initial task or the order, and six-task chains append banking intent, CLINC150, and language identification, which Llama-3.1-8B also runs with three seeds.
Each step trains one binary class decision on up to 4096 labels.

Procedure.
Each chain fine-tunes all backbone parameters on one task at a time with a fresh classification head, and the backbone after step $s$ initializes step $s+1$ .
Old heads are saved and evaluated on each updated backbone without refitting.
Separately, we freeze each updated backbone, extract its representations, and fit new probes for the evaluation tasks by the procedure of Appendix A.1.4.
The 4B and 8B backbones train with gradient checkpointing.
Algorithm 8 states the full procedure.

Estimator.
For task $t$ , let $H_{t}^{(s)}$ be the saved-head AUROC after step $s$ , let $s_{t}$ be the step at which task $t$ was trained, and let $C_{t}^{(s)}$ be the dense-probe ceiling on the backbone after step $s$ , with $s=0$ denoting the pretrained checkpoint.
The two reported changes use different reference states,

 $L^{\rm head}_{t,s}=H_{t}^{(s_{t})}-H_{t}^{(s)},\qquad L^{\rm ceiling}_{t,s}=C_{t}^{(0)}-C_{t}^{(s)}.$ 

(64)

The ceiling $C_{t}^{(s)}$ is the largest dense-probe AUROC over the evaluation layers, and the accompanying count is the neighbor-confirmed count of Equation 14, both computed per class decision.

Remark (the two losses are not a decomposition).
The saved head reads the final non-padding position of the last layer through weights fitted before the update, whereas the ceiling reads mean-pooled representations at the evaluation layers through a probe fitted after it.
Neither predictor bounds the other, so $L^{\rm head}_{t,s}-L^{\rm ceiling}_{t,s}$ has no interpretation as a readout component of forgetting.
Instead, a large $L^{\rm head}$ together with a small $L^{\rm ceiling}$ shows qualitatively that the updated representation still supports an accurate linear readout that the saved head no longer implements.

Algorithm 8 Separate behavioral retention from frozen readability

 1:

 pretrained backbone $\theta_{0}$ , ordered tasks $(t_{1},\ldots,t_{S})$ 

 2:

 for $s=1,\ldots,S$ do

 3:

   Initialize a new classification head and train it with all weights of $\theta_{s-1}$ on $t_{s}$ 

 4:

   Save the updated backbone $\theta_{s}$ and the trained head for $t_{s}$ 

 5:

   Evaluate each available saved head on $\theta_{s}$ without refitting

 6:

   Freeze $\theta_{s}$ , extract representations, and fit new probes for the evaluation tasks

 7:

   Record head AUROC, dense ceiling, and neighbor-confirmed count

 8:

 end for

 9:

 for each recovery budget and recovery seed do

 10:

   Start independently from $\theta_{S}$ , and train fresh LoRA adapters and a new head

 11:

   Evaluate on the original test split of the recovered task

 12:

 end for

 13:

 Read recovery and initial-learning requirements at one target on the shared grid

Baselines.
The Gemma-3-1B baseline $C_{t}^{(0)}$ comes from the pretrained model’s single-seed preliminary sweep of Appendix A.1.6, and the other three models use the first seed of their five-seed measurement.
Baseline and post-update rows pass through the same ceiling and count estimator, so every comparison is internal to one chain and one estimator.
These are within-chain comparisons and do not replace the five-seed task-level counts of Algorithm 2.
Probe coverage varies across chains, and in the third seed of the six-task Llama chain three of the six evaluation tasks were not probed, which we treat as missing rather than as retained.

Recovery estimator.
Each final chain backbone receives three independent recovery seeds at each of the budgets $16,64,256,1024$ .
We first aggregate the recovery AUROCs into one curve per final backbone, so each chain seed yields one recovery curve.
The initial-learning curve on the same pretrained backbone comes from the eight-point grid $16,32,64,256,512,1024,2048,4096$ , and Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B supply learning curves beyond the eight-student panel of Section A.4.3.
We restrict this curve to the four recovery budgets and set the target $\tau=0.5+0.95\,[\bar{A}^{\rm init}(1024)-0.5]$ , the threshold of Equation 55 at the shared endpoint.
Both requirements are the first crossing of $\tau$ under the interpolation of Equation 56, so the initial requirement equals its own $N^{*}$ on the shared grid and the recovery requirement is read against the same AUROC.
Table 48 reports the median and range of the recovery requirements over the three chain seeds, together with recovery $N^{*}$ against each recovery curve’s own endpoint and initial $N^{*}$ on the full eight-point grid.

Design rationale.
Re-probing the frozen updated backbone separates two explanations of forgetting that a saved-head evaluation alone confounds.
A failing head can reflect a readout that no longer matches the representation, or a representation that no longer carries the information.
Each loss in Equation 64 uses the reference state that matches its question, and baseline and updated rows pass through one ceiling and count estimator.
Recovery and initial learning run on the same four backbones with the same adapter family, so the comparison isolates the effect of starting from the updated backbone up to the input-length difference stated in Section A.4.2.
Reading both curves on one grid against one target removes two differences that own-endpoint requirements on unequal grids would carry, a higher endpoint and denser interior points on the longer grid.

Null.
If sequential training erased the task information that a linear probe reads, $L^{\rm ceiling}$ would be comparable to $L^{\rm head}$ .
If the updated backbone offered no advantage for relearning the disrupted task, recovery and initial-learning requirements would be similar.

Table 48: Recovering harmfulness detection after the same three-task sequence requires fewer labels than initial learning. Both curves are read on the recovery grid of 16 to 1024 labels. The target is the AUROC at which initial learning attains 95% of its gain above chance at 1024 labels, and the first requirement columns give the labels that each procedure needs to reach it. Recovery values give the median and, in brackets, the range over three chain seeds. The last two columns give recovery $N^{*}$ against its own endpoint and initial $N^{*}$ on the full grid of 16 to 4096 labels.

Result.
Over the twelve common-chain seeds, saved-head losses of 0.52 to 0.61 coexist with harmfulness ceiling losses of at most 0.010, and the six-task chains of Gemma-3-1B and Llama-3.1-8B extend the head losses to 0.47 to 0.68 while their ceiling losses stay at most 0.029.
The harmfulness medians of the main text are 0.55 to 0.59 for the heads against at most 0.009 for the ceilings.
On the shared grid, recovery reaches the common target with a median of 101 to 244 labels against 194 to 602 for initial learning, a reduction of 1.5- to 6.0-fold, and every recovery median lies below its model’s initial requirement.
One Llama-3.1-8B chain seed needs 544 labels, above that model’s initial 524.
Against each recovery curve’s own endpoint the reduction is 2.2- to 3.3-fold in three models, and on Qwen3-8B the two requirements are 176 and 194, because its initial curve falls from 0.978 at 512 labels to 0.971 at 1024 and so lowers its own reference.
On the full eight-point grid the initial requirements are 269 to 864, so the requirement moves with the grid, which is why the comparison holds the grid fixed.
Figure 12 plots the head and ceiling trajectories of all four models along the chain.
The recovery curves of Table 49 reach AUROC between 0.967 and 0.984 at 1024 labels, so the recovered heads attain high absolute performance and not only a fast approach to a low endpoint.

Figure 12: Saved heads fail while new probes keep reading harmfulness along the common chain.
Each panel follows one model through the chain that trains harmfulness, then sentiment, then jailbreak detection, with three chain seeds.
Gold circles give the AUROC of the harmfulness head saved after step 1 and evaluated on each later backbone without refitting.
Blue squares give the dense-probe ceiling of new probes fitted on the frozen backbone after each step, and step 0 denotes the pretrained model.
Markers show the median over the three chain seeds and whiskers span the seed range.
The dashed line marks chance.
The arrow and the labels report the final losses of Equation 64 from the seed medians.
Head losses are 0.55, 0.56, 0.59 and 0.56 for Gemma-3-1B, Gemma-3-4B, Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B, and the ceiling losses are $-0.009$ , $-0.001$ , $0.002$ and $-0.001$ , where a negative value means that the final ceiling exceeds the pretrained ceiling.
Over every step and seed of these twelve chains the largest harmfulness ceiling loss stays below 0.010.

Scope.
The ceiling bound depends on chain length.
Three-step chains lose at most 0.015 of the pretrained ceiling, whereas the six-step Llama chain shows a small accumulation after the fourth step that reaches 0.029, which rules out a claim of zero information loss.
The comparison uses the recovery grid, which ends at 1024 labels, so it does not show how the two procedures compare at larger budgets, and Table 49 supplies the absolute recovery endpoints.
Across six tasks, pre-update $k^{*}$ and the recovery discount show a positive trend with $\rho=0.77$ and $p=0.072$ , which we report as a descriptive trend and not as a prediction law.

Finding.
After sequential training, saved heads lose 0.47 to 0.68 AUROC while dense probes on the same frozen backbones stay within 0.029 of the pretrained ceiling, and recovery reaches a common target with 101 to 244 labels in median against 194 to 602 for initial learning.

Implication.
A failing saved head does not show that the task information is gone.
Re-probing the frozen backbone distinguishes a displaced readout from lost readability at the cost of one linear fit, and when readability survives, a small recovery budget suffices in the tested chains.

## A.4.8 Pruning Operators, Calibration, and Statistical Family

Question.
Does the pre-pruning count of a task predict how much linear readability that task loses when the weights of the same model are pruned by magnitude or by Wanda?

Models and data.
We prune Gemma-3-1B, Gemma-2-9B, OLMo-2-7B, and Llama-3.1-8B and probe six tasks, namely language identification, CLINC150, jailbreak detection, banking intent, sentiment, and harmfulness detection, at requested sparsities of 30%, 50%, 60%, 70%, 80%, and 90%.

Operators.
Both methods act on every linear map inside the transformer blocks.
Magnitude pruning treats each weight matrix $W$ with $|W|$ entries separately and zeros the entries whose magnitude does not exceed the $\lfloor s|W|\rfloor$ th smallest absolute value in that matrix,

 $W_{ij}\leftarrow W_{ij}\,\mathbf{1}\bigl[\,|W_{ij}|>\theta_{W}\,\bigr],\qquad\theta_{W}=\bigl|W\bigr|_{(\lfloor s|W|\rfloor)},$ 

(65)

where $s$ is the requested sparsity and $|W|_{(k)}$ is the $k$ th order statistic of the absolute entries, so ties at the threshold can raise the realized fraction slightly above $s$ .
Wanda (Sun et al., 2024) scores each weight by its magnitude times the norm of its input feature over a calibration set,

 $\nu_{j}=\Bigl(\sum_{x,t}u_{j}(x,t)^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2},\qquad S_{ij}=|W_{ij}|\,\nu_{j},$ 

(66)

where $u_{j}(x,t)$ is the $j$ th input coordinate of the linear map at position $t$ of calibration text $x$ .
Each output row then zeros its $\lfloor s\,d_{\rm in}\rfloor$ lowest-scoring weights, so every row reaches the same sparsity.
The default calibration draws 128 texts with seed 0 from the union of the evaluated tasks’ training pools, and a Gemma-2-9B calibration control substitutes Pile text with the same count and sampling rule.

Estimator.
After pruning, we fit dense probes on the pruned backbone for up to three class decisions per task, take the median dense AUROC over the evaluated class decisions at each probed layer, and take the maximum of these medians over the probed layers,

 $C^{\rm prune}_{t,m,a,s}=\max_{\ell}\ \operatorname{median}_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}A^{\rm dense}_{t,c,m,a,s,\ell},\qquad D_{t,m,a,s}=C_{t,m}-C^{\rm prune}_{t,m,a,s},$ 

(67)

where $a$ indexes the pruning method and $C_{t,m}$ is the unpruned task ceiling from Algorithm 2.
The baseline count $k^{*}_{t,m}$ comes from the same unpruned measurement.

Test statistic.
Each condition $(m,a,s)$ pairs the six baseline counts with the six ceiling losses and computes the Spearman correlation (Spearman, 1987) $\rho_{\rm obs}$ with average ranks for ties.
The one-sided exact test enumerates all $6!$ relabelings of the losses,

 $p=\frac{1}{6!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{6}}\mathbf{1}\!\left\{\operatorname{Corr}(r_{k},\,r_{D}\circ\pi)\geq\rho_{\rm obs}-10^{-12}\right\},$ 

(68)

where the tolerance $10^{-12}$ guards the comparison against floating-point error.

Lemma 5 (exactness and resolution of the permutation test)

Under the null hypothesis that the pairing of counts with losses is exchangeable, $\Pr(p\leq\alpha)\leq\alpha$ for every $\alpha\in[0,1]$ .
Without ties, the smallest attainable value is $p=1/6!$ , reached exactly when the two rankings agree.

Proof. Under exchangeability, the observed pairing is a uniformly random element of the orbit $\{r_{D}\circ\pi:\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{6}\}$ , and $p$ is the fraction of the orbit whose statistic reaches the observed one.
The standard argument for randomization tests then gives $\Pr(p\leq\alpha)\leq\alpha$ , since at most $\lfloor\alpha\,6!\rfloor$ orbit elements can have a $p$ value of at most $\alpha$ .
Without ties, $\rho=1$ holds for exactly one relabeling, so $\rho_{\rm obs}=1$ gives $p=1/6!$ and every other permutation gives a strictly smaller correlation.
 $\square$ 

Lemma 5 shows that six tasks give the test a resolution of $1/6!$ , so the statistic and its $p$ value take finitely many values.
This discreteness explains the repeated values of $\rho$ and $q$ in Table 50, and it makes the test conservative rather than liberal at every nominal level.

Multiplicity.
We apply the Benjamini–Hochberg adjustment (Benjamini & Hochberg, 1995) to the 32 conditions at sparsities of at least 60%, formed by four models, two methods, and four sparsities.
For sorted values $p_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq p_{(M)}$ with $M=32$ ,

 $q_{(i)}=\min\Bigl\{1,\ \min_{j\geq i}\frac{M}{j}\,p_{(j)}\Bigr\}.$ 

(69)

The 32 conditions share tasks, model weights, and baseline counts and ceilings, so they are not independent replications.
The adjustment is a conventional control of the reported family, and we do not claim a false-discovery guarantee under arbitrary dependence.
Conditions at 30% and 50% sparsity are descriptive, since their losses are too small to order reliably.

Table 50: Correlation of pre-pruning counts with ceiling losses. Cells give Spearman $\rho$ between the six baseline counts and the unrounded ceiling losses of Equation 67. At 60% to 90% sparsity, the small number beside each correlation gives the Benjamini–Hochberg $q$ over those 32 conditions. Each one-sided test enumerates all $6!=720$ permutations. Low-sparsity correlations are descriptive, and at 30% sparsity every maximum ceiling loss is below 0.007.

Design rationale.
Two pruning methods with different scoring rules test whether the order of losses is a property of the tasks or of one scoring function.
With six tasks per condition, an asymptotic correlation test would be unreliable, so we enumerate every relabeling and obtain exact $p$ values.
The adjustment family consists of every condition at 60% sparsity or higher, a cut defined by sparsity and not by the observed correlations.
Magnitude pruning uses no calibration data at all, and the Pile calibration control on Gemma-2-9B replaces the task texts with generic text, so together they test whether the losses arise from calibrating on the evaluated tasks.

Null and result.
If pre-pruning counts carried no information about pruning losses, the correlations would scatter around zero and the $q$ values would rarely fall below 0.05.
Instead, 30 of the 32 conditions at 60% sparsity or higher reach $\rho\geq 0.886$ with $q<0.05$ , and the two exceptions are the 80% conditions on Gemma-3-1B with $\rho=0.600$ .
All 48 correlations in the table are positive, including the descriptive low-sparsity cells.
On Gemma-2-9B, Pile calibration changes every Wanda ceiling at 60% to 90% sparsity by at most 0.004 and leaves every rank correlation unchanged.
At 70% sparsity and above, sentiment loses more readability than harmfulness detection in all four models, although it has the smaller count in three of them, so the association separates stable from vulnerable tasks without ordering the wide-readout tasks among themselves.

Precision.
We compute every correlation from unrounded ceiling losses.
Rounding losses to three decimals would create artificial ties among the small losses of the narrow-readout tasks and change their average ranks.
At 30% sparsity, even a high correlation orders losses smaller than 0.007, so these cells appear for completeness and do not carry the claim.

Scope.
Post-pruning probes cover up to three class decisions per task, whereas the unpruned task ceiling can cover more, and the losses describe this evaluation convention.
They do not constitute a matched five-seed estimate of information loss for every original class decision.

Finding.
At 60% sparsity or higher, the pre-pruning count correlates with the loss of linear readability at $\rho\geq 0.886$ with $q<0.05$ in 30 of 32 conditions, consistently across two pruning methods and four models, and Pile calibration leaves the Gemma-2-9B order unchanged.

Implication.
Tasks with narrow readouts tolerate aggressive pruning, and tasks with wide readouts deserve the closest monitoring when a model is compressed.
The count separates these two groups but does not order losses within the wide-readout group.

## A.4.9 Pruning Recovery and Support Protection

Question.
How much task performance can a small labeled budget of at most 1,024 labels restore after pruning, and does protecting the weights attached to selected readout coordinates preserve readability better than protecting equally many random coordinates?

Recovery procedure.
Each pruned backbone is adapted independently with LoRA and a fresh classification head at budgets $16,64,256,1024$ and seeds $0,1,2$ , using the recovery settings of Table 41.
Table 51 gives the complete Gemma-3-1B Wanda comparison behind the main-text range, with gaps computed before rounding.
The frozen ceiling is a reference for what a linear probe reads, not an upper bound on larger-budget or full-parameter adaptation.

Definition 2 (support protection)

Let $P\subseteq[d]$ be a set of protected residual coordinates.
For a linear map that writes to the residual stream, namely the attention output projection and the MLP down projection, the rows indexed by $P$ stay dense and every other row follows Wanda.
For a linear map that reads from the residual stream, namely the query, key, value, gate, and up projections, the columns indexed by $P$ receive the score $S_{ij}=+\infty$ in Equation 66, and each row still zeros its $\lfloor s\,d_{\rm in}\rfloor$ lowest-scoring weights.
The protected arms differ only in $P$ .
The jailbreak-support arm protects the selected jailbreak coordinates, the single-layer arm protects the selected harmfulness and sentiment coordinates of one layer, the selected-union arm protects the harmfulness and sentiment selections over four layers, and the random control protects as many random coordinates per task.

Remark (what protection guarantees).
The reader-column rule removes protected columns from the pruning set only while each row has at least $\lfloor s\,d_{\rm in}\rfloor$ unprotected columns.
When the quota exceeds that number, the lowest-score selection must include some infinite-score columns, so the rule is a protection heuristic rather than a hard constraint at every requested sparsity.
The writer-row rule leaves $|P|$ rows dense in each writer map, which lowers the realized sparsity

 $s_{\mathrm{act}}=\frac{\#\{\text{zero weights in all pruned maps}\}}{\#\{\text{weights in all pruned maps}\}}$ 

(70)

below the requested value, and we report $s_{\mathrm{act}}$ separately in Table 55 wherever the run log recorded it.
Protecting weights also does not protect the activations of the protected coordinates, since they receive contributions from every unprotected weight upstream.

Design rationale.
We fixed the success criteria before running the protection arms.
The random control protects the same per-task numbers of coordinates as the selected union, so any advantage of the selected arm would reflect which coordinates it protects and not how many.
Reporting the realized sparsity separates a genuine protection effect from the trivial effect of pruning fewer weights.

Null.
If selected readout coordinates marked the weights that task readability depends on, the selected-union arm would retain clearly higher ceilings than the random control of nearly equal size.

Result.
The selected-union minus random-control differences are $+0.003$ , $+0.002$ , and $-0.010$ for sentiment and $0$ , $-0.004$ , and $0$ for harmfulness at the three requested sparsities, so selected protection does not outperform random protection.
Both union arms also retain lower ceilings than ordinary Wanda at the same requested sparsity.
At matched actual sparsity, the comparison with ordinary pruning interpolates the measured ordinary degradation curve, and it is not an additional directly measured run at each protected arm’s exact sparsity.

Scope.
The selected union contains 969 distinct coordinates and the random control 975, since the per-task sets have the same sizes but overlap differently, so the two arms are nearly but not exactly equal in size.
The retained run log lacks the realized sparsity of the selected-union arm, and an earlier summary of the same runs reported values within 0.001 of the random control, so we compare the two union arms at their common requested sparsity.
The selection budgets were 174 coordinates for harmfulness, 227 for sentiment, and 16 for jailbreak detection, which differ from the final Gemma-2-9B counts of 341, 72, and 8.
The protection arm therefore tests this selection rule, not the preservation of the final protocol’s supports.
The result bounds this protection rule and leaves other support-aware compression procedures untested.

Finding.
Protecting the weights attached to selected readout coordinates retains ceilings within 0.010 of protecting equally many random coordinates, and both protected arms retain less readability than ordinary Wanda at the same requested sparsity.

Implication.
Coordinates that suffice for a linear readout do not identify the parameters that sustain it, since every protected coordinate still receives contributions from unprotected weights upstream.
Readout measurements diagnose which tasks pruning endangers, and translating them into a protection rule requires a mechanism that the tested rule does not provide.

## A.4.10 Reproducibility of the Application Results

Every result in this appendix is determined by four ingredients, namely the task pools, the adaptation or modification procedure, the random seeds, and the estimator that turns runs into the reported number.
Table 56 lists these four ingredients for each result of Section 5, and the three paragraphs below state the shared conventions that the table does not show.

Deterministic components.
The least-squares fits, the leave-one-task-out predictions, the allocation replay, the exact permutation tests, and the Benjamini–Hochberg adjustment involve no randomness beyond their inputs, so they return identical values from identical learning curves and probe results.
The task-block bootstrap and the partial-correlation permutations fix their seeds at 0, which makes their intervals and $p$ values exactly repeatable from the same panel.

Stochastic components.
Adaptation runs draw their training subsets by Equation 51 with the run seed and initialize heads and adapters from the same seed.
GPU training in bfloat16 is not bitwise deterministic, so repeated runs can differ in the last digits of AUROC even under identical seeds, and the three seeds per budget quantify the resulting variation.
The continual-learning chains and the pruning calibration draws are seeded in the same way.

Reported precision.
Tables round values for display, whereas every threshold comparison, correlation, and gap uses unrounded values.
Consequently, a displayed gap can differ in its last digit from the difference of the two displayed values in the same row.

## A.4.11 Summary of Findings, Recommendations, and Open Questions

Findings.
The experiments of this appendix establish three predictive results and two boundaries.
First, the count of a frozen reference model anticipates label requirements in other students, with $N^{*}\propto(k^{*})^{0.65}$ and a held-out factor of 5.3.
Second, sequential training can disable saved heads while linear readability persists, and recovery then needs fewer labels than initial learning.
Third, wider pre-pruning readouts predict larger losses of readability, in 30 of 32 adjusted conditions.
The two boundaries concern intervention, since neither proportional label allocation by counts nor protection of readout-coordinate weights outperformed its baseline.

Recommendations.
The results support three practices under the tested procedures.
Before annotation, measure $k^{*}$ on a reference model to rank tasks by expected label needs, and treat individual forecasts as accurate to within a factor of about five.
After sequential updates, re-probe the frozen backbone before retraining from scratch, since a failing head can coexist with intact linear readability.
Before compression, flag tasks with wide readouts for evaluation, since they carry the largest losses.

Open questions.
The results leave three questions open.
The source of the relation between reference counts and student requirements remains unidentified, since the teacher controls change labels and not sparsity alone.
An allocation rule that uses counts to choose where marginal labels help a joint objective most, rather than proportional weights, remains untested.
A compression rule that preserves the activations of selected coordinates, rather than their attached weights, would test whether readout supports can guide preservation under pruning at all.
